El Nino is a 'postcard from the future' — and could cause 451,000 excess heat deaths

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The current El Nino could cause 451,000 excess heat deaths. Researchers warn it offers a glimpse of temperatures that may become normal within 20 years.

Peruvian fishermen gave El Nino its name — "the little boy" or Christ Child — after noticing unusually warm Pacific waters around Christmas that caused the fish they depended on to suddenly become scarce.

But what those fishermen observed locally can have consequences thousands of kilometers away. El Nino can shift temperature and rainfall patterns around the world, bringing extreme heat and drought to some regions and heavy rain and flooding to others.

Researchers now estimate that the unusually high temperatures linked to the current El Nino could cause around 451,000 excess deaths worldwide from June 2026 to February 2027.

The event is a "postcard from the future," Tamma Carleton, head of research at the University of Chicago's Climate Impact Lab, told DW. Land temperatures over the coming months are forecast to run about 1.2 degrees C (2.1 F) above normal.

"For the average person, that's roughly the warming climate change is projected to bring about 20 years from now, arriving all at once," Carleton said.

Normally, winds blowing west across the Pacific push warm surface water towards Asia and Australia, allowing colder water to rise near South America. During El Nino, which occurs erratically every two to seven years, those winds weaken. Less cold water then rises, and much more of the Pacific stays unusually warm.

The warmer water heats the air above it and fuels rainfall over the central and eastern Pacific. The rising air spreads high in the atmosphere, sending waves that disrupt weather worldwide.

"It is like throwing a stone into a lake," climate researcher Fred Kucharski of the Abdus Salam International Centre for Theoretical Physics in Trieste, Italy, told DW. "Initially the surface perturbation is local, but then the energy spreads all over the lake through wave propagation."

Kucharski said the effects can cross the tropics within one week and reach higher latitudes in about two. Where the waves travel determines whether a region becomes hotter, cooler, wetter or drier.

The current El Nino developed in June and has strengthened rapidly since. It is now on course to become an exceptionally strong — sometimes called "super" — El Nino.

The Climate Impact Lab says its estimate covers only the first nine months of El Nino, as far as the current seasonal forecasts allow. The event is expected to continue into Northern Hemisphere spring, and its effect on global temperatures could last longer.

"We expect the final number to be meaningfully higher than what we are currently reporting here," Carleton said.

The estimate of 451,000 excess deaths covers deaths linked to the direct health effects of unusually high temperatures associated with El Nino.

Extreme heat strains the heart and kidneys, makes it harder for the body to maintain a safe temperature and can worsen cardiovascular, respiratory and other illnesses. As a result, the underlying condition is often recorded as the cause of death rather than the heat that aggravated it.

"Most heat deaths never show up in official statistics as heat deaths," Carleton said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

El Nino can also alter rainfall, contributing to drought in some regions and heavy rain and flooding in others.

Floods can kill, contaminate drinking water and spread disease. Drought can damage harvests and cause water and food shortages.

Deaths from these other indirect effects are not included in the 451,000 projection.

The Climate Impact Lab expects some of the largest increases in deaths in the Sahel, South and Southeast Asia and parts of Latin America. The highest numbers of fatalities are predicted for Nigeria, Sudan, Niger, Chad, India, Indonesia and Brazil.

Many are projected to occur between December and February, meaning much of the expected toll still lies ahead.

"Heat headlines usually focus on Europe and North America," Carleton said. "This event's toll is overwhelmingly in lower-income, already-hot countries, where people have the fewest ways to adapt and where these deaths are least likely to be counted."

Some people who die during extreme heat are already frail. This raises an important question: Is the heat cutting their lives substantially short, or merely bringing forward deaths that would have occurred days or weeks later — an effect known as mortality displacement?

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The underlying research uses annual rather than daily death rates, which largely filters out deaths brought forward by only days or weeks.

Ben Armstrong, an emeritus professor at the London School of Hygiene and Tropical Medicine, said he could not verify the 451,000 figure. But because the estimate is based on annual mortality data, it largely excludes deaths brought forward by only days or weeks. If the figure is correct, he said, most of those counted "would have lived at least six months longer."

Scientists have long estimated the death toll from extreme temperatures retrospectively. Better seasonal forecasting is increasingly allowing researchers to look ahead instead.

To produce the estimate, researchers used earlier work showing how death rates change during hot weather in different parts of the world. They combined it with seasonal forecasts showing how hot each place is expected to become.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"Putting the two together lets us estimate the human cost of this event while there's still time to do something about it, rather than counting the deaths after the fact," Carleton said.

Heat warnings, cooling centers and hydration stations, home visits to vulnerable people, protections for outdoor workers and additional health care capacity could all reduce mortality.

The researchers believe targeted interventions could save tens of thousands of lives.

For Carleton, the forecast is a warning to prepare now for the heat that climate change could make commonplace.

"This El Nino gives us a look at future weather in the 2040s," Carleton said. "These temperatures will become normal in 20 years, giving us time and reason to begin investing in adaptive capacity now."

Nobelitis — How winning a Nobel Prize can give you delusions of grandeur

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After being chosen for one of the world's most prestigious awards, some Nobel laureates lose touch with reality and suddenly start spouting unscientific nonsense. They're afflicted by what some have called "Nobelitis."

Linus Pauling won not one, but two Nobel Prizes — the first, for chemistry, in 1954 for his research into the nature of the chemical bond, and the Peace Prize in 1962 for his work in the anti-nuclear weapons movement.

The American chemist not only won two Nobel Prizes, but won them on his own, a rare feat for any scientist. Such an honor often turns laureates into global superstars overnight, with millions hanging on their every word.

That instant fame can be dangerous, said Nils Hansson, a professor in the Department of History, Theory and Ethics of Medicine at Heinrich Heine University in Düsseldorf. Especially, he said, when these world-renowned experts "start spreading crazy, unproven scientific theories" — ideas that, when disseminated by Nobel laureates, risk being taken seriously.

That's what happened with Pauling. Beginning in the mid 1960s he began claiming that vitamin C was a universal remedy, a cure for everything from cancer to cardiovascular conditions and degenerative diseases like Alzheimer's disease, multiple sclerosis or osteoarthritis.

Pauling developed an alternative treatment he called orthomolecular medicine, based on specific doses of vitamins and minerals for each patient. To this day, some 60 years later, evidence for his methods remains inconclusive.

Pauling's case is a classic example of "Nobelitis," or "Nobel disease" — not an official diagnosis, but an informal term used to describe how some laureates begin to suffer delusions of grandeur later in life. It was first coined by Paul Nurse, president of The Royal Society, the UK's national academy of sciences, and himself a winner of the Nobel Prize.

"Be skeptical of everything, including yourself and your own ideas," Nurse said in a 2019 interview with KU Leuven University in Belgium, when asked what made someone a good scientist. In cases of Nobelitis, that self-skepticism is the first to go.

That was also the case with virologist Luc Montagnier, who was jointly honored in 2008 for his discovery of the human immunodeficiency virus, HIV, with colleague Francoise Barre-Sinoussi in the early 1980s.

Montagnier would later go on to support homeopathic medicine, another alternative treatment of disease and claim that HIV transmission in Africa could be diminished through better hygiene and nutrition.

During the COVID-19 pandemic, Montagnier said the SARS-CoV-2 virus had been created in a lab with parts of the HIV genetic code. "It's difficult for a practicing virologist to say that a Nobel laureate in virology is spreading nonsense," said German virologist Christian Drosten on NDR's Coronavirus Update podcast in May 2020. "But that is utter nonsense."

James Watson, who was never known for his modesty, also let fame go to his head. In 1962, he was awarded the Nobel Prize along with Maurice Wilkins and Francis Crick for their discovery of the structure of the DNA molecule. But in the early 2000s, Watson repeatedly spread racist theories that Black people were less intelligent than white people.

For medical historian Hansson, Watson is a prime example that the Nobel Prize is not automatically awarded to a person who should be considered as a role model.

"You may have made a fantastic discovery, but that doesn't make you a universal genius," he said.

But the great expectations of an admiring public may also contribute to the development of Nobelitis. Paul Nurse, who won the Nobel Prize in physiology or medicine in 2001, soon found himself getting daily requests for interviews on all sorts of topics.

"As if suddenly I was acknowledgeable about all workings of the world and of human beings," he told DW.

Nurse had this advice for researchers: "Be careful quite what you say. Talk about things that you do know something about that would be fine. And if you don't know so much or you're not sure about something, be really clear that you're not sure."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Hansson has also noticed that the media eagerly report whatever Nobel laureates say — even when prize winners stray far from their area of expertise.

Nobelitis sufferers are leaders in their fields and rightly honored for their work with the Nobel. But they're also regular individuals, not immune to megalomania and other behavioral problems — meaning that homeopathy, orthomolecular medicine and other pseudosciences don't automatically become credible just because they're backed by a Nobel Prize winner.

This article was originally written in German.

Suspected pneumonic plague in Siberia: What we know about the disease

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After a worker at a Russian plague research institute in Siberia died October 2, 2026, nearly 200 of her contacts were under medical observation. But authorities there denied pneumonic plague had caused the death.

UPDATE: Following initial reports raising concerns that a lab worker had died accidentally of pneumonic plague, the European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control (ECDC) said it was now also "closely monitoring the situation."

However, in a statement published October 6, 2026, the ECDC said that "based on the limited information currently available, there are no reports of secondary cases and no evidence of sustained human-to-human transmission that would cause further spread of the disease."

Following the death of a Russian laboratory worker, there is speculation about an outbreak of pneumonic plague, a highly contagious bacterial infection of the lungs. Russia denies there is a broader risk to the community.

Russian officials have said the laboratory worker's death was not caused by a plague pathogen. The 28-year-old died of "pneumonia of unknown origin," according to Russia's Interfax news agency, citing Rospotrebnadzor, Russia's disease-control agency. According to that agency, samples taken from the woman showed no microorganisms "related to her professional activities." This contradicts earlier information.

Local media had previously reported that the woman, who worked at an institute dedicated to fighting plague, had dropped a test tube.

She reportedly died on Friday, October 2, 2026, at the district hospital in Shelekhov, a town near Irkutsk in Siberia.

According to official Russian figures, authorities placed two hospitals and a children's clinic under quarantine. Local reports said staff at the institute were being quarantined at their workplace. The US news site Axios has reported that around 200 people who had been in contact with the woman had been placed under observation. There were no further reported infections.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Even so, distrust of how Russia handles information persists. On one of DW's Russian-language social media channels, a user wrote: "It wouldn't surprise me if this turns into a disaster now, both in terms of the spread and the quarantine measures."

All that is known for certain, at the time of writing and first publication (October 5, 2026), is that pneumonic plague is highly contagious.

Pneumonic plague is a form of plague that infects the lungs. Like every form of the disease, it is caused by Yersinia pestis, a bacterium usually found in small mammals, such as rodents and in the fleas that feed on them.

The World Health Organization describes the pneumonic form as the most virulent type of plague.

Its incubation period can be as short as 24 hours, and it is the only form that passes easily from person to person through respiratory particles released when a sick patient coughs. This is why, according to the WHO, it can trigger severe epidemics.

Symptoms appear quickly: high fever, headache and weakness, followed by a rapidly worsening pneumonia with shortness of breath, chest pain and a cough that often brings up blood.

A person can catch pneumonic plague by breathing in the bacteria directly, or by developing it when bubonic and/or septicemic plague go untreated and spread to the lungs.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Pneumonic plague is not a separate disease. It is one of three forms of plague: bubonic, septicemic and pneumonic.

What sets them apart is where the infection settles in the body, and that shapes how the disease spreads and how deadly it is.

Bubonic plague is the most common form and the one most people think of when they hear the word "plague." It usually follows a flea bite. The bacteria travel to the nearest lymph node, which swells into a painful lump known as a bubo. According to the WHO, it rarely spreads between people.

If bubonic plague goes untreated, the bacteria can move further through the body. If they spread into the bloodstream, the disease can become septicemic plague, which causes bleeding under the skin, tissue death in the fingers and toes, and organ failure. If the bacteria reach the lungs, the illness becomes pneumonic plague, the form that can pass from person to person through the air. Both septicemic and pneumonic plague can also develop directly, without a bubo appearing first.

Left untreated, bubonic plague kills between 30-60% of patients. The septicemic and pneumonic forms are always fatal without treatment.

Pneumonic plague is curable, but only if treatment starts quickly. The WHO warns that without treatment it can kill within 18 to 24 hours of the first symptoms.

Common antibiotics can cure the infection, and recovery rates are high when patients are treated within about a day of falling ill.

Speed is what decides the outcome. Doctors are advised not to wait for laboratory confirmation and to start antibiotics as soon as plague is suspected, according to the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC).

The drugs themselves are not rare or specialized. They are standard, widely used antibiotics, and patients usually need a course of up to two weeks. To stop the infection spreading, patients are kept in isolation, and wearing masks helps protect those around them, the WHO says.

People close to a patient are treated as well, even if they have no symptoms. Both the WHO and the CDC recommend a seven-day course of preventive antibiotics for close contacts of anyone with pneumonic plague.

The WHO does not recommend a plague vaccine for the public. It reserves vaccination for high-risk groups, such as laboratory staff who work with the bacterium and health workers.

Early-onset cancer rising worldwide: Causes, risk, prevention

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Across Latin America, early-onset cancer rates have risen, especially of bowel, breast and cervical cancers. But it is a worldwide trend.

The numbers are striking. Some cancer rates have doubled in the past 20 years. It's just not yet clear whether we're seeing a rise in actual cases or whether we're seeing more early-stage cases because of better awareness, diagnosis and treatment in hospitals.

Take, for example, colorectal (or bowel) cancer: In 2001, about one in every 26,000 people under 50 in England were diagnosed with it each year. By 2023, the rate of diagnoses was about one in 12,000. Twice what it was at the turn of the century.

That's according to the World Cancer Research Fund (WCRF), an international network of charities, which analyzed registry data from England, the United States and the Netherlands. It found the number of new cancer cases had risen for seven cancers in England alone.

So, rates have doubled, but the numbers overall are still small, said Amy Berrington de Gonzalez, a professor of clinical epidemiology at the Institute of Cancer Research in London.

"Cancer is still very uncommon among young people," Berrington de Gonzalez told DW. "Three times a small number is still a small number."

But she and other scientists still want to know why this is happening, because it is a worldwide trend. Latin America is a case in point.

A 2025 study analyzed registry data from 50 countries and territories up to 2017. It found early-onset colorectal cancer rising in 27 of those regions.

In Latin America, rates rose in Chile, Argentina, Ecuador, Costa Rica and Puerto Rico. Chile's rise — at almost 4% per year — was among the highest.

In Brazil, the evidence comes directly from hospitals. At the A.C. Camargo Cancer Center in Sao Paulo, the number of colorectal cancer patients under 50 grew by 7.6% per year between 2000 and 2023. That's a five-fold increase.

The number of patients over 50 grew even faster — at 8.1% — or about six-fold.

But since these figures came from a single hospital, they do not show whether cases are rising across Brazil. They may only reflect that this one hospital is treating more patients.

"We need better cancer registration systems," said Berrington de Gonzalez, "especially in countries like Brazil and Mexico, where obesity rates have also been increasing."

Marc Gunter, professor of cancer epidemiology at Imperial College London, said he expected Latin America to follow richer countries as diets there become more Westernized and lifestyles more sedentary. "I think it's already happening," he said.

In another study in 2025, involving 42 countries, Berrington de Gonzalez compared data from two age groups — 20 to 49 years and 50 years and older — between 2003 and 2017.

Six cancers — thyroid, breast, colorectal, kidney, uterine and leukemia — had risen among the younger groups in most countries. All but colorectal cancer were seen to rise in older adults, too.

In fact, colorectal cancer stood out. In about a third of the countries, it rose faster in younger adults than in older ones. "It is the one cancer that seems to be increasing in younger people whilst decreasing in older people," said Berrington de Gonzalez.

This has consequences for research. If cancers are rising in all age groups, the causes are likely to be widespread, so studies looking only at young people risk chasing complicated explanations with too little data to test them, she said.

A US study of pancreatic cancer in adults aged 15 to 39 years found the rise was almost entirely made up of early-stage tumors.

The authors said this suggested that instead of the number of cancer cases going up, it was the number of early diagnoses that had gone up. And that can be explained by better awareness in the community, with people getting checked more often.

But the data for colorectal cancer looks different: Some US studies show it rising in advanced stages of the disease as well, which suggests an increase in the number of cases, not just the number of diagnoses.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Obesity is an established cause of at least 13 cancers, according to the WHO's International Agency for Research on Cancer (IARC).

According to a 2021 review of existing studies, young adults with obesity were almost twice as likely to develop colorectal cancer before the age of 50.

Obesity has also spread fastest among the young. The WHO reports that obesity rates have doubled among adults since the 1990s and quadrupled among adolescents.

Some also say ultra-processed foods and microplastics cause cancer. But Gunter said the evidence was inconclusive.

"There's a lot of hype," Gunter said, adding that more research was needed. Berrington de Gonzalez agreed: "Diets have changed, but we don't yet know the role they might play."

Gunter said individuals should not be blamed for living in societies built around convenience foods and desk jobs, and that lasting change needed public health policy.

But he also said that young people could lower their cancer risk by maintaining a healthy weight, moving more, eating more fiber and cutting back on processed meat.

"Going for a walk, being more physically active, eating more fruits and vegetables," he said. "Those things are maybe not so trendy, but they are really important."

For a 30-year-old reading this, Berrington de Gonzalez's message was one of reassurance: Cancer at a young age remains relatively rare.

How green is Europe's urban public transport? A data-driven reality check

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Greener buses and cleaner fleets are reshaping public transport across Europe. But are cities moving fast enough to meet their zero-emission goals?

An electric plug icon on their windows makes them easy to spot in cities like Brussels and Madrid. Quietly navigating the streets, electric buses have become an increasingly common sight on Europe's streets in recent years.

Michela Le Pira, assistant professor of transportation at Italy's University of Catania, says removing tailpipe emissions — the gases and particles generated when a combustion vehicle is running — might be "the easiest strategy to improve public transport."

Most Europeans live in urban settings, and road transport harms air quality. In 2023, almost 300,000 deaths across the EU were caused by air pollution levels exceeding the World Health Organization's (WHO) recommended thresholds.

Of all fine particulate matter (PM2.5) concentrations in European urban areas, about 14% come from road transport, including public, private and freight transport. Additionally, towns and cities account for nearly a quarter of the European Union's transport greenhouse gas emissions (GHG).

DW and the European Data Journalism Network evaluated more than a decade's worth of data from more than 100 reports and sources on public transit and the emissions it causes, and identified 148 sustainability commitments by local authorities and transport operators in 33 cities across Europe. The analysis focuses on motorized mass transit, leaving out bikes and pedestrians as well as taxis, shared vehicles and e-scooters.

The EU wants to become the first climate-neutral continent by 2050. A key part of that plan is cutting transport-related GHG emissions by 90% from 1990 levels.

As DW's analysis shows, Copenhagen is the only EU capital that has almost electrified its entire bus fleet — its vehicles do not have combustion engines and therefore do not produce tailpipe emissions. In cities like Rome, Lisbon and Nicosia, more than 90% of buses still run on diesel, and metros and trams account for less than 10% of their public transport fleet.

Although cars still dominate European streets, "electric buses are one way to reduce local air pollution," said Martin Baierl, data analyst with Transport & Environment, an advocacy non-profit for clean transport.

EU funds enabled many cities to increase their electric and alternative fuel bus fleets from 2021 on.

Electric buses comprise 20% of the total fleet in EU capitals and selected cities. Their number increased by 55% between 2021 and 2025 — equivalent to 1,234 more such vehicles on the road. The number of diesel-only buses has been declining since then, although they still outnumber those running on alternative fuel.

In addition to decarbonizing the bus fleet and helping to reduce air pollution, embracing electric models can help to lower noise and public transport operating costs. All the more so if the city produces its own electricity, explains Baierl. However, the outlay for e-buses is higher than for diesel models. In 20 out of the 31 cities with data to analyze, over 50% of the bus fleet is made up of traditional vehicles.

Brussels, for example, was an early adopter of moving away from traditional diesel-powered buses, switching to hybrids instead. Now the Belgian capital is in the process of electrifying its fleet, but at a slower pace. "It's not easy to know what the right technology will be in the end," Baierl said.

Another example is hydrogen-powered buses, which also produce zero emissions but are more costly to operate than their electric counterparts.

Transportation is the only major sector where greenhouse gas emissions remain above 1990 levels, despite the EU's ambition of a 90% reduction by 2050.

On average, public transport — including buses, trams and metros — is used for more than a quarter of trips in the 10 largest EU capitals, according to recent data from the non-profit group CDP (formerly the Carbon Disclosure Project). In Stockholm, the share is 49%, while in the smaller Slovenian capital of Ljubljana, it's 11%. DW analysis of transport sustainability pledges reveals that only four cities have explicitly committed to achieving zero emissions in buses or all public transport.

Yannis Papadimitriou lives 85 kilometers (about 53 miles) away from Athens, the EU capital with the highest air pollution mortality risk. He travels to work by commuter rail, a means of transport he'd like to see promoted more.

"For me it is a very conscious choice to use electric public transport, especially trains, which are the safest, most environmentally friendly and quickest way of transport."

Though trains, trams and metros run on electricity, there are more buses within city boundaries, and in many urban areas the majority still run on diesel.

In Northern Europe, 15 kilometers away from Copenhagen, Poul Kattler also used to commute to work. He is now retired and takes the bus more frequently with his grandchildren. Despite Copenhagen having an almost completely fossil-fuel-free public transport system (only one bus runs on biomethane), the metro expansion, which is responding to the city's growth, comes with its own set of concerns.

"The whole construction phase [of the new metro line] is extremely environmentally demanding," Kattler said. If the construction emissions are considered, "the CO2 balance would be very poor for the metro now."

But in the long run, the benefits of a new metro line in reducing CO2 emissions outweigh its construction-related emissions, Le Pira said.

Combustion or exhaust emissions are not the only way transport pollutes the air and the environment. Indirect emissions, like those coming from electricity generation or fuel production, add to transport's CO2 footprint. Brakes and wheels release non-exhaust particle matter when wagons or vehicles slow down.

Since 1992, buses and any vehicle running on fossil fuels in the EU have been subject to stricter emissions standards. Fine particle emissions from buses and heavy-duty vehicles had dropped by 92% in 2022 according to the European Environment Agency. Yet overall road transport non-exhaust emissions from tire wear have increased 58% in two decades, as cars have become heavier.

With a few exceptions, EU member states are not close to cutting greenhouse gas transport emissions by 90%, as envisioned by the EU's 2050 target.

Zero-emission public transport is one step toward decarbonizing transport in cities and meeting GHG reduction goals. The next challenge is to get people out of their cars.

"If we talk about sustainable mobility in general and not only clean public transport, the main problem is the modal shift from private cars to public transport," Le Pira said.

On average, private automobiles account for more than a third of transport in the largest EU capitals, according to CDP data.

Cheaper ticket prices for groups, like students, and disincentives for car use, such as low-emission zones, are two complementary ways of attracting more passengers while reducing private transport.

In her research work, Le Pira investigates how public transport can be more efficient and appealing for city dwellers, for example, by combining transport modes where people step off their bikes and onto a train. This kind of solution could be particularly relevant for small cities "where cars are still the main transport mode."

According to Baierl, places with good public transport draw people in. "Cities where not everybody has to use a car are just more livable," he said.

Edited by: Gianna-Carina Grün and Sarah Steffen

DW's data team contributed research to this project.

Brazil's elections matter for the planet. This is why

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Brazil is heading back to the polls, but the impact could be felt worldwide. With the Amazon vital to the health of the planet, the election could help shape its future.

Few national elections carry as much environmental weight as Brazil's presidential race. And this one is being watched from far beyond the country's boundaries.

Home to the largest section of the Amazon rainforest, Brazil is guardian of a carbon sink of vital importance, making the country's conservation policies globally relevant.

Environmentalists have already voiced alarm at Flávio Bolsonaro — son of right-wing former president Jair Bolsonaro — coming out 2% ahead of incumbent Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva in the first round of elections.

"It's going to be a second coming of Bolsonaro, therefore a more radical Bolsonaro," said Claudio Angelo, international policy coordinator at Climate Observatory, a network of Brazilian NGOs.

"At the risk of sounding alarmist, I would say Lula's re-election is the only thing standing between us and the end of the Amazon."

The Amazon rainforest stores huge amounts of the carbon dioxide (CO2) released into the atmosphere from the burning of fossil fuels. As a result, it helps regulate the Earth's climate.

For decades, the tropical forest has acted as one of nature's most powerful defenses against climate change. But it has also suffered from widespread deforestation.

During Jair Bolsonaro's presidency, from 2019 until 2023, environmental agencies were weakened and enforcement efforts curtailed, meaning tropical forest was cleared at unprecedented rates.

"The environment agenda was disregarded and the level of control the government had on pollution overall, but specifically on deforestation, was reversed," said Claudio Forner, head of climate policy at Berlin-based think tank Climate Analytics.

Over Bolsonaro's four-year term, an estimated 45,586km2 of the Amazon was destroyed, an area bigger than the size of Denmark. And the forest is not as resilient as it once was, with scientists warning that parts of it are now emitting more carbon than they absorb.

Lula came to office with a message of protection for the Amazon. Besides calling for an end to deforestation by 2030, he increased monitoring of the forest and strengthened Brazil's environmental enforcement agency IBAMA.

Deforestation has plummeted under his leadership, with 2026 set to be its lowest level in a decade. That represents a 41% drop overall compared to Jair Bolsonaro's tenure.

Experts have already criticized Flávio Bolsonaro's record on forest protection. As a senator in 2019, he authored draft legislation that could have stark impacts for the Amazon. While landowners are currently required to preserve 80% of their property for the growth of native plants, the bill would allow them to use their holdings as they please.

"The signal from the president will say 'now you can deforest' or 'it's harder to deforest,'" said Ana Yang, director of the Environment and Society Center at London-based think tank Chatham House.

"The moment that you decrease enforcement capability, deforestation grows," she told DW.

Bolsonaro has also pledged to end illegal deforestation by 2029, a year before Lula. But Claudio Angelo is critical of that message.

"He will end illegal deforestation not by enforcing the law, but by making it legal," he said.

More generally, environmental experts fear Bolsonaro might dismantle many of the protections strengthened under the current president.

"Unfortunately, climate change has become a partisan issue, so rather than offering a solution from their perspective, the right pictures climate change as an invented problem...that regulation goes against economic growth or it goes against competitiveness, that it's the aim of the left to control the means of production," Forner said.

In an interview with Brazilian media in August, Bolsonaro said he regarded extreme weather events to be cyclical and did not believe they were solely linked to human influence.

A Bolsonaro win is likely to see Brazil move away from its position as a climate leader, a role the country embraced last year as it hosted the COP30 climate conference in the Amazonian city of Belem.

But even a Lula victory would not represent a straightforward climate success story, say experts. While the current administration has overseen significant reductions in deforestation, it has also backed plans to expand oil production.

Angelo said the decision to allow exploratory drilling was a "betrayal" of Lula's mandate but added that the president's overall record on climate had been "quite impressive".

"Lula is the world's worst climate leader, except for all the others," he told DW.

The current president will face a runoff election with Bolsonaro on October 25. But while experts say the outcome could determine the fate of the Amazon and the wider efforts to curb global warming, for many Brazilians climate is not high on their list of priorities.

"Ultimately what people care about is access to education, sanitation and health," said Yang. A road built through the Amazon to access those services, while criticized from outside Brazil for destroying parts of the rainforest, would be desirable to voters, she added.

"They don't have time to care. Not because they don't want to. They care about their own survival," she said.

The United States has pressed Ukraine to suspend strikes on Russian oil refineries, which it argues have added to high energy prices, a sensitive issue for the Trump administration amid its war on Iran, as midterm elections approach next month. File | Photo Credit: Reuters

A Russian strike on Ukraine’s south-eastern city of Zaporizhzhia injured four people overnight, regional Governor Mykhailo Semikin said on Telegram on Monday (October 26, 2026).

Two more were injured in Russian strikes on the Chernihiv region, Ukraine’s emergency service said on the messaging app. Also on Telegram, the Russian Defence Ministry said it hit a power station in Ukraine’s second-largest city of Kharkiv, a warehouse in the surrounding region, and another power station in the Khmelnitskiy region. Updated - October 26, 2026 11:51 am IST - Amman

The interior of a Greek Orthodox church is seen through a shattered window hours after gunmen opened fire during a funeral, in Hamoud village, in Jordan's Karak governorate, Friday, Oct. 23, 2026. | Photo Credit: AP

Jordanian security forces arrested three men on Monday (October 26, 2026) for an attack on a Greek Orthodox Church that killed nine people and wounded more than two dozen.

The Public Security Directorate said the three suspects were arrested following a dawn shootout and that all three were wounded.

No members of the security forces were injured in the dawn operation in Karak, south of Jordan's capital, Amman. Karak is home to a minority Christian population.

The attack on the Greek Orthodox Church in Karak took place on Friday (October 23, 2026) during a funeral. Three men were reported to have taken part in the attack.

There was still no claim of responsibility, and no armed group has been accused of the attack.

Jordan's Interior Minister Mazen Al-Faraya identified the three men as those responsible for the attack.

“The security services have fulfilled their duties, resulting in the capture of those who carried out this terrorist act,” he said in a phone interview with state TV Al-Mamlaka.

“They put up fierce resistance against the security forces, but were ultimately subdued and taken into custody, having sustained severe injuries.”

He added that there “are three individuals involved; they were identified early on in the wake of the terrorist incident, and the security services employed all the means at their disposal to apprehend them”.

King Abdullah II directed authorities to respond with “an iron fist” against what he called the outlaws of Islam and anyone attempting to undermine the unity of the Jordanian people.

China’s Communist Party elite hold key talks on governance

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Updated - October 26, 2026 11:37 am IST - Beijing

Chinese President Xi Jinping. File | Photo Credit: AP

China’s President Xi Jinping opened a key meeting of Communist Party elites in Beijing on Monday (October 26, 2026), state media said, which observers will be closely watching for clues as to the country’s direction and future leadership.

Roughly 300 members of the ruling Communist Party’s Central Committee in attendance at the meeting, called the fifth plenum, will be looking to tighten discipline within their ranks, according to state media.

That includes measures to reduce corruption and make cadres more effective and loyal.

Mr. Xi opened the session on Monday (October 26, 2026), presenting a draft decision by the Central Committee that “sets out strategic plans for strengthening full and rigorous Party self-governance”, state news agency Xinhua reported.

Few details from the closed-door gathering, held under tight security, will be made known until it concludes on Thursday (October 29, 2026), but the timing of the meeting is crucial.

The fifth plenum comes ahead of a major party congress next year, where 73-year-old Mr. Xi is almost certain to secure a historic fourth term and oversee a leadership reshuffle.

Previous plenums have represented key moments for Mr. Xi to cement his authority.

The Chinese leader has waged a 14-year campaign against corruption that has purged thousands of officials, including dozens of the Central Committee.

Yet he remains frustrated with cadres he deems are not implementing his orders.

State media published an article ahead of the plenum, based on a speech Mr. Xi gave in January in which he railed against “two-faced” officials who appear to comply with central directives but “inwardly resist”, pursue “individualism” and are “politically opportunistic”.

Analysts told AFP that Mr. Xi is trying to maximise efficiency and loyalty among cadres by imposing more supervision and rules.

But as a result of his expansive purges, this year’s plenum is expected to have the lowest turnout since former leader Mao Zedong’s death in 1976.

Eight of the Central Committee’s seats lie vacant, with another 15 members under investigation and at risk of being replaced.

Mr. Xi’s anti-corruption drive has also targeted the military, toppling five of the seven members of the Central Military Commission (CMC), the party’s top armed forces body.

Of 44 military members appointed to the Central Committee in 2022, only two have not been purged or disappeared, according to the Asia Society Policy Institute.

The plenum could see new members appointed to the CMC; the party could wait until next year’s congress to replenish it; or Xi might even restructure party control over its vast military.

The talks also come at an unsteady time for the world’s second-largest economy, which has struggled to address persistently sluggish domestic spending and a protracted crisis in the crucial property sector.

Turbulent political and economic ties with the United States are also weighing on China’s leaders.

Authorities are expected to release a summary of the outcomes after the meeting concludes on Thursday (October 29, 2026).

Published - October 26, 2026 10:06 am IST

China / defence / national security / government

Published - October 26, 2026 09:36 am IST - Riyadh

Large plume of black smoke rises from the fuel storage area at King Khalid International Airport, in Riyadh. Image used for representation purpose only. | Photo Credit: Reuters

The Saudi-led military coalition said on Monday (October 26, 2026) it intercepted a ballistic missile fired by Yemen’s Houthis, after the Iran-backed group claimed a new attack on the international airport in Riyadh.

The operation came a day after explosions rattled the Saudi capital and authorities sent text messages to residents warning them of a “hostile aerial threat” targeting the city.

The Houthis have carried out waves of strikes on the King Khalid International Airport in Riyadh, including an attack this month that killed 13 people and injured more than 300, the deadliest in the kingdom in more than a decade.

Coalition spokesman Turki Al-Malki said on Monday (October 26, 2026) that air defences “intercepted and destroyed a ballistic missile” fired by the Houthis toward Riyadh, and destroyed two ballistic missile launchers in Yemen’s capital Sanaa.

“We continue the process of dismantling the ballistic missile network of the terrorist Houthi militia,” Al-Malki wrote on X.

Houthi military spokesman Yahya Saree said Monday the group “successfully targeted” the airport with a ballistic missile, and struck oil pumping stations with drones.

“The Yemeni Armed Forces carried out two qualitative military operations targeting the Saudi enemy troop concentrations and weapons depots” in the Aden and Lahj governorates in Yemen, which caused “dozens of casualties”, Saree added.

On Sunday (October 25, 2026), the Houthis said they had launched an attack on the airport in Riyadh, after the kingdom announced that flights had resumed.

The airport closure was a major blow for Saudi Arabia, forcing the postponement of the kingdom’s flagship investment conference until 2027 and disrupting travel plans for thousands of passengers.

The Dakar Rally also joined a growing list of events that were cancelled, postponed or relocated amid the fallout from the war.

Saree had earlier warned that Riyadh airport was still “unsafe” and a “military target”, and urged airlines and travellers to avoid it.

“The Saudi regime shall bear the consequences of any operations at the airport,” the spokesman said.

Saudi Arabia and the government it backs in Yemen have come under sustained attack in recent weeks from the Houthis as a years-long truce broke down in the summer, shifting the focus of the wider US-Iran war that erupted in February.

As operations resumed at Riyadh airport, Greek Prime Minister Kyriakos Mitsotakis met Saudi de facto ruler Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman.

During the meeting, Mitsotakis “responded favourably to the kingdom’s request to keep the Patriot system in Saudi Arabia, given the current challenging circumstances”, his office said.

Greece has deployed a battery to operate the US-made Patriot air defence system in Yanbu, a major oil export hub on the Red Sea coast.

On Sunday, the Saudi-led military coalition said it intercepted at least 10 ballistic missiles.

“The bombardment has been very heavy since this morning -- may God keep us safe. Everything is shaking because of the explosions,” one resident in Saudi Arabia’s Khamis Mushait told AFP on Sunday (October 25, 2026) on condition of anonymity.

The kingdom’s civil defence agency later said two people were injured in the south by falling debris, also reporting damage to several buildings and vehicles.

Images released by the SPA showed a building with part of its upper facade destroyed, exposing metal rods. Another image captured large windows shattered, with shards of glass strewn across the pavement and part of the road.

Head of Hong Kong’s new drug regulator pledges to build trust, push innovation

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Li Zili, controller of Centre for Medical Products Regulation, says body will use strategic edge to assess and bring Chinese drugs to the world

The head of Hong Kong’s new drug regulatory body has pledged to build a fully capable and globally trusted agency that will become a catalyst for medical innovation rather than merely acting as a “miniature version” of existing overseas models.

Dr Li Zili, controller of the Hong Kong Centre for Medical Products Regulation (CMPR), said that while many other places in Asia, such as mainland China, Singapore and Japan, had established regulatory agencies long ago, Hong Kong had a distinctive competitive edge.

“We are not going to be a mini-FDA, nor a miniature version of any regulatory body in the world,” he said, referring to the United States Food and Drug Administration.

Published - October 26, 2026 08:43 am IST - Rio de Janeiro

Opposition presidential candidate Sen. Flavio Bolsonaro arrives at his election night event after the presidential runoff in Brasilia, Brazil, Sunday, October 25, 2026. | Photo Credit: AP

Brazil's President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, a vocal critic of President Donald Trump, was reelected on Sunday (October 25, 2026) in a result that provides relief to critics of widening U.S. influence in Latin America.

With 100% of the vote counted, Brazil's electoral court announced that the soon-to-be 81-year-old Mr. Lula had a lead of nearly 4.4 million votes — 51.8% of the total vote — over opponent Flávio Bolsonaro. The announcement capped an evening of chaos at polling places across Brazil, following a last-minute decision to extend voting by two hours.

Late Sunday (October 25, 2026), Mr. Bolsonaro refused to concede defeat, calling the move to extend voting hours unprecedented — though both he and Mr. Lula made the request — and asked for the election results to be annulled, saying they were impossible to accept.

Mr. Lula's triumph bucks a trend in Latin America where increasing numbers of right-of-centre presidential candidates allied with Mr. Trump have been winning. Mr. Lula's victory leaves Mexico and Brazil, Latin America's two most populous nations, with left-of-centre leaders. It also positions Latin America's largest economy, with a GDP of $2.6 trillion per year, to remain independent of the U.S., despite the pressure of increased tariffs imposed by Mr. Trump.

A video published by Mr. Lula's campaign shows the veteran leader celebrating with First Lady Rosângela da Silva and allies after his runoff victory was confirmed by Brazil's electoral court.

Wearing star-shaped red glasses, he kissed his wife. Hundreds of his supporters began gathering on São Paulo's main artery to celebrate the victory.

In Rio de Janeiro, cars beeped their horns and a woman dressed in red — the colour of Mr. Lula’s supporters — shouted “You lost! Cry harder!” to four men in yellow and green sipping beers, Mr. Bolsonaro's signature colours. A huge roar echoed in the street as the result was confirmed.

Rodrigo Valentim, a 51-year-old businessman and supporter of Mr. Bolsonaro, said that another Lula government would harm the economy. He pointed to financial markets' positive reaction to the result of the first round, when Mr. Bolsonaro bested Mr. Lula.

“I don't understand how people can vote for Mr. Lula,” he said. “The country is collapsing.”

Mr. Bolsonaro had yet to respond to Mr. Lula's apparent victory, which, if confirmed by electoral authorities, will give him his non-consecutive fourth term as President. The next term begins on January 5, 2027.

The electoral court's announcement ended an evening of confusion at polling places after Brazilian Supreme Court Justice Cristiano Zanin ruled three minutes before the scheduled end of voting to keep polls open for two more hours, following requests by both candidates. Mr. Lula's request came after issues with buses that provide transportation to polls in Sao Paulo and Salvador, two cities that tend to favour the incumbent. Mr. Bolsonaro's request was due to heavy rains in Brazil's south, where he is favoured.

In Brasilia, volunteers who oversaw the vote count at a school debated whether they needed to stay after the announcement to extend voting, but most left 15 to 20 minutes after polls closed and they had given count information to electoral authorities. At a polling station in Rio de Janeiro, the voting machines had already been switched off minutes after polls were set to close and couldn't be turned back on.

The decision delayed tallying the votes by two hours. It was unclear how many polling stations stayed open after the scheduled closing time.

Mr. Bolsonaro’s ally Nikolas Ferreira noted that Mr. Zanin, who made the decision, is Mr. Lula's former lawyer.

Mr. Bolsonaro bested Mr. Lula by more than 2.2 million votes in the first round of the election earlier this month. Sunday (October 25, 2026) night's apparent victory by Mr. Lula marked the first time since Brazil's military dictatorship ended in 1985 that the second-highest vote-getter in the first-round elections won the presidency.

Mr. Lula faced a tough reelection battle because of concerns about the cost of living and security. Mr. Bolsonaro made a tough-on-crime approach the cornerstone of his campaign and pledged to bring down food costs while Mr. Lula, trying to lure undecided voters, has invested in costly incentives.

Both have been hit by corruption suspicions involving family members and allies, with Mr. Bolsonaro implicated in a sprawling scandal rocking Brazil. Mr. Lula previously spent more than a year in prison on corruption convictions that were eventually annulled.

This will likely be the last victory of the veteran leader's political career. Mr. Lula began his political career in the 1970s as a union leader. He then helped found the Workers' Party, which he represented as a presidential candidate three times until he finally won it in 2002. He was reelected four years later and was succeeded by one of his cabinet ministers, Dilma Rousseff, in 2010. She was reelected in 2014.

Ms. Rousseff was impeached for accounting manoeuvering in 2016 amid a sprawling corruption probe, which also ensnared Mr. Lula. He tried to run for president again in 2018 but was jailed in March after being convicted.

Mr. Lula was released from prison after 580 days and Brazil's Supreme Court annulled his sentence after police found messages that showed the judge who convicted him was biased. He quickly became one of former President Jair Bolsonaro's biggest critics and narrowly beat him in 2022.

Since he lost the first round, Mr. Lula reorganised his campaign, appealed to young voters, put millions of Brazilians on the streets to defend democratic values and his legacy, raised fears of another Bolsonaro administration, and boosted several government initiatives.

He joins Mr. Trump and Nicaragua's President Daniel Ortega — all aged 80 — as the oldest presidents in the Americas.

Published - October 26, 2026 08:43 am IST

Brazil / election / politics / political campaigns

Published - October 26, 2026 08:17 am IST - Madrid

A bus crash in the Barcelona area of Spain injured more than 80 people, local emergency services said on Sunday (October 25, 2026).

The Catalan medical emergency services said 83 people were injured, including seven people seriously, 18 less seriously and 58 with minor injuries, in the crash in Fogars de Monclús near Barcelona.

Spanish news agency EFE said the bus had veered off a narrow, winding road and fallen down a 20-metre (65-foot) embankment on Sunday (October 25) afternoon.

The exact circumstances of the crash were not immediately clear and were under investigation, EFE said. Emergency services said they deployed 23 ambulances and two medical helicopters to the scene, as well as three joint teams of firefighters and medical personnel and three psychological support teams.

Olga Pane, Health Minister of the Generalitat of Catalonia, said rescue crews were searching the area to determine whether there might be any more victims.

EFE reported that the fire service also dispatched specialist mountain rescue teams.

Gina Rinehart takes fight for helipad at Hancock’s Perth HQ to tribunal

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

You have reached your maximum number of saved items.

Remove items from your saved list to add more.

You've used all of your gift articles. 3 more will become available on the 1st of every month.

Gina Rinehart’s company Hancock Prospecting has taken its fight to build a helipad atop its West Perth headquarters to the State Administrative Tribunal after it was knocked back by the local council.

The City of Perth grounded plans for the helipad at the Ord Street building in December, despite two councillors backing the idea of a helicopter landing in the city precinct, and the lord mayor describing Rinehart as an “amazing Australian”.

Opposition to the helipad stemmed from concerns over noise, whether it was conducive with the area’s existing planning scheme, and whether allowing the CBD’s first private-use helipad would set an undesirable precedent.

The area, in West Perth, is an office and residential precinct, with the City of Perth flagging nearby sites could accommodate more residents in the future.

But Ord Street Holdings counsel Grant Donaldson, KC, in his opening statement in SAT on Monday, argued the helipad would only be used in “daylight hours” between midday and 7pm up to 12 times a year for “occasional corporate transport”.

“That’s 144 minutes per annum, that equates to the sound of an electric kettle boiling for six minutes, once every fortnight, between the hours of noon and 7pm,” he said.

“This case boils down to what negative impact is this use going to have on the objects of the current or new scheme?”

The mining giant has previously likened Perth to Tokyo and New York as a “global city”, saying “rooftop helipads are commonplace across the world’s leading cities, and our proposal would elevate Perth’s status”.

But City of Perth barrister Tim Houweling said the fundamental issue in the case was noise.

Neighbouring buildings have also objected to the helipad.

The Child and Adolescent Health Service made a submission to the City of Perth stating that sudden, intermittent helicopter noise could interrupt testing of children with conditions including autism, sensory issues, ADHD or anxiety.

The submission also raised concerns that its roof is old and made of asbestos, and helicopter downwash could disrupt the asbestos dust that has settled.

After the council initially refused the helipad application, Hancock Prospecting lodged a revised development plan outlining a new flight path, in a bid to get the request over the line.

Previous plans had the helicopter approaching from the east or west of the eye-popping five-storey headquarters, in a route that would have seen it come down over the inner-city suburb of Subiaco or a mix of residential and office buildings in West Perth.

Instead, the new flight path has the eight-seat Bell 429 helicopter approaching the building from the south-west, tracking Thomas Street on the outer fringe of Perth’s CBD botanic garden Kings Park, before turning and descending once it reaches Ord Street.

A Hancock Prospecting spokesperson said the company’s original application was “developed with expert input and independent technical analysis which confirmed the helipad and flight path can operate safely and appropriately”.

“However, as part of our extensive good faith efforts to reach agreement with the City of Perth, we have proposed an amended flight path and further reduced the proposed operating timeframes,” they said.

SAT judge Henry Jackson is presiding over the appeal, and will make a ruling on the helipad at the conclusion of the two-day trial which will hear from acoustic and planning experts.

A Hancock Prospecting spokesperson said the company’s original application was “developed with expert input and independent technical analysis which confirmed the helipad and flight path can operate safely and appropriately”.

“However, as part of our extensive good faith efforts to reach agreement with the City of Perth, we have proposed an amended flight path and further reduced the proposed operating timeframes,” they said.

SAT judge Henry Jackson is presiding over the appeal, and will make a ruling on the helipad at the conclusion of the two-day trial which will hear from acoustic and planning experts.

How to help your body adjust after the clocks changed last night

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

While these strategies won’t offer a guaranteed overnight reset, they will help you get back on track

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

An extra hour in bed sounds like a good deal. But while our phones may update to the autumn time change automatically, our body clock isn’t quite so quick.

You may even find yourself waking up earlier than normal and ready for bed before your evening has properly begun.

The reason for this partly lies in our circadian system: the roughly 24-hour internal timing system that helps organise tiredness and alertness. Changing the time on a clock does not instantly reset that system.

But there are some things you can do to support your body clock in adapting to the time change.

While these strategies won’t offer a guaranteed overnight reset, they will help you get back on track.

Your body doesn’t judge whether it’s day or night by using the numbers on a clock.

Rather, a tiny region in the brain called the suprachiasmatic nucleus (SCN) – which acts as the body’s master clock – uses light signals from the eyes to help keep our circadian rhythm on track.

Bright evening light can suppress melatonin, a hormone that signals biological night, and shift your internal clock later.

This can help explain why some people feel less sleepy at their usual bedtime during the long, bright evenings of summer.

But even the light from electronic devices such as phones or e-readers can contribute to melatonin suppression.

So as your intended bedtime approaches, switch to lower, softer lighting and reduce screen brightness. It’s recommended you keep light exposure low in the hours before bed and make the bedroom as dark as practical during sleep.

Of course, the autumn time change often means we’re tired before our usual bedtime as the early nights draw in.

Shifting your body clock a little later can be helpful if you’re becoming sleepy and waking too early.

To do this, only start winding down before the bedtime you want. Don’t spend the entire evening in darkness as soon as the sun sets.

A walk, an outdoor lunch break or simply spending more of the day outside can strengthen the contrast between bright days and dim evenings.

That contrast helps synchronise the circadian system, which evolved under a much stronger daily light–dark cycle than many indoor lifestyles provide.

When you get this daylight is important, however.

Morning light generally moves the body clock earlier, while evening light generally pushes it later.

Ronan Lordan is a Postdoctoral Researcher in Circadian Biology at the FutureNeuro Research Ireland Centre and the School of Pharmacy and Biomolecular Sciences at RCSI University of Medicine and Health Sciences.

This article is republished from The Conversation under a Creative Commons licence. Read the original article.

That distinction matters when the clocks go back, because someone waking too early doesn’t necessarily need another signal to wake earlier.

After the clocks change, try to get daylight during your normal waking day rather than deliberately seeking bright light at an unusually early hour. A lunchtime walk is one practical option.

If early-evening sleepiness is the problem, spending time outdoors later in the afternoon, while daylight remains, may be better than prioritising first-thing morning light.

In fact, a 2024 modelling study predicted that later-day light exposure could speed the autumn adjustment. However, the authors emphasised that these proposed interventions still need experimental verification.

Think of daylight as a useful daily cue – not a precisely proven clock-change prescription.

It’s tempting to treat the extra hour of sleep as permission to overhaul your weekend. But a regular routine is key.

Sleep depends on both the pressure that builds while you’re awake and the circadian signals that determine when you feel sleepy or alert. These processes interact, which is why sleep can’t always be summoned simply by deciding to go to bed.

Aim for reasonably consistent bedtimes and waking times according to the time change. Avoid swinging between very early nights, long lie-ins and late bedtimes. Keeping weekday and weekend schedules similar can support healthy sleep.

If you find that even with a consistent sleep schedule you’re struggling to fall asleep, choose a quiet activity done in low light until you feel ready for bed.

Dark autumn mornings can also make waking up feel harder.

Keeping a broadly consistent waking time, getting morning daylight when possible and allowing enough time for sleep may help maintain a stable sleep–wake rhythm – although some grogginess immediately after waking is normal.

The earlier nights that accompany the winter months can make it harder to get through the afternoon.

When you feel groggy, coffee can look like the obvious solution. But as much as it pains me to give this advice as a coffee lover, you might want to skip that midday coffee.

Caffeine interferes with sleep long after its effects have worn off. A recent, small study found that a 400mg dose of caffeine (equivalent to around 3-5 cups of coffee) affected sleep even when consumed eight hours before bedtime.

That’s quite a substantial dose, of course – and not one many of us are consuming in the mid-afternoon.

Fortunately, the study also found that when a 100 mg dose of caffeine (equivalent to a single cup of coffee) was consumed four hours before bedtime, it had no effect on sleep compared to a placebo.

If sleep is difficult, moving your last caffeinated drink earlier and reducing the amount you drink are sensible things to try.

Evening caffeine can also push the body clock later, so avoiding it may help when you’re trying to keep an earlier sleep schedule.

The autumn time change is a useful reminder that sleep depends on more than the number displayed on your phone’s clock.

Most people adjust to the time change within a few days to about a week, although some can take longer. But if you’re still struggling a month later, the clock change may not be the whole story.

Serbia's student movement ousts longtime leader in Parliament poll

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Account subscription benefits alongside Premium Stories, Editorials, Opinions and more. Unlock these with Subscription

Updated - October 26, 2026 07:35 am IST - Belgrade

Supporters of the electoral list "Student List - Students Win" celebrate after Aleksandar Vucic, Serbian Progressive Party (SNS) prime ministerial candidate, conceded defeat in the parliamentary election in Belgrade, Serbia. | Photo Credit: Reuters

Serbia's student-led movement declared victory in parliamentary elections Sunday (October 25, 2026) ending 14 years in power for Aleksandar Vucic's right-wing populist party.

The victory in the snap vote comes after nearly two years of anti-government protests sparked by the 2024 collapse of a canopy at Novi Sad railway station that killed 16 people.

The disaster has been widely blamed on corruption and triggered some of Serbia's largest protests since the fall of strongman Slobodan Milosevic in 2000.

"Preliminary results show that students are convincingly leading in almost all polling stations," Radomir Saicic, a candidate for the Students' List, said in a speech at the movement's election HQ.

"Students won and with them also a hope that Serbia can be a country of justice, freedom and dignity."

Voters cast ballots in huge numbers on Sunday (October 25, 2026), with the country recording its highest parliamentary election turnout in more than three decades.

Mr. Vucic conceded defeat less than two hours after polls closed, triggering raucous scenes in the capital as supporters of the movement celebrated the defeat of a government they blamed for deep-rooted corruption.

"I feel incredible," student Mila Martic told AFP from the List's headquarters. "I feel as though I am finally living in a free country."

Thousands poured into the capital's streets to celebrate the victory, with passing cars honking, crowds chanting, and fireworks lighting up the skyline.

According to the country's election officials, nearly 65% of its 6.4 million registered voters had cast their ballots by 6:00 p.m. (1700 GMT), two hours before polls closed.

The figure surpassed the 1993 parliamentary election, when just over 61% voted.

During his speech to supporters, Mr. Vucic said preliminary figures suggested the student movement could win between 135 and 140 seats, handing it an outright majority in the 250-seat parliament.

The 56-year-old statesman, who has maintained close ties with Russia throughout his tenure, blamed foreign powers for the result.

"I want people to know, this was the validation of a revolution carried out with the direct support of various European countries and the U.S. deep state, the one that is also against President Trump," he said.

For nearly two years, students have staged rallies and marched across the Balkan nation to reach voters directly and counter the media machine of Vucic's Serbian Progressive Party.

Their protests have drawn huge crowds, a final pre-election rally in Belgrade on Thursday (October 22, 2026) attracting around 150,000 people, according to an independent protest monitor.

EU chief Ursula von der Leyen and Hungarian Prime Minister Peter Magyar congratulated the Students' List on their victory.

"We stand ready to support reforms on the rule of law, media freedom, the fight against corruption and strong democratic institutions, and to work together to advance Serbia's future in the European Union," von der Leyen said on X.

Mr. Magyar said he had talked with the Students' List leaders and assured them of his country's support.

"I was delighted to hear how much strength our victory in April had given them during this difficult struggle," Mr. Magyar said on X.

Mr. Vucic's defeat comes months after his close ally and Mr. Magyar's right-wing predecessor Viktor Orban also lost the elections.

"A two-year-long dream has come true," Mirjana Nikolic, a candidate on the Students' List, said shortly after the win was announced.

Most of the student list's 250 candidates are experts from fields including academia and the arts with little political experience.

The movement has a broad platform, centred on a pledge to fight corruption.

It evolved from demands for a transparent investigation into the Novi Sad tragedy into calls for an early election.

Despite pushing back against the movement for almost two years, Mr. Vucic, under mounting pressure, called the vote last month, citing "major turbulence on the domestic political scene".

The right-wing populist leader resigned the presidency to run for Prime Minister.

Domestic and international observers were monitoring the vote, with students mobilising large numbers of volunteers to oversee the process.

‘Bajo la superficie’: El ‘kraken’ ha vuelto, ha vuelto el caos

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Josh Hartnett protagoniza esta miniserie con monstruo marino tan trepidante como aparentemente superficial en la que se reflexiona sobre el fanatismo, la pequeña comunidad amenazada por un Goliat y un nuevo orden natural

Existe, en la mitología nórdica y la literatura de horror cósmico de H. P. Lovecraft, un monstruo marino conocido como kraken. ¿Y saben qué aspecto tiene? El de un calamar gigante, extremadamente inteligente, un habitante de una profundidad tan abismal que su existencia tiene más de mito que de realidad, aunque nunca se ha descartado que no esté ahí abajo. De hecho, el naturalista Carl von Linné —Carlos Linneo—, el creador de la clasificación de los seres vivos, lo incluyó como cefalópodo con el nombre científico de Microcosmus en la primera edición de su Systema naturæ (1735), pero lo eliminó en las posteriores. Bien, Bajo la superficie (Netflix), de Jesse McKeown (The Umbrella Academy, The Sinner), recupera tan fantástica criatura —en más de un sentido— y la sitúa en un presente de recursos menguantes y comunidades desplazadas de lo más interesante.

¿Cómo lo hace? Coloca a una familia con un padre, Calvin (un recuperado Josh Hartnett en un papel sorprendentemente atípico para alguien como él: de un lobo de mar deconstruido), traumatizado por un episodio del pasado que está a punto de repetirse —su familia fue reasentada y el padre murió en el traslado de la casa de una isla a otra, en muy extrañas circunstancias: sí, el kraken—, en un momento clave: el de la vuelta a casa de sus hijos y buena parte de la pequeña comunidad en la que viven, cada vez más desplobada. El lugar es curioso. Viven sobre el mar sobre el que se hundió el Titanic, rodeados de icebergs, sacando partido no tanto de la pesca como de la captura de pedazos de hielo gigantes que vender a distribuidores de hielo. Ocurre que el hielo que capturan empieza a apestar, y que desaparecen bancos de peces, y luego, turistas.

Y lo que empieza siendo una especie de psicosis de un solo personaje, el propio Hartnett, similar a la que se da en los primeros fotogramas de Tiburón, de Steven Spielberg —con, al menos, una línea de guion idéntica: “¡Salgan todos del agua!”—, va tejiendo capas a su alrededor hasta lograr edificar en tan solo seis capítulos un artefacto que igual sirve para atacar la idea del fanatismo —la familia que vive al margen y resiste no siempre es lo que parece, o se diría, es justo lo que parece—; la de la pequeña comunidad amenazada por un Goliat mentiroso —aquí representado por otra familia, los Crocker, con un interés oculto en el reasentamiento de los habitantes de la isla—, y la de la naturaleza como agente de un caos que intenta sembrar otro tipo de orden involutario pero imparable. El fin de un mundo y el nacimiento de otro genera todo tipo de monstruos.

Y uno es real, el enorme cefalópodo, el kraken de ojos aparentemente empáticos —¿parece o no reconocer a Calvin (Hartnett)?— y centra buena parte del metraje. Son formidables y trepidantes los capítulos de busca y captura de la cosa en cuestión, una vuelta al mar desde el descontrol total, desde el lado salvaje de los clásicos, Moby Dick en cabeza. El resto tienen que ver con la codicia del ser humano. Con la codicia, y a la vez, su desconsideración. La noche en la que todo el pueblo se reúne en el Linda’s, el único bar de la isla, un lugar acogedor con aspecto de cabaña, para celebrar que los que se fueron han vuelto por unos días —su peculiar fiesta mayor—, Linda decide utilizar el hielo extraído de un iceberg que se descartó por estar “contaminado”, ¿y adivinan qué? Se desata algo parecido a lo que se desata en La cosa, de John Carpenter: el virus de la locura.

Lo que mejor maneja McKeown, su creador, es precisamente el espejo con todo eso que le precede en la colocación de, por un lado, el monstruo, en el mundo, y por otro, la relación con los montones de hielo que están dejando de serlo. Esto es, con el pasado —eso que los icebergs contienen y de lo que nada sabemos, y que también contenía el hielo arcaico de la Antártida en La cosa— que la acción desconsiderada del ser humano está desenterrando. Un pasado que, Lovecraft estaría de acuerdo, no puede ser sino cósmico, estar relacionado con todo aquello que el planeta fue antes de nuestra llegada, y que podría volver a ser después, y que, elucubra McKeown, podría convertirnos —observen bien a Wade (Charlie Heaton, el Jonathan Byers de Stranger Things)— en instrumentos de algún tipo de reordenamiento salvaje del, en palabras de Linneo, Systema naturæ.

Even just two drinks a day can be far riskier for health than previously thought, study warns

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Any possible benefits of drinking small quantities of alcohol do not translate into overall health benefit, study says

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Even two drinks a day, a quantity considered moderate by many, can lead to premature death, disability, cancer, and heart disease, a major new study warns.

Researchers found that among people who consumed an average of 14 alcoholic drinks per week, 1 in 25 will die from alcohol-related causes.

In comparison, consuming up to seven drinks weekly was linked to only small increases in risk for most health conditions, they said.

"Even low levels of alcohol use come with health risks... and that risk continues to increase the more someone drinks,” said Kevin Shield, senior author of the study published in the Journal of Studies on Alcohol and Drugs.

In the study, researchers assessed how alcohol consumption over a lifetime contributes to disease and premature death among Americans.

They examined more than 7,200 scientific publications covering illnesses and injuries associated with alcohol.

Researchers used the data from these studies to estimate the risks for individual health conditions and combined those findings with large national health data sets.

Scientists examine alcohol's connection to a wide range of immediate and long-term health problems, including oesophageal, oral, and breast cancer, as well as cardiovascular disease, liver disease, and injuries.

They calculated how different levels of alcohol consumption affected the likelihood of developing serious health problems or dying prematurely.

Current US Dietary Guidelines simply encourage Americans to "limit alcoholic beverages" without identifying a particular consumption threshold, while previous versions advised men to drink no more than two alcoholic beverages per day and women no more than one.

"While the new US Dietary Guidelines contain a useful 'less-is-best' message, they provide no quantitative framework. Our study was designed to do just that across the drinking spectrum," said study co-author Timothy Naimi from the University of Victoria's Canadian Institute for Substance Use Research.

"It turns out that two drinks per day, which might be considered 'moderate' from a social standpoint, is associated with a substantially elevated risk of a premature death caused by alcohol," Dr Naimi said.

The latest study’s core finding challenges the longstanding belief that consuming small amounts of alcohol can improve health, particularly heart health.

Some earlier studies hint that drinking some forms of alcohol, like wine or sake, may benefit certain aspects of heart health.

But researchers now caution that those possible advantages do not translate into an overall health benefit once alcohol's links to cancer and other diseases are taken into account.

"We did not observe a significant protective effect of alcohol on health at any level of consumption," Dr Shield said.

“When you look across the full range of health outcomes, including cancer and other chronic diseases, those potential benefits are outweighed by the risks even at 7 drinks per week,” he said.

Researchers, however, noted that a person’s individual risk depends on other factors like “lifestyle, genetics, drinking patterns, and other choices that differ person to person."

"Understanding those relationships, and how much alcohol contributes to those risks, is an area that still needs further work," Dr Shield said.
