Economic woes transform Midwest into massive midterms battleground...

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Democrats could be poised for a revival in the heartland, as furor over high costs and doubt about President Donald Trump's willingness to change the nation's trajectory threaten to loosen the GOP's grip on working-class voters.

A decade after the Trump era began, when he first turned Midwestern political battlegrounds into reliably Republican states, the fallout from policies of his second term have rippled through the agriculture sector and broader economies across the Great Plains and the Rust Belt.

Iowa and Ohio, which have not been competitive since former President Barack Obama was on the ballot, are in play. And ruby-red states like Kansas, Nebraska and Indiana are not exempt. The shift is happening up and down the ballot, with tight contests for the House, Senate and governor blanketing the middle of the country.

From spring planting to fall harvest, anxiety over Trump's war with Iran and the fallout from his trade battles has turned into alarm.

In conversations across the region, voters point to the soaring cost of gas and diesel, as well as high grocery prices and interest rates. A whipsaw tariff policy has forced farmers to pay more for fertilizer, while some manufacturers, footing higher bills for imported components, have laid off workers. Trump's decision to allow imported beef has infuriated ranchers. And spending cuts targeting healthcare and grant programs have caused further strain.

Promises to revitalize the nation's manufacturing sector remain unfulfilled. And even before the Iran war sent costs soaring, several of these battleground states were in fiscal decline.

Iowa, for example, recorded the weakest annual growth rate in the country in 2025, according to the federal Bureau of Economic Analysis, a searing indictment for Republicans who have had control of state government for more than a decade.

"I think everybody in agriculture is tired, would be the best way to put it. They're just tired," Steve Kuiper, a farmer and president of the Iowa Corn Growers Association, told CNN. "They feel very beat up right now, demoralized."

It has all fueled a hunger for change — and created an opening for Democratic candidates.

"A lot of the promises the president made to bring costs down, to not start new foreign wars, to deliver on his economic message, have not come to fruition," said Beau Bayh, the third-generation scion of Indiana's most famous Democratic political family. If he wins his race for secretary of state, he'd be the first member of his party to win statewide in Indiana in 14 years.

Trump knows better than most presidents that midterm elections are a reflection — and referendum — on the party in power. He experienced it in 2018, when Republicans held control of the Senate but lost their majority in the House — including three seats in Iowa. GOP officials tell CNN they fear a repeat of that scenario.

The US Capitol building seen on October 1 in Washington, DC. - Anna Moneymaker/Getty Images

The travel plans of both parties tell the tale, with Trump set to visit Michigan on Sunday after making stops in Ohio, Kansas, Nebraska and Iowa in a monthlong campaign blitz. Obama traveled to Michigan and Ohio on Saturday, rallying support in two states he once won.

Polls suggest voters are willing to punish the Republicans in power in Washington, even though they also hold dim views of the Democratic brand.

"It's not that they've been overwhelmed by the Democratic Party, it's just that they're really, really pissed off," Tom Vilsack, a Democratic former Iowa governor, told CNN. "They want to send a message of disappointment. When you're upset with someone, you want to find a way to send a message. You don't swallow it, you send that message."

Vilsack, who served two terms as agriculture secretary in the Obama administration, said economic frustrations across the agriculture and manufacturing sectors have been rising long before the Trump era. But he said the promises Trump made during his three campaigns, particularly when he pledged to bring prices down only two years ago, are feeding the anger.

"He builds up the hope that it's going to be fundamentally different," Vilsack said. "That was interpreted that he's going to bring back the good times. He failed to do that."

Former Minnesota Gov. Tim Pawlenty, who was the last Republican to win that office in his reelection two decades ago, said Trump would be better off talking about rising costs and how he intends to change course.

"It's going to be a blue wave," Pawlenty told CNN. "We just don't know if it's knee-deep, waist-deep, or going to hit us right in the face."

The House is all but certain to fall from GOP control, he said, but added: "The real chance for Republicans to salvage something out of this is to hang on to the Senate and that's a real possibility."

In Iowa and Ohio, Democrats have a shot at winning Senate races that are crucial to the net four-seat gain they need to claim a majority — a goal that eluded them in 2018. Their odds look even better in those states' governor's races, potentially giving the party a foothold in statehouses where Republicans have trifectas: control of the executive, legislative and judicial branches.

But the Democratic revival could extend even further. Megachurch pastor Adam Hamilton has the GOP scrambling to save the seat of Sen. Roger Marshall in Kansas. An independent candidate, Dan Osborn, has rallied Democratic support and thrown a curveball into Republican Sen. Pete Ricketts' bid for a second term in Nebraska.

And a slew of House seats across the Midwest held by Republicans are in play. Democrats believe they could win as many as three GOP-held seats each in Iowa and Ohio, plus potentially four more in Michigan. The party is also targeting an Omaha-based district in Nebraska and one in southeastern Wisconsin's Driftless Area. If the election breaks in Democrats' favor in a big way, another House seat in Nebraska, as well as one seat each in Indiana and Kansas, could also prove to be much more competitive than either party expected early in the 2026 election cycle.

Republicans aren't conceding any of those races. And they believe they have Democrats on defense in the Upper Midwest — particularly Michigan, where progressive former public health official Abdul El-Sayed is locked in an expensive and bitter Senate race against Republican former Rep. Mike Rogers.

Speaking in Sioux City, Iowa, on Thursday, Trump urged voters to pretend he was on the ballot and vote to ensure a "booming Iowa economy."

"If you get out and vote like you did in 2024, we win in a landslide, you know, we win in a landslide," he said

Even as the president boasts of America's golden age, with an economy envied by the world, he also makes clear he understands the challenges people are facing. At most every stop along the way of his red-state October blitz, he has offered sweeteners to voters if they stick with Republican candidates.

From $8 billion in new aid to farmers announced in Kansas to promises of building the nation's largest steel plant in eastern Iowa to a commitment he made in Nebraska to temporarily ease diesel costs, Trump has made a series of pledges that follow his offer of giving every adult American $5,000 if Republicans retain their grip on Washington. The promises are barely even repeated by GOP candidates, who know they ring hollow with many voters.

Democrats acknowledge the uphill climb for winning all of the narrowly divided Senate races across the Midwest, particularly given their own brand and favorability challenges. But Republican officials wonder whether Trump's extensive travels to many of these states will backfire on down-ballot races.

"When our leader, the president himself, does things that make me cringe, that's difficult to tell people to vote for me," said Jim Howell, a Republican member of the Sedgwick County Board of Commissioners in Wichita, who is also on the November ballot. "I really wish we had leaders that don't make us cringe, but help us consider a way to come together."

A question hanging over the final week of the race is whether Trump's relentless campaigning has awakened Republican voters to rally to his aid one last time in hopes of closing an enthusiasm gap. Iowa could be a place to watch, given Trump's long history of winning there and his decision to hold two rallies in opposite corners of the state in the homestretch.

But the president's approach, a little more than one week from Election Day, has been to double down on the policies that have led many voters to sour on his second term.

In Trump's Sunday trip to Michigan, he'll hold a rally at Port Huron — on the Canadian border. It will put on display his tariff battle with the United States' northern neighbor, even though the party's Senate nominee, former Rep. Mike Rogers, has attempted to break with Trump over the tariffs.

"Here in Michigan, we know that Canada is not our enemy. We need to end this tariff war — now," Rogers said in a television ad his campaign launched this month.

The GOP's best hope might be to lean into the cultural battles that have helped them win over White, rural, working-class voters in recent elections. In Michigan, both Trump and Rogers have attempted to brand the Democratic nominee, El-Sayed, as supportive of extremist Islamist interests.

But Democrats are fighting back. El-Sayed in recent days began airing an ad that features clips of Republicans and conservative media figures, including Fox News hosts, saying nice or helpful things about him and his campaign.

Before those clips play, he lists issues where he suggests common cause with some in the MAGA movement, such as releasing the Jeffrey Epstein files and combatting Washington corruption.

Ohio is another headache for Republicans. The party's nominee for governor, Vivek Ramaswamy, faced sharp criticism from the man he is running to replace, two-term GOP Gov. Mike DeWine, for airing a controversial advertisement featuring the mother of his Democratic opponent, Amy Acton.

Gubernatorial candidate Vivek Ramaswamy, alongside US President Donald Trump, speaks in Youngstown, Ohio, on October 12. - Kevin Lamarque/Reuters

Both Ramaswamy and the president have dug in, defending the ad — and in doing so have turned the race into a test of whether voters will accept Trumpian tactics from candidates who are not Trump.

And what's receded from the forefront is the issue Ohio voters who spoke with CNN in recent days — including Republicans who support Ramaswamy — said is responsible for the state's sudden competitiveness: the cost of living.

"We all have to get gas. I have to go get gas right now in my car, and that's difficult," said Sheri Vorherr, a 55-year-old mother of five from Loveland who volunteered at a Ramaswamy rally in Cincinnati on Wednesday. She said she helps her oldest daughter buy groceries each week and sees the economic pinch brought on by inflation.

"I don't think it's an easy fix. I think it's things that are going to take time," she said. "I do believe Trump is trying to do that — it's just taking a lot longer."

CNN's Phil Mattingly contributed to this report.

VIDEO «NZZ STANDPUNKTE» - «Antisemitismus wird zum Instrument, um die westliche Lebensart zu bekämpfen»

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Seit dem 7. Oktober 2023 herrsche bei der Frage des Umgangs mit militanten Palästinensern Konsens in Israel, sagt die Strategieexpertin Melody Sucharewicz. Dem Rest des Westens drohe eine tiefgreifende Spaltung.

Der 7. Oktober 2023 hat die israelische Gesellschaft traumatisiert. Nach dem Pogrom auf Boden des einzigen jüdischen Staats der Welt folgte ein zweiter Schock: die Geschwindigkeit, mit der westliche Gesellschaften in eine neue Welle des Judenhasses hineingezogen worden seien, sagt die deutsch-israelische Politikberaterin und Terrorismus-Expertin Melody Sucharewicz in NZZ Standpunkte.

NZZ.ch benötigt JavaScript für wichtige Funktionen. Ihr Browser oder Adblocker verhindert dies momentan.

Extremisten sei erschreckend rasch eine breite Mobilisierung gelungen, bis tief in akademische und bürgerliche Kreise hinein. Judenhass werde dabei gezielt eingesetzt. «Antisemitismus ist vom Virus zum Instrument mutiert», so Sucharewicz weiter. «Zum Instrument für die Kräfte, die die Lebensart, für die wir in westlichen Gesellschaften gekämpft haben, zerstören möchten.»

Diverse zivilgesellschaftliche Anliegen seien systematisch gekapert worden: Demonstrationen für Frauenrechte, Klimaschutz, LGBTQ-Anliegen oder Sozialreformen in Europa und den USA – nahezu jede Protestbewegung werde von linksradikalen und islamistischen Gruppierungen mit Palästina-Flaggen vereinnahmt. Der offene Flirt von Teilen der Politik und Kultur mit Islamisten und Clanchefs habe den Antisemitismus fast zu einem Teil der Popkultur verkommen lassen.

Diese Entwicklung, die zur Polarisierung westlicher Gesellschaften geführt habe, sei bedauerlich und ein Grund zur Sorge für alle Menschen, «denen ihre Freiheit lieb ist». Der entgrenzte Hass auf den Strassen von Berlin, Paris oder New York offenbare Respektlosigkeit gegenüber den Werten und Rechtsordnungen jener Staaten, die den Protestierenden Schutz und Heimat böten.

Sucharewicz hofft, dass die vernünftige Mitte der Bevölkerung dies erkennt und sich nicht von Extremisten in die Irre führen lassen wird. Israel selbst könne sich nicht primär damit beschäftigen, wie das Geschehen in Nahost in westlichen Wohnzimmern wahrgenommen werde. Das Land befinde sich in einem existenziellen Verteidigungskampf.

Wichtig sei dabei die enge strategische und militärische Zusammenarbeit mit den USA. Mit Blick auf die Kritik an der israelischen Kriegsführung fordert Sucharewicz eine klare Trennung zwischen Empathie und militärischer Notwendigkeit. Jedes einzelne zivile Opfer sei eine Tragödie und wecke Mitleid, auch bei ihr.

Doch habe die Hamas während Jahrzehnten inmitten der zivilen Bevölkerung eine Infrastruktur für ihren Terror aufgebaut – finanziert mit internationalen Hilfsgeldern und mit der Hilfe Irans. Sie verweist ausserdem darauf, dass das Verhältnis von getöteten Zivilisten zu getöteten Terroristen deutlich geringer sei als bei vergleichbaren Operationen westlicher Alliierter, etwa beim Kampf gegen den Islamischen Staat in Mosul.

Für Israel gebe es keine vernünftige Alternative zum entschlossenen Vorgehen. Nach Ereignissen wie jenen am 7. Oktober könne kein souveräner Staat hinnehmen, seine Bürger schutzlos zu lassen. «Es muss getan werden, was getan werden muss, um dafür zu sorgen, dass die Israeli nie wieder etwas wie den 7. Oktober erleben müssen», sagt Sucharewicz.

Die heutige israelische Haltung sei das Ergebnis bitterer Erfahrungen. Schon der vollständige Abzug aus dem Gaza-Streifen im Jahr 2005 und andere israelische Vorleistungen seien von den Palästinensern nicht mit Frieden, sondern mit der Errichtung einer Terror-Hochburg beantwortet worden.

Noch kurz vor dem 7. Oktober habe die israelische Regierung die Zahl der Arbeitsgenehmigungen für Palästinenser erhöht. Einige dieser Arbeiter hätten die Hamas später mit detaillierten Informationen über die Kibbuzim versorgt, deren Bewohner bei dem Pogrom dann abgeschlachtet wurden.

Diese Kette von Vertrauensbrüchen habe Israel ins Mark getroffen. Menschen in den Grenzregionen, die sich jahrelang für palästinensische Nachbarn eingesetzt und sie medizinisch versorgt hätten, mussten mit ansehen, wie am 7. Oktober auch palästinensische Zivilisten mordend und plündernd über sie und ihre Häuser herfielen.

In der Frage des Umgangs mit den Palästinensern gebe es einen breiten Konsens im Land. Auch Linke und die Friedensbewegung hätten erkannt, mit welcher zerstörerischen Kraft Israel konfrontiert sei. «Das ist vielleicht eine der tragischsten und traurigsten Konsequenzen des 7. Oktober», sagt Sucharewicz. «Versöhnung (mit diesen Kräften) ist leider utopisch.»

Solange jedenfalls, bis die Kultur des Hasses nicht grundlegend überwunden ist. Der Todeskult der Hamas, ihre Verherrlichung des Märtyrertums und die Erziehung palästinensischer Kinder zum Hass hätten tiefe Spuren hinterlassen. Dass selbst nach dem 7. Oktober eine breite Mehrheit der Bevölkerung im Gaza-Streifen das Massaker gutgeheissen habe, mache eine schnelle Wiederannäherung unmöglich.

Dennoch gibt Sucharewicz nicht alle Hoffnung auf. Die weitere Entwicklung hänge von der vollständigen Entwaffnung der Hamas ab. Wenn gesichert sei, dass zivile Infrastruktur nicht erneut als Machtzentrum einer Terrororganisation diene, sei ein nachhaltiger Wiederaufbau möglich.

Seit dem Terrorangriff der Hamas auf Israel am 7. Oktober 2023 weitet sich die Gewalt im Nahen Osten täglich aus. Längst ist nicht mehr nur der Gazastreifen betroffen.

Israel steht kurz vor der Parlamentswahl. Wer gewinnt? Seit dem Massaker vom 7. Oktober ist Israel weiter nach rechts gerückt, aber Benjamin Netanyahus Partei profitiert davon kaum. Die Stimmen holen sich andere.

Der frühere Generalstabschef liegt in den Umfragen zur israelischen Parlamentswahl vor dem Amtsinhaber Benjamin Netanyahu. Doch jede denkbare Koalition wäre mit einem hohen Risiko verbunden.

Attackers kill nurse, injure health worker responding to DRC Ebola case

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The attack in eastern DR Congo is the latest on health workers and responders to the deadly Ebola outbreak.

A nurse has been killed and another health worker severely injured after they were attacked while responding to an Ebola alert in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), according to the World Health Organization.

It was the latest in a series of attacks on health workers and responders that authorities say is hampering efforts to slow the fastest-growing Ebola outbreak in the world.

The attack took place on Saturday in the town of Bafwasende in the DRC’s eastern Tshopo province, WHO Director-General Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus said in a post on X on Sunday.

“Everyone working to protect communities from this deadly disease must be able to work in dignity and safety. Healthcare must always be protected,” he said.

The WHO’s regional director for Africa, Dr Mohamed Yakub Janabi, also condemned the attack on X.

“Health is NOT a target,” he said. “Those responding to the Ebola outbreak must be protected. Misinformation and fear must never fuel violence.”

Local authorities have not yet commented on the attack or the suspects.

Data from the DRC’s Ministry of Health indicate that more than 9,800 cases, including more than 4,700 deaths, have been recorded since the start of the outbreak in May, making it the deadliest Ebola outbreak in the country’s history.

The government’s handling of the outbreak, combined with chronic corruption, has sown widespread distrust, with some even dismissing Ebola as a hoax.

Responders say scepticism about the disease among communities has complicated their efforts, including in remote areas and emerging hotspots already facing shortages of medical supplies and healthcare workers.

Last month, a DRC politician was beaten to death in North Kivu after publicly defending the government’s response to Ebola. He had called on the public to obey response measures, particularly social distancing and hand-washing, on a radio show.

In May, at the start of the outbreak, angry residents set alight two tents at health centres in two separate attacks in just one week.

Responders say some residents have held captive or threatened team members in designated health zones.

The current outbreak has now been reported in four countries across eastern, central ⁠⁠and western Africa, giving it the widest geographic spread of any Ebola outbreak on the continent to date.

On Sunday, Uganda’s Minister of Health Chris Baryomunsi announced that all citizens returning to the country with a history of travel to the DRC will be quarantined for 21 days.

Uganda says it is ⁠Ebola-free after declaring an end to its latest outbreak in July. But health experts remain on alert after two people who had recently passed through the country later tested positive in Kenya and Nigeria.

Ebola spreads through direct contact with the bodily fluids of infected people.

The strain responsible for the current outbreak, known as Bundibugyo, has no approved vaccine or specific treatment.

Symptoms can take up to 21 days to appear, according to the WHO. In an October 23 update, it urged stronger monitoring and cooperation across borders, while advising against restrictions on travel or trade with affected countries.

Trump dials up Islamophobia against Abdul El-Sayed at Michigan Senate rally

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Al Jazeera looks at the key takeaways from the US president’s campaign event in Michigan ahead of the midterm vote.

Port Huron, Michigan – United States President Donald Trump has sharpened his anti-Muslim attacks against Democratic Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed, going so far as to suggest that Christmas would be cancelled if the progressive doctor won.

Trump visited Michigan on Sunday to shore up support for Republican candidates in the Midwestern state ahead of the midterm elections on November 3.

But from his podium, he levelled unfounded allegations against El-Sayed, including calling him a “jihadist”.

“He would be the only senator that will have to go through a metal detector before he’s allowed to go into the Senate,” Trump said of El-Sayed.

The president, whose popularity has been slipping in part due to the US-Israel war on Iran, spoke for nearly two hours to hundreds of supporters.

His remarks largely focused on Michigan’s Senate election, which is one of only about seven toss-up races for the upper chamber of Congress.

El-Sayed faces Republican candidate Mike Rogers for the state’s open Senate seat, and the race is considered crucial for Republicans to retain control of the 100-member Senate.

If Rogers wins, he would flip a seat currently held by retiring Democratic Senator Gary Peters, leaving Democrats with the difficult task of flipping five other seats to regain control of the chamber.

Currently, Republicans have a 53-seat majority in the Senate. Only 35 Senate positions are up for election in 2026.

Here are five key takeaways from the Republican event.

While Trump has repeatedly targeted El-Sayed’s Muslim identity in recent weeks, including by misrepresenting the Democrat’s name as “Mohammed”, the US president took his rhetoric to new heights on Sunday.

Throughout the speech, Trump returned to themes related to El-Sayed’s Muslim faith, falsely accusing the candidate of wanting to impose Islamic law in the US.

“So Abdul… also wants our country to go to Sharia law, and if he ever pulls that off, he should be the first one that’s tried. I’ll be a witness,” Trump said, apparently imagining El-Sayed being tried in a religious court.

Several times during the rally, some attendees blurted out “traitor” when El-Sayed’s name was mentioned.

“I’m here to keep a jihadist Muslim Brotherhood member from becoming a United States senator,” Trump said.

El-Sayed is a US-born citizen and former public health official who has pledged to uphold the Constitution. There is no evidence that he was ever a member of the Muslim Brotherhood or any foreign organisation.

Since Trump first ran for president during the 2016 election cycle, he has weaponised anti-Muslim bigotry for political gain.

In 2015, for instance – near the beginning of his first successful campaign – Trump said he would support “a total and complete shutdown of Muslims entering the United States”.

Less than two years later, Trump used the presidency to sign a short-lived executive order barring travel from multiple Muslim-majority countries, what became known as the “Muslim ban”.

In Sunday’s speech, Trump appealed once more to fears of Muslim immigration.

“If Abdul and the Democrats win, they will open the floodgates and invite hordes of jihadists to our shores, creating a nightmare of violence and terror until you cannot even have a Christmas,” Trump said.

El-Sayed has previously largely dismissed the anti-Muslim rhetoric against him as an attempt to create “distractions”, saying he is focused more on domestic issues, including lowering the cost of living and expanding access to healthcare.

With Trump’s approval ratings plummeting, some Republican candidates in tight races have kept their distance from the president.

Rogers has previously called for an end to the Iran war and expressed reservations about Trump’s tariff campaign, urging an end to the trade war with Canada, which borders Michigan.

But Rogers attended Sunday’s rally and took the stage alongside Trump in a display of mutual support.

A survey by the research firm Marketing Resource Group suggested earlier this month that only 35 percent of voters in Michigan view Trump positively.

Rogers set the tone for the anti-Muslim attacks against El-Sayed earlier in the rally, also claiming the Democrat is connected to the Muslim Brotherhood, a political movement in Egypt designated as a “terrorist” group by the US this year.

As a congressman, however, Rogers voted in 2012 to continue US aid to Egypt under Muslim Brotherhood President Mohamed Morsi.

In Sunday’s speech, Rogers also tried to paint El-Sayed as part of the Democratic Socialists of America (DSA), but unlike other progressives, El-Sayed is not a member of the left-wing group.

“This DSA-affiliated candidate is also taking a lot of money from organizations that support the Muslim Brotherhood, Hamas and Hezbollah,” Rogers said, without offering evidence.

All three groups are designated as “terrorist” organisations in the US, making it illegal to provide any material support to them.

On Sunday, the average price of a gallon (3.8 litres) of petrol in Michigan was $4.83, up from $2.91 before the start of the war on Iran.

Tehran has restricted passage through the Strait of Hormuz in response to the war, sending energy prices soaring.

Trump initially promised the conflict would only last four to six weeks.

But nearly eight months after the US and Israel launched the war on February 28, the conflict is showing no signs of concluding.

On Sunday, Trump said he has no regrets about the war.

“I made that decision, and I have no second guess,” Trump told the crowd.

He added that he thought petrol prices would have increased by much more than they had. He also defended his decision by referencing the threat of nuclear warfare.

“The number one state sponsor of terror is Iran by far, and we had to make sure they didn’t have a nuclear weapon,” Trump said.

Iran has denied seeking a nuclear weapon. It has long argued its uranium enrichment programme is for civilian energy production.

Before the war, Trump also argued that US strikes against Iran’s uranium enrichment facilities in June 2025 had completely “obliterated” the country’s nuclear programme.

Despite the economic anxiety related to the cost of living and skyrocketing petrol prices, Trump bragged about the amount of profit US oil companies are making from the war.

Demand for energy products has increased globally in recent months, as supply chains tighten.

“Our country is making hundreds of billions of dollars because of what’s happened in the Middle East, because they’re coming to Texas, and they’re coming to Oklahoma, and they’re coming to Louisiana, and they’re taking that oil,” Trump said. “They’re making hundreds of billions of dollars.”

Trump added that his advisers asked him not to bring up this issue because “people are paying a little higher” for petrol and gas.

“I could just see the fake headline: ‘President Trump is talking about all the money they’re making while people are suffering at the pump.’ That’ll be the headline. I said, ‘I don’t give a damn. I’m going to say it. We’re making a freaking fortune,'” the US president said.

A line of Trump supporters stretched several blocks outside the rally on Sunday.

Attendees, dressed in Make America Great Again (MAGA) gear, filled the hockey arena where Trump spoke.

The crowd roared and laughed with Trump, with attendees frequently blurting out expressions of admiration for the president and insults against his opponents, especially El-Sayed.

Trump may appear politically on the back foot amid dwindling popularity and mounting anger over inflation and the Iran war.

But the president has highlighted his continued ability to mobilise his core MAGA base, often pointing to his crowd size as a metric for his success as a campaigner.

The audience remained engaged and seated throughout the speech on Sunday.

At one point, the address took a bizarre turn as the president spoke about the backside of Jon Husted, a Republican incumbent from Ohio competing in another tight Senate race.

“I looked at his a**, and I’ve never done that with a man before. Women? Yes. Men? No,” Trump said.

The US president also complimented the derriere of the rapper Nicki Minaj, one of his prominent supporters in attendance.

“By the way, I looked at one hell of a beautiful a** before: Nicki Minaj. That’s hard to miss,” Trump said.

Architect is struck off after calling her neighbours racist during row 'because they cheered for England and occasionally flew a Union Jack flag'

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Published: 01:53 GMT, 26 October 2026 | Updated: 01:57 GMT, 26 October 2026

An architect was struck off after making a string of false allegations against her neighbours - including accusing one of being racist because they cheered for England and occasionally flew a Union Jack flag.

The Architects Registration Board struck Karin Elliott off for a minimum of two years after finding her guilty of unacceptable professional conduct by sending inappropriate communications.

The tribunal found she used 'inflammatory language, making a series of misleading and potentially damaging accusations' in 2024 against her neighbours 'with scant regard to their accuracy'.

The board said the Irish-American architect's actions were 'misleading, lacking integrity and dishonest'.

Ms Elliott accused one neighbour of 'racial harassment' after falsely claiming that he wielded a chainsaw in her garden in Norwich, the tribunal heard.

The architect's partner, Francois Girardin, told the tribunal that none of the neighbours said 'anything directly racist'.

However, Mr Girardin, who is French, made an 'inference' of racism from how 'sometimes a Union Jack would appear' and 'a wall had been constructed after the US President had talked about building a big wall to keep out immigrants', the tribunal heard.

Mr Girardin also said whenever England played sport, a neighbour would 'leave their doors open, and – especially when England played France – their family would cheer for England'.

The Architects Registration Board struck off Karin Elliott (pictured) for a minimum of two years after finding her guilty of unacceptable professional conduct by sending inappropriate communication

Ms Elliott also accused a neighbour of gender violence for driving his 'phallic symbol' of a car across her driveway.

During cross-examination, Ms Elliott said from 'a psychoanalytic perspective' her neighbour repeatedly driving across her driveway with the 'phallic symbol' of his car was a manifestation of gender violence.

The tribunal found this 'underscored how tenuously she constructed serious accusations against other people, and how casually she dispensed them'.

The architect had made a planning application for the installation of CCTV cameras on her property in December 2023 following disputes with her neighbours.

She wrote a letter to local residents in February 2024 asking for support for her application and made serious allegations about her neighbours that the tribunal considered to be 'dishonest' and 'misleading'.

In the letter to residents, she claimed one neighbour had 'smashed down several of our historic walls, chopped down many of our historic trees, and built on our property, all without our consent'.

Discussing another neighbour, Ms Elliott wrote: 'He calls us either a "piece of s***", "disgusting" and/or exclaims "the stench" regularly.'

In another allegation, the architect wrote: 'He threatened to hit one of us over the head with a hammer at one stage, after he was asked to desist.

'He had been starting to build a wall on our property illegally on a Sunday, following Trump's declaration that he would "Build a big wall" to keep out immigrants.

'It is very upsetting. We are Irish/American and French.'

Discussing claims about a third neighbour, Ms Elliott accused her of building an 'ugly concrete foundation' and using influence at the local council 'to escape justice'.

She also accused her neighbour of singing Tragedy by the Bee Gees 'from the enormous balcony they built overlooking our home' after the death of her father.

Discussing the three neighbours, Ms Elliott wrote: 'We hope that the CCTV might deter them all from further trespassing and further destroying our listed garden, and from any further gender violence and racial harassment.'

In cross-examination, the architect said: 'I think I'd give this letter a serious editing if I did it again.'

Ms Elliott made further allegations in a planning appeal form to Norwich City Planning Portal in March 2024.

Discussing one neighbour in the planning form, the architect wrote: 'I begged him to turn off his chainsaw and explained that I considered it to be racial harassment.'

One neighbour told the tribunal that the architect's 'bizarre accusations of threatening behaviour and the racist accusations have been a source of acute embarrassment and personal discomfort'.

Another said the allegations were 'very depressing and upsetting' and they do not spend time in their garden because Ms Elliott and her partner are 'always looking at us', adding that the architect is an 'absolute disgrace to her profession'.

The tribunal found Ms Elliott's conduct occurred after she had previously been found guilty of unacceptable professional conduct in 2021 for calling neighbours 'racists and Nazis' as well as 'making offensive references to their physical characteristics and mental health'.

College homecoming party turns to chaos as deck collapses, leaving at least 28 people injured

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Images show a collapsed deck at a Williamsburg, Virginia, home near William & Mary’s campus after at least 28 people were injured during an off-campus homecoming party. Credit: City of Williamsburg

A college homecoming party took a frightening turn Friday night when a back deck collapsed at a Virginia home, leaving at least 28 people injured and sending emergency crews rushing to the neighborhood.

William & Mary confirmed students were among those injured at the off-campus residence in Williamsburg. All reported injuries were non-life-threatening, according to city officials.

Dispatchers received a report of a structure collapse around 10:07 p.m. on the 200 block of Matoaka Court. Responding police and firefighters found the home’s back deck had collapsed, the city said.

TERRIFYING SCENE UNFOLDS AS METAL CABANA FRAME CRASHES INTO PACKED CASINO POOL AREA

A collapsed wooden deck sits behind police tape at a Williamsburg, Virginia, home after a homecoming party incident injured at least 28 people Oct. 23, 2026. (City of Williamsburg)

Emergency crews took 13 people to hospitals, while another 15 reportedly sought treatment on their own, according to the city’s Saturday morning update.

Neighbors told WAVY that a homecoming party was underway when the deck gave way. One described emergency vehicles stretching through the neighborhood.

"All the way down the street and wrapped around just ambulances and fire trucks," the neighbor, who asked not to be named, told WAVY. "We saw a lot of hospital gurneys coming out of ambulances. At least nine ambulances kind of wrapped around the street."

WATCH: VIRGINIA COPS CRAWL THROUGH BURNING HOUSE TO SAVE MAN TRAPPED IN FIRE

A view of William & Mary’s campus in Williamsburg, Virginia. The university confirmed students were among those injured when a deck collapsed at an off-campus home Oct. 23, 2026. (Jeffrey Greenberg/Universal Images Group via Getty Images)

Joshua Wise, who lives next to the home, told the outlet some of his classmates were involved. He returned after emergency crews had finished responding but learned his housemate had been helping people get home.

"Word was traveling pretty fast," Wise told WAVY. "By the time I got back here, the emergency response was over, but I had a housemate who was helping people, trying to help out as much as possible, drive people home, get people where they needed to be."

Wise credited neighbors with coming together after the frightening incident.

"We did everything we could to help each other out and I think that that’s beautiful," he said.

5 VICTIMS RELEASED FROM HOSPITAL AFTER FLORIDA A&M UNIVERSITY SHOOTING, 1 REMAINS CRITICAL

William & Mary offered support to students injured in an off-campus deck collapse during homecoming weekend. (Alex Perry/Virginia Gazette/The Virginian-Pilot/Tribune News Service via Getty Images)

In a Saturday message to the campus community, William & Mary’s senior vice president for student affairs and public safety, Ginger Ambler, said the university was still gathering information. Additional students may have sought care beyond those initially counted, she noted.

"We are grateful that none of the injuries were life-threatening," Ambler wrote.

College of William & Mary on Monday, October 25, 2021, in Williamsburg, Virginia. (Getty Images)

She said staff across the university’s health, academic and student support offices were working to assist affected students.

"We recognize that such an experience may affect our students deeply and in different ways," Ambler wrote.

Wise also praised the emergency response.

"Thank goodness for the emergency responders," he told WAVY. "They were great. The ambulances and everything got here pretty fast."

Local police and firefighters responded, and city officials were working with area hospitals to confirm the total number of people treated.

The city’s Codes Compliance Division is evaluating and inspecting the site.

Published - October 26, 2026 01:40 am IST - Gaza City, Palestinian Territories

Mourners during the funeral of three Palestinians who were killed in an Israeli strike, according to medics, at Al-Shifa Hospital in Gaza City. | Photo Credit: Reuters

An Israeli strike on a university in Gaza City killed three people on Sunday (October 25, 2026), staff and a local hospital said, while the civil defence agency reported five others killed elsewhere in the territory.

Reached for comment on the strike at the Islamic University, the Israeli army said that it was currently under review.

Al Shifa hospital reported receiving three bodies and treating eight people who were wounded in the drone strike, with two sources at the university saying the dead were employees and the injured were students.

The university said the campus and its surroundings had been targeted twice on Sunday (October 25, 2026), condemning the attacks and mourning those killed in a statement.

Gaza's Education Ministry likewise condemned the strike and said, "The global silence regarding attacks on students and universities serves as encouragement for the continuation of these crimes".

"This strike occurs at a time when the higher education sector is facing total annihilation, with 95% of the buildings belonging to academic institutions having been damaged as a result of the war," it continued.

"This heinous assault is part of a continuous series of attacks on education; over the past six days, 10 students and educators have been killed by the occupation's fire."

In a statement, the army said it had been "targeting a terrorist from Hamas' military wing".

"Following the identification of uninvolved individuals in the area, the munition was diverted in order to mitigate harm to them. The incident is under review," it said, without specifying whether those killed at the university were hit by accident.

A truce has been in effect in Gaza for over a year, but it has failed to completely halt the violence there.

Gaza civil defence spokesman Mahmoud Bassal said five other people were killed on Sunday (October 25, 2026), three in strikes around the southern city of Khan Yunis, one in central Deir al Balah and one northwest of Gaza City.

One more person was wounded by Israeli gunfire in Jabalia refugee camp in the north.

Lula wins fourth term as Brazil president after historic upset over far-right Flávio Bolsonaro

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Leftist incumbent Lula says unprecedented comeback was a ‘defeat for denialism’ as Bolsonaro refuses to accept election result

Lula wins election in stunning comeback – live updates

Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, the Brazilian president, has staged a dramatic election fightback, defeating his far-right opponent, Flávio Bolsonaro, to claim a historic fourth term in power.

With 100% of votes counted the 80-year-old leftist received 4.4m votes more than Bolsonaro, with a 51.79% share to his 45-year-old rival’s 48.21%. However, soon after Brazil’s electoral authority confirmed Lula’s victory, Bolsonaro refused to concede defeat and asked for the results to be annulled.

Lula narrowly lost the first round of the election to Bolsonaro in early October, casting his political future into doubt and plunging progressive Brazilians into three weeks of despair. Never, since the return of democracy in the 1980s, has the first round runner-up won the second vote.

But Lula did precisely that on Sunday, prompting joyous scenes on the streets of São Paulo, where he had been following events, and at the hotel where he was watching the count.

Addressing a packed crowd of ecstatic supporters in a São Paulo conference hall, Lula said: “We are going to change the way that this country is governed … This election was a defeat for denialism … I want to thank God and to thank you, Brazilian men and women of this country … it was democracy that made this happen.”

After the result was confirmed, Alexandre Padilha, Lula’s health minister, said: “This is a huge victory for the Brazilian people who in the second round had the chance to think about what President Lula means for the environment, for the defense of our national health service and our education system and the Brazilian constitution. And the risks that the extreme right candidate entailed.”

“It’s a massive victory – not just for Brazil. It’s also an important signal to the rest of Latin America and to the world,” Padilha added.

The 8o-year-old appears to have made up much of the difference through what his allies had identified as his main opportunity: persuading his voters to turn out. After a record abstention rate of 21.08% in the first round, which disproportionately affected Lula voters because they tend to live further from polling stations, the rate fell to 19.21%.

Speaking during Lula’s victory speech, Bolsonaro said it was “impossible to concede the election result” and that his legal team would seek to have the election annulled.

Following in the footsteps of his father, former president Jair Bolsonaro, who four years ago also refused to acknowledge his defeat to Lula – before attempting a coup and being convicted for it – the senator cited a supreme court justice’s decision to extend voting hours as the alleged reason.

His speech made no mention of the fact that his campaign had also asked the electoral court to extend voting hours, but he nevertheless said Justice Cristiano Zanin’s decision to extend voting by two hours to 7pm “was not normal, it was unacceptable” and amounted to “changing the rules of the game while the game was being played”.

For Lula, who turns 81 on Tuesday, the victory represents a sensational final chapter in a storied political career that began when he emerged as a union leader in São Paulo’s industrial suburbs in the late 1970s.

After entering politics, the former factory worker who was born in rural poverty made three unsuccessful presidential bids, in 1989, 1994 and 1998, before finally becoming Brazil’s first elected working-class president in 2002.

Lula stepped down after the maximum two consecutive terms allowed by the constitution but returned to power after winning the 2022 election against Bolsonaro’s father, then the incumbent president Jair Bolsonaro, who was subsequently jailed for plotting a coup.

Lula’s win is bad news for Jair Bolsonaro who is serving a 27-year sentence for that botched power grab and was hoping to be freed and given a role in government had his son prevailed.

It is also a blow for Donald Trump, the Bolsonaro clan’s most important international ally. The US president had been hoping Bolsonaro would become the latest in a series of pro-Trump leaders to take power in Latin America, including Abelardo de la Espriella in Colombia, Daniel Noboa in Ecuador, José Antonio Kast in Chile and Javier Milei in Argentina.

Confirmation of Lula’s victory was greeted with anger and bewilderment in Brasília, where Bolsonaro is living under house arrest and where his son watched the count.

On Sunday night, political leaders applauded Lula. Mexican president Claudia Sheinbaum said: “Long live democracy, the people of Brazil won. Congratulations, ⁠President Lula!”, while Paraguayan president Santiago Peña said “I salute the ⁠Brazilian people for this great democratic journey.”

Lula’s victory came after his supporters staged two massive shows of support on Saturday, with more than a million people flocking to Rio’s Copacabana beach and an even larger number marching on Paulista Avenue.

Many people on the streets of São Paulo were angry at an apparent attempt by pro-Bolsonaro politicians to derail the election by sabotaging public transport in Lula-leaning voting areas across the country.

“I think it’s another coup attempt that the extreme right is trying in Brazil. But we need to really fight so we can win,” said Lucas Lopes, a 32-year-old from São Paulo who works with public relations.

But that alleged attempt to scupper a Lula victory failed.

“I’ll be sincere: in the first round the left failed. But in the second round they woke up,” said Caio Marques, a 40-year-old photographer from Franca, a city in the interior of São Paulo state. “And there is no coup that will take this away from us.”

“I feel so, so happy … There’s not enough room in my chest for all this joy. Lula’s victory represents so much. It means life for those who believe in love and in life,” said Maicon Douglas Bento, a 34-year-old photographer, as he joined tens of thousands of jubilant Brazilians on Paulista Avenue at the heart of São Paulo.

Attackers kill nurse, severely injure health worker responding to Ebola case in eastern Congo

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

A nurse was killed and a health worker severely injured after being attacked while responding to an Ebola alert in Congo, according to the World Health Organization.

It was the latest in a series of attacks on health workers and responders that authorities say is hampering efforts to slow the fastest-growing Ebola outbreak in the world.

The attack took place Saturday in the town of Bafwasende in the country's eastern Tshopo province, WHO Director General Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus said in a post on X on Sunday.

“Everyone working to protect communities from this deadly disease must be able to work in dignity and safety. Healthcare must always be protected,” he said.

WHO regional director for Africa, Dr. Mohamed Yakub Janabi, also condemned the attack on X. “Misinformation and fear must never fuel violence,” he said.

Local authorities have not yet commented on the attack or the suspects.

The country’s health ministry’s most recent data indicates more than 9,800 cases including more than 4,700 deaths have been recorded since the start of the outbreak in May, making it the deadliest Ebola outbreak in Congo’s history.

Last month, a Congolese politician was beaten to death in North Kivu after publicly defending the government’s response to Ebola. He had been calling on the public to obey response measures, particularly social distancing and handwashing, on a radio show.

It came after authorities launched a village-centered approach to fight the outbreak which faced resistance from some residents who dismiss Ebola as a hoax. There is widespread distrust of the country’s government because of how it has managed past Ebola outbreaks as well as chronic corruption.

Responders say skepticism about the disease among communities has complicated efforts to work with residents, including in remote areas and emerging hot spots that already face shortages of medical supplies and healthcare workers.

Back in May at the start of the outbreak, two tents at health centers were set alight in two separate attacks in just one week by angry residents.

Those on the front line of the response to the outbreak have previously said some members of various response teams have been held captive in some health zones, and some teams responsible for safe and dignified burials have been threatened.

Ebola: Uganda introduces 21-day quarantine for people entering from DRC

📅 2026-10-26  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Ugandans returning home from DR Congo -- which has been hit by its worst ever Ebola outbreak -- will be required to spend 21 days in quarantine, the health ministry said Sunday.

The DRC, which shares a border with Uganda, has recorded more than 4,600 deaths since it declared its 17th Ebola outbreak in May. The virus has killed more than 15,000 people in Africa over the last 50 years.

In August, the World Health Organization officially declared Uganda Ebola-free after 20 cases, including two deaths, on its territory, almost exclusively among people coming from visits to DRC.

Ebola is transmitted through contact with bodily fluids and causes severe haemorrhagic fever. The current outbreak is caused by the virus' Bundibugyo strain for which there is still no vaccine or specific treatment.

"Returning Ugandans with a history of travel to the DRC will undergo 21 days of quarantine at their own cost." the Ugandan ministry stated, adding it had "intensified traveller screening at Entebbe International Airport and all land border crossings, supported by screening at designated road checkpoints."

Kenya, where two people have died, and Nigeria, the continent’s most populous country, reported their first cases in October.

Nigeria recorded its first case on Tuesday -- a woman who arrived in the capital Abuja on a flight from Uganda.

A Ugandan government spokesperson rejected meanwhile the idea that a patient identified in Nigeria might have contracted the virus in Uganda.

Alan Kasujja told AFP it was "absurd" to suggest that because someone was on holiday Uganda -— "which, incidentally, has no cases of Ebola" -- could spark a discussion about Ebola in Nigeria.

Medieval Children Were Exposed to Shocking Levels of Lead, 1,000 Years Before the Industrial Revolution

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Children in medieval English mining communities faced lead exposure matching or exceeding that in Industrial Revolution cities, with social standing apparently shaping who bore the greatest burden.

Between the 16th and 19th centuries, lead producers in the Derbyshire Peak District of central England repeatedly remelted waste left by Roman and medieval workings. Reusing that material erased many of the industrial traces archaeologists would normally examine to establish when mining and smelting resumed after Roman Britain. A record survived, however, in the teeth of the people who lived there.

Lead preserved in tooth enamel has now provided the first definitive evidence that production was established by the middle of the seventh century AD. It also reveals the extent of childhood exposure. Even in the seventh century, lead concentrations in the mining communities matched or exceeded those found in people from industrial cities around 1750 to 1850.

Mining and smelting exposed communities to lead through contaminated dust, fumes, and materials. Tooth enamel forms during childhood and does not remodel later in life, preserving a record of the lead absorbed while a person was growing up. Measuring the metal locked within enamel allowed researchers to reconstruct childhood exposure from about 650 to 1500 and compare its extent across individuals and communities.

The research was led by the University of Nottingham in collaboration with the British Geological Survey, the University of Sheffield, York Archaeology, and Castleton Historical Society. Combining archaeology, history, chemistry, and environmental science, it is the first study to analyze lead contamination in people who mined and smelted the metal in western Europe’s largest medieval lead-producing region.

The samples came from three cemetery populations spanning the industry’s revival and expansion. Wigber Low, in the Derbyshire White Peak, dates to the seventh century. At Castleton, Joule Yard dates from the seventh to 11th centuries, while Spital Field dates from the 12th to 15th centuries. The research builds on decades of archaeological investigation, including earlier work by Colin Merrony at the University of Sheffield, who identified and excavated key medieval burial sites in the region.

To place the measurements in context, the researchers compared these communities with populations elsewhere in Britain, ranging from the Neolithic period through the Industrial Revolution. This comparison revealed how high childhood exposure already was during the early medieval period, long before the industry reached its greatest scale.

Lead author Christopher Loveluck, Professor of Medieval European Archaeology at the University of Nottingham’s Department of Classics and Archaeology, said, “The team is delighted to have finally confirmed definitively the date by which the early medieval lead industry was established in the Peak District, by the mid-7th century AD, through evidence provided by the people themselves, providing the foundations of western Europe’s largest lead-producing region between the late 12th and 16th centuries.”

By the middle of the eighth century, large-scale production had contaminated the wider environment around mining centers such as Castleton. Childhood lead exposure remained high throughout the medieval period, reaching its greatest levels in the late 12th and early 13th centuries. Historical records place the medieval peak of Peak District lead production in the same period.

That timing also connects the people’s teeth with evidence collected far beyond their communities. Previous studies of Alpine ice cores and sediment cores from river channels, lakes, and peat bogs across western Europe identified a rise in atmospheric lead pollution around 1200, attributed especially to British production. The enamel findings provide a direct link between those environmental records and the people living and working in one of Europe’s major lead-producing landscapes.

Within the mining communities, exposure varied with apparent social standing. People buried with high-status grave goods, including weapons, generally had much lower lead concentrations than those whose burials were less richly furnished. The pattern suggests that elites may have controlled or benefited from lead production while lower-status households worked more directly with ore processing and smelting. Children growing up in those households would consequently have faced greater exposure.

Isotopic analysis indicated that most of the individuals studied had grown up locally. This suggests that mining and smelting knowledge passed through generations within relatively stable communities, rather than the industry depending on large numbers of incoming workers.

Merrony said, “Our recent excavations in Castleton have provided a lot of new information about the people living in the Hope Valley during the medieval period. We now understand more about the size of the population in the valley, and we can see from the archaeological evidence that these working communities were involved in industries that required hard physical labor. However, this study has enabled us, for the first time, to see beyond this broader understanding and to look more deeply into the profound impact lead production had on individuals and the communities they lived in.”

Lead can damage the nervous system, kidneys, and reproductive system, making the childhood exposures a concern even for people who survived for many years afterward. Three women analyzed in the study had elevated childhood lead exposure and died between approximately 17 and 25 years of age. The relationship between childhood exposure and premature death is complex, so these findings suggest a possible association with increased health risks without establishing that lead caused their deaths. Pregnancy may have been a particularly vulnerable time because lead stored in the body can be released back into the bloodstream.

Dr. Anna Bloxam, co-director of the Castleton excavations, said, “We know from modern studies that lead can have profoundly harmful effects on the health and development of children: there is no safe level of exposure. All the individuals included in our study survived into adulthood, but they were exposed to lead while they were young and growing. We can tell from their skeletons that most of the men we studied lived until middle or old age, whereas the women primarily died much earlier, in young adulthood.

“Our results indicate that in these mining communities, girls and boys alike were raised in dangerously polluted home environments. This exposure will likely have caused negative health effects in life, and perhaps contributed to higher rates of premature death among women of childbearing age.”

The findings concern medieval mining and smelting between the seventh and 15th centuries and do not describe modern environmental conditions. The legacy of historic mining in the Peak District is now well understood and carefully monitored, with land management, conservation, and environmental regulation helping to minimize risks associated with former mining activity.

The results will contribute to the developing medieval archaeological research agenda for the Peak District National Park. A temporary display in the region during fall and winter 2026–27 will present the research alongside archaeological discoveries from the Castleton excavations.

Reference: “Social dynamics of toxic metal industry in medieval Europe: Peak District lead production, AD 600–1500” by Christopher P. Loveluck, Hannah J. O’Regan, Anna Bloxam, Catrin Fear, Jane Evans, Simon Chenery, Carolyn Chenery, Colin Merrony, Fiona E. Moore and Angela Darlington, 13 October 2026, Antiquity. DOI: 10.15184/aqy.2026.10416

The research was funded and supported by the University of Nottingham, the Peak District National Park Authority, Castleton Historical Society, the University of Sheffield, Sheffield Museums and Historic England.

Published - October 25, 2026 10:48 pm IST - Caracas (Venezuela)

Venezuelan Opposition leader Maria Corina Machado arrives at a church after returning to the country following nearly a year abroad, in Lara, Venezuela, on October 25, 2026. | Photo Credit: Reuters

Opposition leader and Nobel laureate Maria Corina Machado reappeared in Venezuela on Sunday (October 25, 2026) for the first time since December, returning from exile at a time when doubts about the country's democratic future — and the role of the U.S. —are growing.

It was her first appearance since the U.S. military deposed the country's then-President Nicolás Maduro, replacing him with an interim president who has exceeded the time she was authorised to serve, with no election scheduled.

Ms. Machado announced her arrival on social media, writing, “I am here. With you.”

Another leading figure in the Venezuelan opposition, Edmundo González, wrote on X confirming that Ms. Machado had arrived and that he would be next to her “very soon.”

“Today is a very important day for Venezuelans. Our sovereign voice returns with her to lead and guide this process!” he wrote. “You know it, this is until the end.”

On Sunday afternoon (October 25, 2026), Ms. Machado strode into a church in the town of Lara, west of Caracas, to the applause of dozens gathered there as they shook her hand and hugged her, with cell phones aloft to record her return. “Maria! Maria!” someone cried out.

“Welcome to Venezuela!” another person shouted.

Asked by reporters on Sunday (October 25, 2026) about Ms. Machado's return to Venezuela, U.S. President Donald Trump said, “I don't know anything about it, but I think she's a very nice woman. She gave me her Nobel Prize. So how can I say anything else? And I hope she's safe. She's a very nice woman. But we're doing really well in Venezuela.”

Since Mr. Maduro's ouster, Venezuela has been run by acting President Delcy Rodriguez, a longtime Maduro ally who received the Trump administration's blessing to succeed him.

Ms. Rodriguez was initially supposed to serve only 90 days as acting president, but has remained in office and has made no move to call an election.

Ms. Machado was briefly detained in January 2025, following a protest against Mr. Maduro's planned swearing-in ceremony, and went into hiding in Venezuela for 11 months. After making a clandestine escape from the country, she reappeared that December in Norway, where she was awarded the Nobel Peace Prize for her peaceful challenge to Mr. Maduro's authoritarian government.

Ms. Machado and her allies have been demanding a date for a presidential election in Venezuela, but U.S. officials have emphasized economic stabilization first.

In October 2023, Ms. Machado won an opposition primary and intended to challenge Mr. Maduro in the 2024 presidential election, but the government barred her from running for office.

Mr. González took her place on the ballot, and ample credible evidence showed that he defeated Mr. Maduro by more than a 2-to-1 margin. But the National Electoral Council, which is stacked with ruling-party loyalists, declared Mr. Maduro the winner.

The lead-up and aftermath of the election were marked by widespread government repression, including forced disappearances, torture and other human rights violations. More than 2,000 people, including poll workers, were detained in the days after the vote.

In January, the U.S. military captured Mr. Maduro in Caracas and whisked him to New York to face drug-related charges, which he has denied. Many Venezuelans were stunned by the Trump administration's decision to work with Ms. Rodriguez, instead of Ms. Machado, to try to revitalise the crisis-racked country.

Ms. Machado had repeatedly vowed to return to Venezuela and, according to her party, she made seven unsuccessful attempts.

One of the efforts would have seen her take a charter boat, but the operator canceled the trip, alleging the vessel was unfit for navigation. In another attempt, her team said, Ms. Machado tried to fly to the Caribbean island of Curacao, from where she could take a speedboat to Venezuela, but her plane was denied landing permits.

Venezuela’s Nicolas Maduro wins third term as President amid Opposition claims of irregularities

Opposition activists have accused Venezuela's government of blocking Ms. Machado's return, a claim denied by Venezuelan authorities. Others have also questioned whether the Trump administration has also been involved in the efforts to stop her from returning.

Trump administration officials deny they are holding Ms. Machado back but have said that, on some occasions, they have advised her against returning due to safety concerns.

After Mr. Maduro was deposed, Venezuela's highest court said Ms. Rodriguez could serve as acting president for up to 90 days — an interim appointment that could be extended by the National Assembly for an additional 90 days. But the ruling party-controlled Assembly never voted to extend her term.

Ms. Machado and her allies have demanded that the Rodriguez government set a date for a presidential election. Rodriguez told the UN General Assembly in September that Venezuelans will determine the “right moment” for the next election.

U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio has argued that before holding new elections, Venezuela must stabilise its economy and rebuild its institutions, including its courts and electoral council. But he said in a news conference earlier this month that Ms. Machado has “every right to enter her country whenever she wants to return.”

Supporters of Ms. Machado have held demonstrations in Caracas demanding her return.

Uganda imposes 21-day quarantine on citizens returning from Congo

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Sign up now: Get ST's newsletters delivered to your inbox

KAMPALA, Oct 25 - Ugandan citizens returning to the country with a history of travel to Democratic Republic of Congo will be quarantined for 21 days, Health Minister Chris Baryomunsi said on Sunday as Uganda tightened Ebola precautions.

Uganda says it is Ebola-free after declaring an end to its latest outbreak in July, but there has been concern among health experts after two people who had recently passed through the country later tested positive in Kenya and Nigeria.

Uganda says it is investigating the Nigerian case, which involves the wife of a Swiss diplomat. The woman, 42, returned from Uganda through Addis Ababa to Abuja on Oct. 3, according to Nigeria's Centre for Disease Control, which said she is in isolation and has improved remarkably.

The agency said 53 contacts had been traced.

Ugandan officials are reviewing genomic sequencing, travel information, interactions and movements in coordination with Nigerian authorities and international partners.

Swiss authorities had said the woman is believed to have contracted the virus in Uganda. Baryomunsi pushed back, saying Switzerland was "speaking prematurely". He said the woman's relatives from West Nile visited her in Kansanga, a suburb of Kampala, and that those relatives had visited Congo.

"What evidence do they have that she never travelled there?" he said. "We should speak science, not politics."

Kenya's first Ebola patient had travelled from Congo to Uganda and taken a flight from Entebbe airport to Nairobi on Oct. 3. The patient died two days later.

Both the Kenyan and the Nigerian cases travelled through the same Ugandan airport on the same day but there was no physical interaction between the two, Diana Atwine, the health ministry's permanent secretary, told the press conference, citing a review of the airport's CCTV system.

Baryomunsi said all 60 contacts linked to a Kenyan Ebola case had completed their 21-day monitoring period and been discharged.

"The ministry has intensified traveller screening at Entebbe International Airport and all land border crossings. All travellers intending to enter Uganda by air, land or water must complete the passenger locator form," he said.

Genomic analysis of Nigeria's only confirmed Ebola case showed the virus was closely related to the strain detected in Kenya's index case, though Nigerian authorities said there was no evidence that the Kenya case travelled to Nigeria or had a direct connection.

Uganda borders Congo, where the outbreak began, and has previously battled Ebola outbreaks.

Late on Sunday, World Health Organization Director-General Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus said a nurse was killed and a health worker severely injured when they were attacked while responding to an Ebola alert in the northern Congolese town of Bafwasende.

"Everyone working to protect communities from this deadly disease must be able to work in dignity and safety," he wrote on X, saying the attack occurred on Saturday. REUTERS

Two dead and 160,000 properties without power as Storm Boelo batters UK

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Man aged 60 and another in his 20s killed by falling trees in Scotland and London as Met Office reports gusts of up to 84mph

Two people were killed and more than 800,000 properties left without power after Storm Boelo and Storm Austen battered the UK over the weekend.

Two men, one aged 60 and the other in his 20s, were killed by falling trees on Sunday, police said. The 60-year-old died in south-west Scotland while the man in his 20s was killed in London.

A spokesperson for Police Scotland said: “We were made aware of a 60-year-old man having been struck by a falling tree on a minor road at Mid Kelton at about 10.30am on Sunday, 25 October 2026.

“The man was pronounced dead at the scene. There are no suspicious circumstances and a report will be submitted to the procurator fiscal in due course.”

In London, the man was killed when a tree fell on to his car at 12.40pm as he drove through Mill Hill in the north of the city.

Ch Insp Guy Ellwood said: “Our thoughts are with the loved ones of the young man who lost his life.”

Storm Boelo arrived in northern parts of the UK on Saturday evening and was the second named storm in as many days, after Storm Austen brought strong winds to south-west England on Friday.

The Met Office said wind speeds widely reached 60-70mph, affecting areas in an amber warning zone that came into force at 9pm on Saturday.

The warning covered Scotland, parts of Northern Ireland and the far north of England, with gusts reaching more than 80mph in exposed coastal spots.

The island of Tiree in the Inner Hebrides was hit by 82mph winds, according to the forecaster, while wind speeds reached 84mph in Argyll and Bute and 83mph in Snowdonia (Eryri).

Power cuts hit 838,700 homes across Britain during the two storms. By 7pm on Sunday, 90,600 customers were still without power, said the Energy Networks Association (ENA), which represents operators in the UK and Ireland.

Northern Powergrid, which distributes electricity across Yorkshire and the north-east, said on Sunday evening that 13,000 customers in the region were expected to be without power beyond Sunday night. The energy company added electricity had already been restored to 102,000 customers.

The Scottish government’s “resilience room” met to discuss the impact of the storm on Sunday afternoon, with ministers focusing on the outages and how they were being restored.

Scotland’s first minister, John Swinney, met senior politicians, emergency agencies and forecasters to discuss the government’s response, amid warnings that similar conditions would return early next week.

The ENA said: “The networks have been focused on reconnecting customers this morning, though the continuing severe weather in some regions is limiting network engineers’ opportunities to safely start the work of restoring customers’ power today and access to certain areas remains difficult. Engineers are working around the clock to identify and fix faults.” (Delete)

Train travel was disrupted on Sunday. Network Rail Scotland shared an image of flooding from the tracks to the platform at one Glasgow station, and fallen trees above the lines near another. Speed restrictions were in place across Scotland and several sections of its railways were closed, the operator added.

Network Rail shared images of a trampoline beneath a train and a shed by the tracks, and it reminded people living near rail lines to firmly secure objects in their gardens.

Our morning email breaks down the key stories of the day, telling you what’s happening and why it matters

Some flights were diverted from landing at Leeds Bradford airport and others were cancelled.

The Met Office advised people in affected areas to secure loose items in gardens and to stay indoors as the storm posed a danger to life from flying debris and crashing waves.

The Met Office chief forecaster, Jason Kelly, said: “Storm Boelo will be felt across the UK this weekend, with the strongest winds and greatest potential for disruption in the north.

“Amber warnings are in place across mainland Scotland, parts of Northern Ireland and the far north of England, where gusts of 60-70mph are expected widely, with the potential for 80mph in exposed coastal locations.

Several events have been cancelled this weekend due to the inclement weather. Visit Liverpool said the River of Light event, featuring illuminated artworks, had been cancelled “in the interests of safety”, while London’s annual Diwali on the Square celebrations on Sunday were cancelled on the advice of independent health and safety experts.

Edinburgh Castle was closed on Sunday because of the high winds and adverse weather, while the Fountains Abbey estate in North Yorkshire was also closed for the day, after its Fountains by Floodlight event on Saturday evening was cancelled.

The Environment Agency early on Monday issued 10 warnings that flooding is expected and 65 alerts where it is possible. The Scottish Environment Protection Agency said 10 flood warnings and 11 flood alerts were in place, while Natural Resources Wales reported nine flood alerts were active at the same time.

Cumbria police said they had received a number of calls overnight to localised flooding and fallen trees blocking roads across the county, and urged motorists to drive with care while emergency services worked to clear the obstructions.

The amber warning sits within wider yellow wind warnings, which cover parts of England farther south, as well as north Wales and the whole of Northern Ireland.

The Scottish social justice and housing secretary, Shirley-Anne Somerville, said on Sunday: “Disruption to travel is likely to continue into tomorrow and I strongly recommend people check official sources such as the Met Office, Traffic Scotland, transport operators and local authority channels before setting out.

“While we expect some respite from the adverse weather during the day on Monday, there is a possibility of more severe weather conditions early on in the week and we urge everyone to continue to follow advice and heed any warnings.”
