Eating 30% Less Food Has an Unexpected Effect on DNA, Study Finds

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Eating fewer calories may do more than slow certain aspects of aging. Scientists have found that mice consuming 30 percent less food accumulated fewer DNA mutations across several tissues, suggesting that diet can influence how genetic damage builds up over time.

The study, published in Cell, examined mutations across a large portion of the genome rather than focusing on individual genes. The results showed that calorie restriction affected different organs and regions of DNA in surprisingly different ways.

DNA mutations develop throughout life as cells experience genetic damage or make mistakes while copying and repairing their DNA. Most mutations are harmless, but some interfere with normal gene function and can contribute to cancer and other diseases. Although animal studies have established that calorie restriction can extend lifespan and slow certain effects of aging, its broader influence on mutation accumulation has remained unclear.

To investigate, researchers led by NYU Langone Health and University Hospitals Cleveland worked with scientists at the University of Texas Southwestern Medical Center and the National Institutes of Health. Using highly accurate DNA sequencing technology, they examined genetic changes in mice fed 30 percent fewer calories than animals allowed to eat freely.

The restricted diet reduced several types of mutations, including substitutions, which occur when one DNA letter is replaced by another, and insertions and deletions, which add or remove DNA letters. However, the reductions differed across tissues, with liver cells showing a stronger response than kidney or brain cells.

“While links between diet and lifespan have been established in animals, our results show that diet and mutations across the genome are also linked,” said co-corresponding study author Gilad D. Evrony, MD, PhD, a researcher at NYU Langone Health and associate professor at NYU Grossman School of Medicine. “Mice on a calorie-restricted diet are healthier for longer, but this diet is too challenging for humans. Nevertheless, considering the role mutations play in cancer and many other diseases, it’s exciting to have found a way to decrease mutations, which we can now work to understand.”

The researchers also discovered that calorie restriction did not affect all regions of DNA equally. In liver and kidney cells, the greatest reductions in mutations occurred in the least active portions of the genome. These regions contain either no genes or genes that are not being used by the cells.

One possible explanation involves differences in DNA repair activity. Regions containing actively used genes already undergo frequent DNA repair, which may limit how much additional protection they receive if calorie restriction reduces DNA damage. Less active regions, where repair occurs less frequently, could experience a larger reduction in accumulated mutations. The researchers have not yet established whether this mechanism explains the pattern.

“We didn’t expect to find this asymmetry across the genome,” said co-corresponding author Jonathan Shoag, MD, an associate professor in the Department of Urology at University Hospitals Cleveland and Case Western Reserve University School of Medicine. “By profiling more dietary interventions and mutations in additional tissues and cell types, we hope to learn what drives the connection between diet and mutations.”

The findings establish a connection between calorie restriction and lower mutation levels in mice, but they do not demonstrate that people would experience the same benefits. Sustained, substantial calorie restriction is also difficult to maintain and can carry health risks.

Researchers now hope to identify the biological processes responsible for the reduction in mutations. Understanding those processes could help scientists investigate approaches that protect DNA without requiring people to follow severely restricted diets.

“Understanding these mechanisms could eventually reveal new ways to reduce mutations – without the challenges and risks of an extreme diet—to counter diseases of aging,” said first study author Marta Grońska-Pęski, PhD, a postdoctoral fellow in Evrony’s laboratory.

The research relied on a specially established group of mice used to study the biological effects of calorie restriction. “We were fortunate to have collaborated with Dr. Victoria Acosta-Rodríguez, Dr. Joseph Takahashi, and Dr. Carla Green, who established this mouse caloric restriction cohort,” said Dr. Evrony.

Reference: “Caloric restriction modulates genome-wide somatic mutation in mice” by Marta Grońska-Pęski, Victoria Acosta-Rodríguez, Amoolya Srinivasa, Amanda Garrido, Shany Picciotto, Carla B. Green, Joseph S. Takahashi, Jonathan E. Shoag and Gilad D. Evrony, 9 September 2026, Cell. DOI: 10.1016/j.cell.2026.08.013

This work was supported by National Institutes of Health grants T32AG052909, F32AG076287, and UH3NS132024, the Pew Charitable Trusts, the Jacob Goldfield Foundation, the Damon Runyon Cancer Research Foundation, the Smythe Family Impact Fund, the Vail family, and the Howard Hughes Medical Institute.

Психолог предупредила о последствиях жизни по принципу «на том свете выспимся»

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Психолог Розер Горт предупредила, что жизнь по принципу «на том свете выспимся» — его она раньше придерживалась и сама — может привести к негативным последствиям для организма. Ее слова передает издание Men's Health.

Горт подчеркнула, что из-за недосыпа на первых порах проблемы возникают не со здоровьем, а с темпераментом. К примеру, человек может стать нетерпеливым, импульсивным и склонным к негативным эмоциям, отметила специалистка.

Кроме того, у него зачастую снижается способность запоминать информацию, поэтому закономерно возникают проблемы с учебой и работой, добавила психолог. «Ваша работоспособность падает гораздо быстрее, чем вы это воспринимаете. Вы все еще считаете себя способным на что-то. Это тот же механизм, что и у человека, который выпил алкоголь и чувствует себя совершенно способным сесть за руль», — объяснила она.

Ранее сомнолог Николай Ильин рассказал, какие напитки приводят к бессоннице. Он посоветовал воздержаться от употребления алкоголя в вечернее время.

U.S. measles cases more than double what they were last year as outbreak spreads

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A sign is posted outside a Pennsylvania Department of Health measles vaccination clinic located in Lancaster County in Peach Bottom, Pa., Tuesday, Oct. 20, 2026. Matt Rourke/AP hide caption

The U.S. is grappling with its worst resurgence of measles in decades, with cases particularly high in the state of Pennsylvania.

New data from the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention shows there have been more than 4,580 measles cases across the U.S. since Oct. 22, which is more than double the number for the whole of 2025.

The vaccination status of 96% of those who contracted measles in 2026 was "Unvaccinated or Unknown," according to data from the CDC.

A measles outbreak across Pennsylvania has grown as officials urge residents to get fully vaccinated against the disease.

At least 1,338 cases of measles have been confirmed in 44 counties across the state so far this year, according to the Pennsylvania Department of Health on Friday. There were 21 new measles cases as of Oct. 21. More than 250 people have been hospitalized and 5 people have died, the department also said.

In Lancaster and Mifflin counties, two of the counties with the highest number of cases, local Amish communities, which have low vaccination rates, are being impacted the most. Of the five who have died, three lived in Lancaster County

On Thursday, Pennsylvania Secretary of Health Dr. Debra Bogen held a roundtable with Mifflin County healthcare providers to assess their needs in combatting the disease.

"Our state metrics and data tell part of the story of the measles outbreak in Pennsylvania, but listening to the people on the ground caring for people in affected communities like Mifflin County is essential to refining our public health response," Bogen said in a statement.

One of the best ways to protect oneself from getting the measles is vaccination, according to health officials. Two doses of measles, mumps, and rubella vaccine known as MMR, is nearly 97% effective against measles, according to the CDC. One dose is about 93% effective.

In Pennsylvania, the health department said healthcare providers have seen more residents interested in getting vaccinated for measles. Across the state, more than 39,000 measles vaccines were administered during the first three weeks of October – higher than an average of 25,000 vaccines administered across the state in one month, the health department said.

Earlier this month, New York Gov. Kathy Hochul declared a disaster emergency after rising measles cases. There have been over 160 confirmed measles across the state, according to Oct. 20 data from the New York State Department of Health.

Utah has also experienced a high number of measles cases. More than 700 people in Utah have been diagnosed with measles this year, according to Oct. 20 data from the Utah Department of Health and Human Services. There have not been any measles diagnoses in the state in 3 weeks, according to the department.

In South Carolina, which was dealing with an outbreak earlier this year, there have been at least 997 measles cases in 2026, according to the South Carolina Department of Public Health. The outbreak ended in April.

French protest groups and unions called on Sunday (October 26, 2026) for further ‌demonstrations as they sought to maintain pressure on the government after a ​huge security operation helped to contain unrest during nationwide rallies on Saturday (October 25, 2026) .

Architect struck off after bizarre neighbour dispute over 'phallic symbol' car

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Dr Karin Elliott said that her neighbour driving across her driveway was a manifestation of gender violence

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

A Norwich architect who accused a neighbour of gender violence by using his car as a “phallic symbol” on her driveway has been struck off for making unfounded claims against her neighbours.

Dr Karin Elliott was removed from the register for at least two years by the Architects Registration Board after sending inappropriate communications that amounted to unacceptable professional conduct.

A tribunal decided that she used “inflammatory language, making a series of misleading and potentially damaging accusations” in 2024 regarding her neighbours, “with scant regard to their accuracy”.

The board described the Irish-American architect’s actions as “misleading, lacking integrity and dishonest”.

Ms Elliott accused one neighbour of “racial harassment” through false claims that he wielded a chainsaw in her garden, the tribunal heard.

The architect’s partner, Francois Girardin, told the tribunal that none of the neighbours said “anything directly racist”.

However, Mr Girardin, who is French, made an “inference” of racism from how “sometimes a Union Jack would appear” and “a wall had been constructed after the US president had talked about building a big wall to keep out immigrants”, the tribunal heard.

Mr Girardin also said whenever England played sport, a neighbour would “leave their doors open, and – especially when England played France – their family would cheer for England”.

During cross-examination, Dr Elliott said from “a psychoanalytic perspective” her neighbour repeatedly driving across her driveway with the “phallic symbol” of his car was a manifestation of gender violence.

The tribunal found this “underscored how tenuously she constructed serious accusations against other people, and how casually she dispensed them”.

The architect had made a planning application for the installation of CCTV cameras on her property in December 2023, following disputes with her neighbours.

She wrote a letter to local residents in February 2024 asking for support with the application, and made serious allegations about her neighbours that the tribunal considered to be “dishonest” and “misleading”.

In the letter, she claimed one neighbour had “smashed down several of our historic walls, chopped down many of our historic trees, and built on our property, all without our consent”.

Discussing another neighbour, Dr Elliott wrote: “He calls us either a ‘piece of s***’, ‘disgusting’ and/or exclaims ‘the stench’ regularly.”

In another allegation, the architect wrote: “He threatened to hit one of us over the head with a hammer at one stage, after he was asked to desist.

“He had been starting to build a wall on our property illegally on a Sunday, following Trump’s declaration that he would ‘build a big wall’ to keep out immigrants.

“It is very upsetting. We are Irish/American and French.”

Discussing claims about a third neighbour, Dr Elliott accused her of building an “ugly concrete foundation” and using influence at the local council “to escape justice”.

She also accused her neighbour of singing “Tragedy” by the Bee Gees “from the enormous balcony they built overlooking our home” after the death of her father.

Discussing the three neighbours, Dr Elliott wrote: “We hope that the CCTV might deter them all from further trespassing and further destroying our listed garden, and from any further gender violence and racial harassment.”

In cross-examination, the architect said: “I think I’d give this letter a serious editing if I did it again.”

Dr Elliott made further allegations in a planning appeal form to the Norwich City Planning Portal in March 2024.

Discussing one neighbour in the planning form, she wrote: “I begged him to turn off his chainsaw and explained that I considered it to be racial harassment.”

One neighbour told the tribunal that the architect’s “bizarre accusations of threatening behaviour and the racist accusations have been a source of acute embarrassment and personal discomfort”.

Another said the allegations were “very depressing and upsetting” and they do not spend time in their garden because Ms Elliott and her partner are “always looking at us”, adding that the architect is an “absolute disgrace to her profession”.

The tribunal found that Dr Elliott’s conduct occurred after she had previously been found guilty of unacceptable professional conduct in 2021 for calling neighbours “racists and Nazis”, as well as “making offensive references to their physical characteristics and mental health”.

Published - October 25, 2026 05:54 pm IST - COLOMBO

File photo for representational purposes only. | Photo Credit: Reuters

Around 40 stranded Iranian sailors aboard U.S.-sanctioned oil tankers anchored just outside Sri Lanka’s territorial waters received urgent medical treatment in the island nation on Saturday (October 24, 2026), Sri Lanka’s Foreign Ministry said.

On Saturday (October 24, 2026), Sri Lankan authorities facilitated the transfer of the Iranian sailors from their vessels to the shore through the port of Galle in the country’s Southern Province, Foreign Affairs Ministry spokesman Thushara Rodrigo told The Hindu. “We coordinated the transfer alone as per our humanitarian obligations. The Iranian Embassy bore all the expenses,” he said.

A senior official at the Iranian Embassy in Colombo confirmed the development and said the sailors were taken from Galle to Colombo for medical attention before leaving Sri Lanka on Saturday night (October 24, 2026), indicating they spent only a few hours on the island.

The development comes a month after The Wall Street Journal reported that U.S. “pressure” through sanctions had left the Iranian vessels stranded in Sri Lankan waters. At the time, the Sri Lankan Foreign Ministry said Colombo would not be extending assistance to the sanctioned vessels, while emphasising that the secondary sanctions were part of a broader directive from Washington, D.C. and not specific to Sri Lanka. Denying media reports of U.S. pressure on Sri Lanka, Foreign Minister Vijitha Herath said the Sri Lankan government made decisions “strictly protecting the country’s sovereignty”, even as it was prepared to intervene appropriately on humanitarian grounds.

Also read | U.S. will ‘bitterly regret’ attacking IRIS Dena: Iranian Foreign Minister

Earlier this year, Sri Lanka made headlines when a U.S. submarine attacked and sank Iranian frigate IRIS Dena in the Indian Ocean, off the island’s southern coast. At least 84 people were killed in the torpedo attack, which sent shockwaves across the region. Sri Lanka’s ruling Anura Kumara Dissanayake administration, while reiterating its commitment to a “neutral foreign policy”, directed its Navy to evacuate the injured sailors. They were subsequently treated at a public hospital in the Galle district.

Uganda tightens Ebola border controls and requires returnees from Congo to pay for quarantine

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Uganda is reinforcing border controls to stop the spread of the deadly Ebola virus and now requires citizens returning from neighboring Congo to spend 21 days in quarantine at their own expense, the Health Ministry said Sunday, as investigators examine how a woman who traveled from Uganda to Nigeria contracted the disease.

The outbreak in Congo has killed thousands, but Uganda has recorded no confirmed cases since declaring its outbreak over on July 28. It first established border controls in May, restricting all but essential travelers, including humanitarian workers and truck drivers, subject to screening.

Travelers with a history of travel to Congo cannot transit through Uganda, including on chartered flights.

Under the new measures announced Sunday, nonessential Congolese travelers will be returned through the nearest border crossing at their own expense, while those entering illegally face prosecution, the Ugandan ministry said.

Screening has intensified at Entebbe International Airport and land borders. All arrivals must complete a passenger locator form and undergo health screening, including diplomats and United Nations personnel.

Asked about reports that Nigeria’s first Ebola patient had not visited Congo, Health Minister Chris Baryomunsi said Ugandan investigators had not established whether she traveled there. He said she had relatives in Uganda and Congo and held two passports, one of them Ugandan. He did not identify the second issuing country.

Switzerland’s foreign ministry told The Associated Press on Friday that the woman, married to a Swiss diplomat in Nigeria, had not visited Congo and was believed to have contracted Ebola while traveling to Uganda.

Baryomunsi said Nigerian authorities had not provided all the information Uganda needed. Investigators are working with Nigeria and international partners to review genomic findings and verify her movements and interactions in Uganda and en route to Abuja. Findings will be released later, Baryomunsi said.

Nigeria’s Health Minister Muhammad Pate said Friday that the woman's infection appeared closely related to Kenya’s first case, while cautioning that this did not necessarily establish the transmission route.

The Africa Centers for Disease Control and Prevention previously said both patients passed through Entebbe airport on Oct. 3. Where the Nigerian patient contracted Ebola remains unresolved.

Uganda also said all 60 contacts of Kenya’s first patient, including border personnel, hotel staff and transport operators, completed 21 days of monitoring without symptoms and were released from follow-up.

The patient, a 40-year-old Kenyan man living in Congo, traveled through Uganda and arrived in Kenya Oct. 3. He tested positive and died Oct. 5. Eight contacts subsequently tested positive; a 65-year-old close relative died Friday, bringing Kenya’s death toll to two.

Uganda said it had deployed Ebola experts to Congo to help contain the Bundibugyo Ebola strain.

The Guardian view on Oxford Street’s pedestrianisation: cities should be designed around people, not cars | Editorial

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The transformation of Europe’s busiest shopping street should be a case study in making cities greener, less congested and more inviting places to live

Sadiq Khan has been trying to pedestrianise Oxford Street since he first became the mayor of London in 2016. On Monday, after numerous setbacks, his plan will finally come to fruition. The busiest stretch of the nation’s famous shopping street will be closed to traffic in a scheme designed to improve the area and make it more attractive to pedestrians. It should be an emblematic case study in making cities greener, less congested, more inviting places to live and visit.

The Greater London Authority hopes it will rejuvenate a high street fallen on hard times. Since the pandemic, the number of vacant shops on Oxford Street has more than quadrupled. It estimates that the scheme will generate £82m of value a year in the area by increasing footfall and encouraging visitors to linger. This impact remains to be seen, but research from other cities suggests that shops experience higher sales once an area is pedestrianised. Given the prime minister’s focus on rejuvenating British high streets, whose dilapidation is correlated with support for Reform UK, he should be watching with interest.

The benefits of pedestrianisation are far greater than increased shopping. Domestic transport remains the largest contributor to greenhouse-gas emissions in Britain. This sector’s emissions have fallen more slowly than others largely because too many people still rely on cars. Mr Khan’s city is an outlier: 59% of all journeys in Britain were made by car in 2024, while in London cars accounted for 34%. This is hardly surprising when the Treasury spends twice as much on London’s transport system as it does on other regions in England, and the capital’s bus fares are cheaper. In Leeds, which has no mass-transit system, nearly half of people drive to work. Car use cannot be reduced without simultaneously improving public transport.

The critics of pedestrianisation – including Westminster council – suggest it merely displaces traffic elsewhere. But the evidence on this is more nuanced. One study from the University of the West of England suggests the closure of strategic roads causes some traffic to disappear permanently, as people switch to public transport or walk. Low-traffic neighbourhoods (LTNs), the pedestrianisation schemes that were rushed through in the capital during Covid-19 and have since been introduced in Manchester and Birmingham, were fiercely criticised for causing a buildup of road traffic elsewhere. Yet one study of 46 LTNs in London suggested their impact on traffic elsewhere was minimal.

Car-free areas should be accompanied by other policies that reduce pollution on surrounding roads, such as the low-emissions zones introduced in London and Oxford. Mr Khan was criticised by the then leader of the opposition, Keir Starmer, for the expansion of Ulez in 2023, and also suffered racial abuse. He has since been vindicated by research from Imperial College suggesting Ulez has significantly improved the health of primary school children in the capital, whose lung growth had been stunted by pollution.

That Transport for London estimates the pedestrianisation of Oxford Street will have only a minimal impact on nitrogen dioxide emissions, which were already decreasing as a result of Ulez, is testimony to the policy’s success. In 2014, Oxford Street had the highest concentration of nitrogen dioxide pollution in the world. Its pedestrianisation should be an inspiration to other cities to embrace policies that work for people, not cars.

Published - October 25, 2026 05:09 pm IST - Washington

U.S. President Donald Trump. File | Photo Credit: Reuters

U.S. President Donald Trump announced on Saturday (October 24, 2026) that German automaker Audi planned to invest $4.2 billion to build its first factory in the United States, 10 days before midterm elections.

Audi confirmed the investment in a release, saying the plant would create about 4,500 jobs.

"I'm thrilled to announce a massive auto manufacturing investment in the great state of Georgia by a fantastic company," Mr. Trump said at a campaign rally ahead of the November 3 vote that will determine control of the U.S. Congress.

"Audi has just committed to invest $4.2 billion in nearby Macon, Georgia, to begin the construction next month on a massive plant that will produce 200,000 cars a year," Mr. Trump continued.

Audi, which is a unit of Volkswagen (VW), said in its release that construction was scheduled to begin next year and production in the early 2030s.

"The planned investment intends to mark a long-term commitment to the United States and to establish North America as a third major pillar of Audi's global production network alongside Europe and China," it said.

VW, long a titan of German industry, has struggled with U.S. tariffs, Chinese competition and patchy demand in Europe.

Increasing production in the United States represents an "opportunity", "provided that factories and employees in Germany also benefit from it", said Olaf Lies, president of the German region of Lower Saxony, where VW is headquartered.

In September, the 10-brand Volkswagen Group struck a deal with unions to axe up to 100,000 jobs by 2030, increasing by 50,000 the number of expected cuts across the group.

The cuts will be the largest restructuring ever carried out in the global automotive industry.

Mr. Trump has claimed a "roaring comeback" in American auto manufacturing in response to his aggressive trade policy.

Since returning to the White House in January 2025, Mr. Trump has wielded executive authority to slap tariffs on various products -- including autos -- and countries.

In response, carmakers including General Motors, Toyota, and Ford have announced plans to expand U.S. plants or shift production from overseas.

Grievances over Mr. Trump's trade policies are among fault lines that Democrats hope will work in their favor in the midterm elections.

Mr. Trump is putting all his efforts into campaigning ahead of the elections as he faces dismal polls and unexpectedly tight races in Republican strongholds.

DR MAX PEMBERTON: Heart disease is the hidden killer of women over 40. It's alarming doctors often miss these symptoms. It kills twice as many women as breast cancer. But you can improve your odds

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

As a champion of women’s fitness and wellbeing, Davina McCall has long been a trusted voice in highlighting important health issues faced by women in mid-life and beyond.

Not only has she been influential in urging women to take the menopause seriously but she’s also been remarkably candid about her brain tumour and breast cancer treatments – speaking out in the hope her experience would help others.

So her disclosure last week, in her podcast Begin Again, that she also has cardiovascular disease, was typically brave as well as important.

Davina, 59, said she’d had high cholesterol for ‘quite a long time’, emphasising that heart disease is rife in women after the menopause – yet it’s an issue that is often overlooked.

Cardiovascular disease, or CVD, is an umbrella term for conditions affecting the heart and blood vessels.

The four main types are coronary heart disease (where the arteries supplying the heart become narrowed by fatty deposits), stroke, peripheral arterial disease (which affects the arteries to the legs) and diseases of the aorta, the body’s main artery. Between them they cause about a quarter of all deaths in the UK, roughly 170,000 a year, which is one every three minutes.

More than eight million Britons are living with CVD, and over four million of them are women. Yet ask most women what they fear most and they’ll say breast cancer.

Davina McCall is a trusted voice in highlighting important health issues faced by women in mid-life

In fact, coronary heart disease kills more than twice as many women as breast cancer in this country. We still think of the heart attack as a man’s problem – the overweight businessman clutching his chest – and that misconception is costing lives. Research funded by the British Heart Foundation found that women are 50 per cent more likely than men to be given the wrong initial diagnosis when they have a heart attack, and women often wait longer before getting help.

And midlife is when the risk for women changes dramatically. Before the menopause, oestrogen offers a degree of protection, helping to keep blood vessels supple and cholesterol in check. As it falls, that protection goes. A large American study that tracked women through the menopause found that levels of LDL, the so-called bad cholesterol, rise sharply in the year either side of a woman’s final period.

The cruel thing about CVD is that its early stages are usually silent. High blood pressure and high cholesterol cause no symptoms at all, which is why CVD is often only discovered after something has gone wrong.

Chest pain or tightness remain the most common signs of a heart attack, but these may be accompanied by, or overshadowed by, breathlessness, nausea, sweating, light-headedness, or pain spreading to the arm, jaw, neck or back.

Angina, the chest discomfort that comes on with exertion and eases with rest, is another warning the arteries are narrowing, as is getting unusually breathless or exhausted doing things that were once easy.

A woman in her fifties was referred to me because her panic attacks weren’t responding to treatment. She described a tight chest and breathlessness. But when I asked her more, it became clear that the episodes came on when she climbed the stairs or hurried for a bus and eased within a few minutes of stopping. That’s the classic pattern of angina.

I wrote to her GP asking for an urgent referral to the rapid access chest pain clinic, and a scan showed narrowing of the arteries supplying her heart.

The consequences of ignoring CVD are severe.

Untreated, it can lead to heart attack, stroke, heart failure and vascular dementia. But the good news is that most of the risk is modifiable, and the earlier you act the bigger difference it makes.

The good news about cardiovascular disease, says Dr Max, is that it's preventable

Start with food. A Mediterranean style of eating – with plenty of vegetables, pulses, wholegrains, oily fish, olive oil, nuts and seeds – has good evidence behind it.

Cut back on saturated fat from processed meat, pastries and cheese, keep salt down, and get plenty of fibre, which helps lower cholesterol. Keep alcohol within the guideline of 14 units a week and, if you smoke, stop.

Aim for at least 150 minutes a week of moderate activity, the kind that leaves you a little out of breath, and add some strength work twice a week.

Look after your sleep, too, and keep an eye on your waistline, as fat around the middle is bad for the heart.

Then get your numbers checked. If you’re aged 40-74 and don’t already have a heart condition, you’re entitled to a free NHS Health Check every five years.

If you haven’t been invited, ask your GP surgery or pharmacy. Tell them if a close relative had heart disease or a stroke before the age of 60.

If your risk is 10 per cent or more, you’ll usually be offered a statin, and I’d urge women not to dismiss them out of hand because of scare stories online.

Above all, if you get chest pain or any other symptoms of a heart attack, call 999. Don’t wait to see if it passes, and don’t worry about making a fuss.

I hope Davina’s revelation gets more women in midlife to improve their heart health.

After my Channel 4 programme The Great ADHD Myth? this summer, and my article in these pages two weeks ago on the explosion in ADHD diagnoses, I braced myself for the usual backlash. Some were furious but I was also overwhelmed by letters and emails from readers and viewers who share my concerns.

So, I was pleased to see two of the most respected figures in the field add their voices last week. Writing in the British Medical Journal, Professor Dame Uta Frith, a pioneer in autism research, and the clinical psychologist Dr Naomi Fisher argue that private providers are helping to drive the surge.

‘Convincing more people they could be autistic or have ADHD is a marketing strategy to increase their customer base,’ they write. The Health Secretary Yvette Cooper has said all ADHD and autism assessments will be regulated by the Care Quality Commission.

Researchers at two British universities have found that the bacteria and chemicals in our digestive system could help us spot the early signs of dementia. They analysed stool samples from 35 older adults and those with cognitive impairment had a different mix of gut bacteria. The gut and the brain constantly ‘talk’ through the vagus nerve, the immune system and the chemicals gut bacteria produce. It’s another reason to eat a varied diet rich in fibre, which feeds the useful bacteria inside us.

Consultant neurologist Suzanne O’Sullivan asks an uncomfortable question in her excellent book: are we turning too many ordinary human struggles into medical conditions? Drawing on her own patients, she looks at the rise in diagnoses of everything from autism to long Covid and argues that a label can sometimes do more harm than good. It’s compassionate and thought-provoking, and a timely challenge to the idea that more diagnoses always means better care.

Updated - October 25, 2026 04:15 pm IST - Tehran

Iranian Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi. (Image for representational purpose only) | Photo Credit: AP

Iran's Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi said on Sunday (October 25, 2026) that a potential resumption of war with Israel and the United States would not change his country's positions.

The remarks come amid mounting U.S and Israeli threats of renewed attacks on Iran, as President Donald Trump said on Friday (October 23, 2026) that Washington "may do another little Epic Fury," referring to the U.S-Israeli campaign on Iran that began on February 28.

"Recently, there has been talk of a new war, but I am already thinking about the post-war period. Whether there is war or not, our policies remain the same," said Mr. Araghchi during a televised event in Tehran.

"They have already done so twice and have seen the results. If they want to try again, they know they will achieve no different outcome from those of the past," he added.

An April 8 ceasefire between Iran and the United States put an end to the most intense fighting, apart from sporadic exchanges of fire in and around the strategic Strait of Hormuz, a key route for global energy supplies.

Iran has ‘lost complete control’ of Hormuz Strait: Rubio

Iran has detailed plans to hit Gulf undersea cables if war resumes, officials say

Iran says 7 oil tankers catch fire or damaged overnight in Strait of Hormuz

These military flare-ups have lulled in recent weeks.

Iran has claimed control over Strait of Hormuz since the outbreak of the war and has regularly targeted vessels that attempt crossing without its authorisation, moves strongly opposed by Washington and countries in the region.

In response, the U.S has been maintaining a naval counter-blockade on Iranian ports.

In June, Iran agreed with the United States to a memorandum of understanding laying out a pathway to a permanent settlement of the conflict, only for the deal to later unravel as each side accused the other of violating it.

Tehran has since called for a return to the accord to end the conflict.

"The current war has changed everything. We must accept that new realities are taking hold. Shipping routes through the Strait of Hormuz will no longer be the same, and the management of the strait will no longer be the same," said Mr. Araghchi.

"To achieve peace and stability, our region must move towards the complete absence of foreign forces," he added.

Updated - October 25, 2026 05:12 pm IST - Dhaka

Bangladesh has sought detailed information from Saudi Arabia about the execution of two of its citizens in the Makkah region, including the charges against them, court verdicts, manner of execution, whereabouts of their bodies and whether they had legal representation during their trials, according to a local media report.

The Bangladesh Consulate General in Jeddah sent a diplomatic letter to the Saudi Ministry of Foreign Affairs' branch in Makkah on October 19, seeking answers to six questions, according to a report by Bangladeshi newspaper Prothom Alo published on Saturday (October 24, 2026).

Bangladesh's Consul General in Jeddah also raised the matter with a senior Saudi foreign ministry official during a meeting the same day, the report said.

The two Bangladesh nationals, a man and a woman, were executed on October 14, after being convicted of committing an “indecent act” inside a mosque in Makkah.

The diplomatic letter seeks details of the offences, when and where they allegedly took place, the dates of arrest and certified copies of the court verdicts, the report said.

Also Read : Bangladesh urges India to expedite Sheikh Hasina’s extradition

It also asks whether the death sentences were carried out by beheading or another method, where the bodies are being kept and whether they will be repatriated to Bangladesh or have already been buried in Saudi Arabia.

Bangladesh has also sought information on whether lawyers represented the two during the investigation and trial and whether they had requested legal assistance, the report said.

According to Prothom Alo, Bangladeshi officials said the country's missions did not have detailed information about the proceedings leading to the executions, including whether the two had legal representation.

A list of Bangladeshi prisoners shared by Saudi authorities in September reportedly contained allegations of adultery and an information technology-related offence involving the two, but did not specify their sentences.

Dhaka is seeking clarification on the discrepancy between those entries and the Saudi Interior Ministry's public explanation of the executions.

Bangladesh had also raised the executions with the Saudi ambassador in Dhaka, according to the Dhaka Tribune.

Bangladeshi officials said the government had not received detailed official information from Saudi authorities before learning that the executions had been carried out, the newspaper reported last week.

Bangladesh has also sought information about 20 other citizens whose names appeared on the Saudi prisoner list as convicted but whose sentences were not specified, according to The Daily Star.

Saudi authorities had yet to respond to the consulate's latest diplomatic communication, Prothom Alo reported, citing Bangladeshi officials.

A Single Enzyme Could Hold the Key to Treating a Devastating Brain Disorder

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Blocking an enzyme’s production restored brain growth and healthy behaviors in mice engineered to develop Angelman syndrome, pointing to a possible treatment target.

Children with Angelman syndrome begin to show difficulties with thinking and movement at around 6 months of age. The rare condition occurs in approximately one in 20,000 births and is caused by the absence of a gene normally inherited from the mother. Researchers know which gene is missing, but they have struggled to explain why its absence causes these problems, making treatments elusive.

Research in mice now points to a chain of events that connects the missing gene to a stress response that interferes with brain growth. A team led by Jason Yi, PhD, an assistant professor of neuroscience at Washington University School of Medicine in St. Louis, traced that response to a buildup of a normally harmless enzyme. The study was published in Nature.

The missing gene, UBE3A, produces an enzyme called ubiquitin ligase, which helps keep cells orderly by marking unneeded proteins for disposal. It is active throughout the brain and participates in 60 different cellular processes. Identifying which of those processes contribute to Angelman syndrome required narrowing that broad field of possibilities.

Yi and his colleagues developed modified cells in the laboratory with UBE3A deactivated, then looked for proteins that accumulated without it. They identified 23 candidate proteins whose levels increased. One stood out for its strong connection to ubiquitin ligase’s normal housekeeping function: asparagine synthetase, an enzyme that helps cells produce essential proteins.

In mice genetically modified to develop Angelman syndrome, elevated levels of asparagine synthetase activated the brain’s stress response. Yet the enzyme does not interact with the usual triggers for that response, and its overabundance does not otherwise damage the cell. The consequences instead arise indirectly, as the stress response sharply reduces protein production and growth.

“The neurons are tricked into thinking that they’re in stress when they’re not — that’s bad because they will then not grow normally,” Yi said, noting that most children with Angelman syndrome have smaller brains than their peers of the same age. “It’s a reaction from the brain trying to conserve resources to protect itself. It does suggest that there is a dysregulation in the brain we might be able to correct.”

Blocking the production of asparagine synthetase restored normal brain growth and healthy behaviors in the mice. The team had corrected a later step in the biological sequence set off by the missing gene, allowing healthy development to resume.

The study is the first to link an increase in asparagine synthetase to a neurological condition. It adds to evidence that an imbalance in proteins can contribute to neurodevelopmental disorders by triggering stress responses, even when the proteins themselves are not directly damaging cells.

Yi said medications could potentially target the stress response triggered by asparagine synthetase, but much remains to be learned before work on a possible treatment can begin. His team is investigating precisely why the enzyme activates that response, where in the brain the response is most active, and when during development medical interventions would be most effective.

“We still don’t know a whole lot about the disease,” said Yi. “We haven’t been able to identify all of the things that the missing gene is doing and rescue the things that it is not doing in Angelman’s syndrome. But I think our data are the most complete so far to begin answering those questions.”

Reference: “Persistent neuronal stress signalling underlies Angelman syndrome phenotypes” by Kwang-Soo Kim, Cory Gardner, Alissa Cullen, Jalin A. Stelzer, Kevin Cho, Ashish Sharma, Mati T. Nemera, Travis Law, Huma Naz, Guoyan Zhao, Harrison W. Gabel, Gary J. Patti, Erik S. Musiek, Tae-Hyuk Ahn and Jason J. Yi, 21 October 2026, Nature. DOI: 10.1038/s41586-026-11165-4

Support for this work came from the Simons Foundation (Bridge to Independence award #387973), the Whitehall Foundation, the Hope Center for Neurological Disorders at Washington University (Pilot Award), and the National Institute of Mental Health (R01MH122786 and R01MH144153).

Updated - October 25, 2026 04:40 pm IST - Riyadh

Aerial view photo of Riyadh International Airport (RUH) in Saudi Arabia. File | Photo Credit: Getty Images/iStockphoto

Explosions rang out in the Saudi capital on Sunday (October 25, 2026), as the kingdom was due to reopen Riyadh international airport despite repeated attacks by Yemen's Houthis.

Saudi Arabia, which leads a military coalition fighting the Iran-backed Houthis, has faced a barrage of strikes from the group in recent weeks.

The fighting erupted after a years-long truce between the Houthis and Yemen's Riyadh-backed government fell apart, and has shifted the focus of the wider U.S.-Iran war.

Riyadh's international airport said flights would resume on Sunday (October 25, 2026) after a two-week closure following the Houthi strikes.

However, it remained unclear to what extent the airport was operational.

The Houthi fighters have carried out waves of strikes on the airport, including an attack this month that killed 13 people and wounded more than 300, the deadliest in the kingdom in more than a decade.

Houthi military spokesman Yahya Saree warned that the facility was still "unsafe" and a "military target".

"The Yemeni Armed Forces warn all international, regional, and local airlines — as well as staff and travellers — against using King Khalid Airport in Riyadh, as it is unsafe and serves as a military target for our forces," he said.

"The Saudi regime shall bear the consequences of any operations at the airport."

An AFP journalist in the city heard three explosions in the early hours of Sunday (October 25, 2026), with residents receiving alert messages for a "hostile aerial threat" urging them to stay in a safe place.

The warning came as the Saudi-led coalition fighting the Houthis said it had downed a ballistic missile launched by the group towards Riyadh, as well as two drones in the capital area.

"Air defences intercepted and destroyed a ballistic missile launched by the terrorist Houthi militia toward Riyadh," the coalition posted on X.

Later Sunday (October 25, 2026) morning, the coalition said its forces intercepted a cruise missile fired by the Houthis at the kingdom's southern city of Khamis Mushait.

Attacks appeared to target areas across the south, with warnings also issued for Abha city and Namas Governorate.

"The bombardment has been very heavy since this morning — may God keep us safe. Everything is shaking because of the explosions," one resident in Khamis Mushait told AFP on Sunday on condition of anonymity.

The attacks came after the Houthi fighters said that — in the space of 24 hours — they targeted three Saudi airports, including in Riyadh, energy installations, a pipeline and an air base.

The Saudi civil defence agency said it had also responded to falling debris in the Mecca and Medina regions on Saturday (October 24, 2026) after intercepting two Houthi missiles.

The Houthis denied targeting the cities, the two holiest in Islam, calling the Saudi claim a "huge lie".

The Houthis also fired at the southern Yemeni city of Aden, the interim seat of the Saudi-backed government, saying they targeted a naval base and military camp overseen by Saudi forces.

The flare-up in Yemen, with the Houthis taking control of the entire Red Sea coast in a lightning offensive last month, has further destabilised the region and disrupted oil markets.

The Houthi attacks have dealt a blow to Saudi Arabia, the world's biggest exporter of crude, disrupting businesses, airlines and residents, and rattling its drive to shift its international image.

Published - October 25, 2026 02:59 pm IST

Saudi Arabia / Iran / Yemen / act of terror / war / unrest, conflicts and war / West Asia

Michigan Gov. Gretchen Whitmer says America is ready to elect a female president

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Michigan Gov. Gretchen Whitmer pushed back Sunday on comments made by former first lady Michelle Obama last year that U.S. voters aren’t ready to elect a female president.

“Listen, I have got a lot of colleagues who I think are probably interested in running for president, and I’ve heard everyone say, ‘You can’t have this person or that person,’” the governor told NBC News’ “Meet the Press.”

“I would put my money on any Democratic governor in the country against [Vice President] JD Vance or [Secretary of State] Marco Rubio in 2028,” she added, pointing to two Republicans widely expected to run for president in two years.

Asked directly whether a woman could be the best choice to beat a potential GOP presidential nominee in 2028, Whitmer simply said, “I do.”

In 2025, Obama said in public remarks: “As we saw in this past election, sadly, we ain’t ready. That’s why I’m like, don’t even look at me about running, because you all are lying. You’re not ready for a woman. You are not.”

Several women, including Whitmer and former Vice President Kamala Harris, are widely speculated to be potential candidates in the 2028 Democratic presidential primary.

On Sunday, Whitmer did not deny that she’s exploring a potential run for president in 2028, saying that she’ll decide on her next steps after she departs from her post as governor in January.

“When I exit office … I’m going to take a little time off and figure out what I want to do next, so I’ve got nothing new to share with you, unfortunately,” Whitmer told “Meet the Press” moderator Kristen Welker.

Earlier this year, Whitmer denied that she planned to run for president but quickly walked back the comments.

“There will be a robust group of people running for president,” she said at the time, adding, “I will not be one of them in 2028, I can tell you that.”

Later the same day, the governor said: “I never thought I would run for governor. So I guess I should know better to never say never.”

Several other Democratic governors, including California Gov. Gavin Newsom, Illinois Gov. JB Pritzker, Maryland Gov. Wes Moore and Pennsylvania Gov. Josh Shapiro, are also widely regarded as potential 2028 presidential candidates. Each has been crisscrossing the country ahead of the midterms campaigning on behalf of Democratic gubernatorial candidates in competitive races.

Whitmer on Sunday highlighted her support for former public health official Amy Acton, the Democratic nominee for governor in Ohio.

Earlier this week, Acton’s opponent, GOP businessman Vivek Ramaswamy, released a TV ad featuring Acton’s mother calling her a “liar.” Acton has publicly shared that she’s been estranged from her mother for nearly 50 years and has accused her late stepfather of sexually abusing her. Acton and her brother were removed from her mother’s custody in 1978 after her stepfather, Cledith Arthur, was arrested on a child rape charge. Acton’s mother has not responded to requests for comment.

“I’m going to stay focused on getting people like Amy Acton over the finish line. What’s happened to her in Ohio is absolutely abhorrent, and any survivor knows there’s always a backlash. But this is what makes it harder for women to seek help after they’ve been sexually assaulted in this country,” Whitmer said Sunday. “It is unreal what’s going on, and that’s why we’ve got to — we’ve got to win races like this and elect good people who are going to keep the people at the heart of their work, like Dr. Amy Acton.”

Ramaswamy’s decision to air the ad has faced widespread bipartisan condemnation, some of it from GOP Sen. Jon Husted of Ohio, who is locked in a tight re-election race.

Alexandra Marquez is a politics reporter for NBC News.

Updated - October 25, 2026 02:46 pm IST - Islamabad

Pakistan reopened a key border crossing with Iran to resume bilateral trade and cross-border movement after a week-long suspension that stranded freight vehicles, disrupted supplies and aggravated concerns over LPG shortage ahead of winter.

Trade operations resumed on Saturday (October 24, 2026) at the Gabd-Rimdan crossing in Balochistan's Gwadar district, with border officials allowing commercial consignments accompanied by consignment notes dated October 16 or earlier to cross, the Dawn newspaper reported.

Mir Naveed Jan Kalmati, president of the Gwadar Chamber of Commerce and Industry, confirmed that the crossing, also known as Gate 250, has reopened.

"Pakistan has opened the border with Iran at Gabd-Rimdan... and allowed trucks and other vehicles to move toward Iran and Pakistan with import and export goods,” Mr. Kalmati said.

Welcoming the decision, he said the resumption of cross-border trade will bring immediate relief to traders and transporters stranded on both sides. He said the restored supply chain is expected to ease a nationwide LPG shortage and reduce soaring energy and food prices across Pakistan.

The reopening of the border comes days after Pakistan Army chief Field Marshal Asim Munir's visit to Iran earlier this week, during which he held talks with the Iranian leadership on bilateral relations and regional issues.

Pakistan's Commerce Ministry had closed the border and stopped cross-border movement on October 17. Though the Ministry did not specify a reason for the decision, the suspension was believed to be linked to security concerns.

The closure of the border had affected LPG supply in Pakistan, the demand for which rises with the onset of winter.

The suspension was followed by an attack on freight vehicles stranded near the Gabd-Rimdan crossing. On Thursday (October 22, 2026), unidentified gunmen targeted the parked trucks, setting dozens ablaze and damaging several others.

The reopening on Saturday (October 24, 2026) followed a review of the security situation by local administrators, who deployed additional forces to protect trade convoys.

Pakistan and Iran share a 909-km border that separates Iran's Sistan and Baluchestan province from Pakistan's Balochistan province. The border crossings serve as important routes for bilateral commerce and the movement of goods in the region.

The advisory comes amid escalating tensions in the region due to a series of drone and missile attacks targeting civilian infrastructure by Yemen’s Houthis. File. | Photo Credit: Reuters

India on Sunday (October 25, 2026) advised its nationals in Saudi Arabia to abide by local rules and regulations and refrain from filming or publishing information related to missile and drone interceptions.

The advisory comes amid escalating tensions in the region due to a series of drone and missile attacks targeting civilian infrastructure by Yemen’s Houthis.

"The Indian community residing in Saudi Arabia is requested to abide by local rules and regulations," the Indian Embassy said on social media while reposting the notification issued by the Directorate of Saudi Civil Defence.

"Filming, publishing, or sharing information related to countering missiles and drones and their impact sites exposes you to legal liability," the notification said.

The Indian mission also shared an update about the suspension of flights from Air India and Air India Express to and from Riyadh and Dammam.

On Saturday (October 24, 2026), the Consulate General of India in Jeddah said its 24×7 Control Rooms in Jeddah and Makkah continued to provide necessary assistance to affected Indians, with support from community associations and volunteers.

The assistance includes food, essential medicines and other immediate requirements wherever needed and feasible, it said.

The mission also said it remained in regular contact with Saudi Arabia's Ministry of Hajj and Umrah and Ministry of Foreign Affairs to address difficulties caused by the flight disruptions, particularly issues concerning visa validity and delayed departures.

Meanwhile, King Khalid International Airport in Riyadh is preparing to resume domestic and international flight operations on Sunday, the Gulf News web portal reported.

It also quoted the Saudi-led coalition fighting Yemen’s Houthis as saying on Sunday that air defences had intercepted and destroyed a ballistic missile launched towards Riyadh.

Just Two Days on a Vegan Diet Triggered Unexpected Changes in the Liver

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Switching from a vegetarian to a vegan diet altered liver metabolism in mice within just two days, making glucose production and toxic waste removal less efficient.

Millions of people follow a vegan diet, excluding all animal-derived products. Plant-based eating is recommended in part for its environmental benefits, but the health consequences of getting all dietary protein from plants are not yet fully understood. For researchers, that raises a question about how the body responds when the source of its protein changes.

Scientists at the University of Geneva (UNIGE) investigated that question in mice, focusing on the liver, which helps maintain blood sugar and processes the components of dietary protein. Their study, published in Molecular Metabolism, compared a lacto-vegetarian diet containing both animal and plant proteins with a vegan diet.

“In just two days, the mice fed an exclusively vegan diet showed less efficient glucose production,” explains Pierre Maechler, full professor in the Department of Cell Physiology and Metabolism and at the Diabetes Center of the UNIGE Faculty of Medicine. “Furthermore, their livers were less effective at eliminating ammonia, a toxic substance released during the breakdown of amino acids. A significant reduction in physical activity also suggests effects on the body as a whole.”

Proteins from plants and animals consist of amino acids, small molecules joined together like links in a chain. Their types and proportions differ depending on the food source, so changing protein sources gives the body a different mixture to process. Plant proteins naturally contain fewer branched-chain amino acids than animal proteins. Along with helping build the body’s proteins, these amino acids supply some of the raw material the liver uses to produce glucose and maintain sufficient blood sugar during fasting.

“Overall, a change in diet alters liver metabolism very quickly, but this effect is just as rapidly reversible,” emphasizes Maechler. “The body adapts within one to two days to even subtle changes in dietary composition and returns to normal upon the reintroduction of animal proteins.”

The combination of less efficient glucose production and ammonia removal pointed the team toward a liver enzyme called glutamate dehydrogenase, or GDH. Located in mitochondria, the structures inside cells that help process fuel, GDH connects protein metabolism with sugar metabolism. To investigate its role, the researchers compared two groups of mice lacking the enzyme in their livers. One group ate a lacto-vegetarian diet, while the other ate a strict vegan diet.

Without GDH, the mice eating only plant proteins developed a severe buildup of ammonia and impaired blood glucose regulation compared with mice whose diet also included animal proteins. This confirmed the enzyme’s essential role in the metabolic response to an exclusively plant-based diet.

The experiments were conducted in mice, so they do not establish that people would experience the same changes. They do, however, provide a way to investigate the physiological questions that accompany a change in protein source.

“In addition to the ethical and environmental considerations that often motivate a switch to a plant-based diet, there are also scientific factors relating to the physiological functioning of our metabolism that are worth being aware of,” notes Maechler.

For people following a strict vegan diet, the researchers emphasize choosing protein sources rich in branched-chain amino acids, such as nuts, tofu, and lentils, to support the liver’s metabolic functions. They place that consideration alongside the need to ensure an adequate intake of specific vitamins.

Reference: “Transition to dietary plant-based proteins requires functional liver GDH for proper control of ammonia and energy homeostasis” by Yan Zhou, Angela M. Ramos-Lobo, Tingting Fu, Hector Gallart-Ayala, Julijana Ivanisevic and Pierre Maechler, 4 September 2026, Molecular Metabolism. DOI: 10.1016/j.molmet.2026.102435

The work was funded by the Swiss National Science Foundation (#310030_192486 to P.M.) and the canton of Geneva.

Trump admin. progressing toward treating millions of veterans with psychedelics

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Nearly one in three veterans who relies on the government for health care could benefit from taking psychedelic drugs like magic mushrooms or MDMA.

That's not the assessment of some hippie or fading 1960s rock star. It came from Andrew Gradison, a senior adviser at President Donald Trump's Veterans Health Administration. Gradison told a conference for psychedelics enthusiasts this month that he is eager to ramp up the VA's capacity to give the drugs to as many as 2.1 million veterans with mental illnesses like post-traumatic stress disorder or depression. Gradison said 6.4 million veterans regularly rely on the VA for care.

No government regulator has deemed psychedelics safe or effective for mental illness. Under former President Joe Biden, the Food and Drug Administration in 2024 resoundingly rejected a proposal to authorize MDMA — also known as ecstasy — combined with talk therapy, to treat people with PTSD.

But psychedelics are getting a second chance in the Trump administration. Trump in April issued an executive order to accelerate research, clinical trials and patient access to the drugs. Republicans in Congress are among psychedelics' biggest backers. And the constituency they are seeking to please, veterans, are disproportionately Republicans.

Veterans, frustrated with high suicide rates among their fellow ex-service members, are lobbying aggressively to see Trump follow through.

"We were able to roll out a vaccine for Covid because of President Trump's Warp Speed initiative," said Juliana Mercer, a Marine Corps vet who also spoke at the Horizons: Perspectives on Psychedelics conference in New York City. "Why aren't we warp-speeding this treatment to save our veteran lives? What's keeping us from doing that?"

Gradison said practical challenges, and the need to ensure safety, stand in the way. He said that "it'll be a challenge" for the VA "in the first year to treat four digits worth of patients" with an FDA-approved psychedelic drug.

"Our demand will exceed our ability to supply these therapies, no doubt," Gradison said.

Still, psychedelic enthusiasts can also point to some momentum coming out of the Trump administration. A week after Trump's April executive order, the FDA allowed fast-track application reviews for three drugmakers developing psychedelic treatments. One, Compass Pathways,is set to finish submitting an application to the agency this quarter for its synthetic formulation of psilocybin for treatment resistant depression. Psilocybin is better known as magic mushrooms by those who use it for fun, rather than for healing. Compass anticipates an approval during the first half of 2027.

The psychedelic movement's excitement over the federal action dominated discussions at the three-day New York conference, which drew attendance from federal officials, top executives at psychedelic-focused drug companies, biotech investors, clinicians and mental health advocates.

"We stand here on a new day and a 180 degrees different trajectory than what 2024 brought to us," W. Bryan Hubbard, CEO of the psychedelic medicine advocacy group Americans for Ibogaine, said while speaking on Oct. 17, earning applause from the audience. Ibogaine, a psychedelic drug derived from an African shrub widely available in Mexico, has drawn interest from veterans for treating conditions like PTSD.

Trump's psychedelics push has even been embraced by former skeptics of the drugs. Nora Volkow, director of the National Institute on Drug Abuse, told senators in 2024 she thought psychedelic medicine backers were getting ahead of the evidence. "When people get excited, they want to start believing a little bit in fairy tales," she said. However, she stood in the Oval Office with Trump in April as he signed his order, applauding the effort. She's also spoken positively about the drugs in recent interviews.

Though psychedelics have yet to meet government regulatory standards, advocates of their approval often point to anecdotes about veterans' experiences receiving psychedelic therapy in Mexico and promising clinical trial data as reasons to move forward.

Glenn H. Curtis, a retired Army major general, described the impact of taking ibogaine last year in Mexico as having a "miraculous" effect on his PTSD and traumatic brain injuries.

"Those same people that had issues coming home from deployments, even like myself, 20 years later, still have issues today that they have not received any kind of treatment [for]," said Curtis, who is a volunteer for Americans for Ibogaine.

Veterans have multiple champions in Washington for their cause. Sen. Tim Sheehy (R-Mont.), a former Navy SEAL who served in combat in Iraq and Afghanistan, has offered his support,sponsoring legislation to establish an office within the VA dedicated to helping the agency prepare for psychedelic drug approvals and integrate the therapies into patient care. Reps. Lou Correa (D-Calif.) and Jack Bergman (R-Mich.), a former Marine Corps general, co-chair the Psychedelics Advancing Therapies Caucus, which recently released a suggested framework for implementing FDA-approved psychedelic therapies into the U.S. health care system.

At the same time, psychedelic enthusiasts are set to lose two prominent advocates on Capitol Hill. Texas GOP Rep. Dan Crenshaw, a former SEAL ousted by a primary challenger in March, led a successful push in 2023 to pass legislation that funded federal clinical trials on psychedelic therapies for active-duty military members. Another Texas Republican, Rep. Morgan Luttrell, a former SEAL who sought ibogaine treatment in Mexico for a traumatic brain injury he incurred during training, is retiring from Congress in January after deciding not to seek reelection.

"Executive orders have a tendency to go away as the tides ebb and flow, and I would just hate for something like this to be removed," Luttrell told POLITICO, emphasizing his desire for Congress to codify Trump's executive order.

Veterans are a reliable Republican voting bloc, with 61 percent voting for Trump over Kamala Harris in 2024.

The VA has led the push in Washington to advance access to psychedelic therapy. It's involved in 20 psychedelic clinical trials and was one of the first major health care systems to roll out Spravato, an FDA-approved treatment for depression using esketamine, a chemical component found in ketamine. Ketamine — an anesthetic frequently referred to as a psychedelic due to its psychoactive effects — is currently the only psychedelic-like drug approved for medical use.

Trump administration officials say they hope more will be approved soon.

"We want to see the drug approved if it's safe and effective, and the reason why is because there's a mental health crisis," Matt Zorn, deputy general counsel at the Health and Human Services Department, told POLITICO.

Despite the federal government and advocacy groups' ongoing efforts to support the veteran community, mental health issues among the population have long persisted. The VA has cited research finding that 23% of veterans that receive care through the VA had PTSD at some point in their lives, compared to 7% of veterans who don't seek care through the VA. The agency has pointed to war zone deployment, training accidents and military sexual trauma as some of the main situations that could lead to a military service member developing PTSD.

U.S. veterans take their own lives at a rate roughly 57% higher than the general adult population, with an average of nearly 18 veterans dying by suicide every day.

While psychedelic enthusiasts feel closer than ever to scoring an FDA approval, cracks within the coalition have widened over what treatment should look like. Drugmakers and biotech investors have financially supported a drug-first model but some clinicians, mainly psychotherapists, and lawmakers argue that psychedelics are safer and more effective for treating mental health conditions when paired with psychotherapy.

"This is not dropping a pill and saying 'I'm going to be cured tomorrow,'" Correa said during a press briefing in September. "There's pre-preparation, psychiatric, psychological counseling, the actual treatment and then post-treatment psychological, psychiatric treatment in order to be very effective, most effective."

This tension came to a head with the FDA's 2024 rejection of a MDMA-assisted therapy application from Resilient Pharmaceuticals, then known as Lykos Therapeutics. Among the agency's chief concerns was that regulators struggled to determine how much of the clinical improvement came from the effects of MDMA versus the intensive talk therapy component of the treatment. The agency also cited concerns about missing safety data, trial manipulation accusations and the potential risk for misconduct among therapy practitioners.

But Compass' psychedelic therapy doesn't include a talk therapy component, allowing the FDA to more easily evaluate the efficacy of the drug.

"Patients are in need, and this is a class that's been well studied. We're making sure that all standards are met so that patients can receive treatment safely," Lori Englebert, Compass' chief commercial officer, told POLITICO in a recent interview.

Pope Leo calls for Yemen violence to end, in address two weeks after surgery

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Sign up now: Get ST's newsletters delivered to your inbox

Pope Leo waved and smiled to the crowds after reaching the window unaided.

VATICAN CITY – Pope Leo called for an end to the violence in Yemen on Oct 25, as he addressed the faithful from the window over St Peter’s Square less than two weeks after having surgery for lung cancer.

The 71-year-old pontiff said he was particularly concerned about the humanitarian situation in the south-western Yemeni city of Taiz, “where the population suffers due to the lack of food, water and medical assistance”.

He waved and smiled to the crowds after reaching the window unaided – the same spot where he announced on Oct 11 that he would have an operation to remove a small growth on his lung, in the first health scare of his papacy.

“I'm making a heartfelt appeal for the hostilities to stop so that the people in Yemen and across the entire region can live in peace,” he said in a very firm and clear voice.

The Pope said he was also praying for those who had been killed and wounded in the “terrorist attack against Orthodox faithful” at the Hamoud church in Jordan on Oct 23.

“I'm close to that community and the entire country, which is known for the peaceful and respectful coexistence of different religions,” the pontiff said.

Pope Leo urged everyone to accompany with prayers a meeting of religious leaders to promote peace that will take place in the Italian city of Assisi on Oct 27.

He had been due to attend that event, but cancelled his visit on the advice of doctors to aid his recovery, the Vatican said on Oct 23.

The Pope had the lower lobe of his right lung removed in surgery on Oct 13 after being diagnosed with adenocarcinoma, the most common form of lung cancer.

Last week, doctors said he would not need any further therapy, indicating that his tumour had not spread. REUTERS

Serbia enters into elections amid ascending students’ movement against SNS

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Gender Agenda Stories from beyond the binary.

The Hindu On Books Books of the week, reviews, excerpts, new titles and features.

Published - October 25, 2026 01:19 pm IST - Belgrade

Supporters of the electoral list "Student List - Students Win" attend a final campaign rally at Slavija Square in Belgrade, Serbia. | Photo Credit: Reuters

Serbia votes on Sunday (October 25, 2026) in what is set to be its most fiercely contested parliamentary election in more than 14 years, as a student-led movement seeks to oust the party of veteran leader Aleksandar Vucic.

The snap vote is the culmination of nearly two years of student-led protests sparked by the 2024 collapse of a canopy at Novi Sad railway station that killed 16 people, a tragedy widely blamed on corruption.

The disaster triggered some of Serbia's largest protests since the fall of strongman Slobodan Milosevic in 2000.

The Students' List, a selection of candidates backed by the largely leaderless movement, now poses the biggest challenge to Mr. Vucic's right-wing populist Serbian Progressive Party (SNS), which has dominated politics throughout its tenure.

Eighteen electoral lists are vying for seats in Serbia's 250-member Parliament, but polls suggest the contest is largely a choice between the ruling party and the student-backed list.

Several leading opposition parties have opted not to run, instead urging supporters to back the students.

An unusually high turnout is expected among Serbia's 6.4 million registered voters.

In the decades since Milosevic fell, when around 70% cast ballots to oust him, parliamentary turnout has declined, averaging just over 55% during SNS rule.

For nearly two years, students have staged rallies and marched across the Balkan nation to reach voters directly and counter the SNS's media machine.

Their protests have regularly drawn huge crowds, with a final pre-election rally in Belgrade on Thursday (October 22, 2026) drawing around 1,50,000 people to the streets, according to an independent protest monitor.

Serbian students step up crusade against corruption

Serbia's protesting students rally to mark 7 months since train station tragedy

Thousands rally in Belgrade against crackdown on student-led protests

Mr. Vucic has also campaigned nationwide, embracing citizens in villages and towns, while repeatedly accusing the student movement of plotting a foreign-funded coup.

"Let us never forget how they tried to bring down our State. Let us always protect and defend it," he told supporters at his party's final rally in Belgrade.

Most of the Students' List's 250 candidates are experts from fields including academia and the arts, with little political experience.

The movement has a broad platform, centred on a pledge to fight corruption.

It evolved from demands for a transparent investigation into the Novi Sad tragedy into calls for an early election.

Against them is Mr. Vucic, the face of his party, who has tried to cultivate an image as an indispensable statesman, walking a diplomatic tightrope between the neighbouring European Union and his close ally Russia.

Amid mounting pressure, the 56-year-old called the snap vote last month, citing "major turbulence on the domestic political scene".

The right-wing populist leader cut short his second presidential term to run for Prime Minister.

Days before the vote, Serbian civil society groups warned that conditions for "free and fair elections" were lacking.

"This assessment is not based on isolated incidents, opposition claims or political disagreement with the government," a joint statement by 24 organisations said.

"It is supported by documented findings from independent Serbian organisations working in different fields."

Domestic and international observers will monitor the vote, with students mobilising large numbers of volunteers to oversee the process.

Polls in Serbia will open at 7:00 am (0600 GMT) and close at 8:00 pm.

Electoral authorities have ordered overseas polling to follow Serbian voting hours, forcing some voters to vote in the early hours.

In Chicago, home to one of the largest Serbian communities in the United States, polls will open at 1:00 am local time, prompting voters to announce "pyjama voting parties" on social media.

But many overseas voters face a bigger hurdle, with a limited number of polling stations forcing some to travel hundreds or even thousands of kilometres to cast their ballots.
