Genetic test may spare younger women chemotherapy for breast cancer

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Published: 13:09 GMT, 25 October 2026 | Updated: 19:52 GMT, 25 October 2026

The NHS could save £4m a year by offering women with low-risk breast cancer a genetic test that could allow them to avoid toxic and unnecessary chemotherapy.

The test - called Prosigna - is currently only approved for use in post-menopausal women whose cancer has spread to no more than three nearby lymph nodes.

But experts at University College London say offering the test more widely, to women as young as 40, could save the NHS £654 per patient.

It could also spare more than 4,000 women a year the side effects of chemotherapy, which can include nausea, vomiting, hair loss and an increased risk of infection.

Presenting the findings at the annual meeting of the European Society of Medical Oncology in Madrid, Dr Rob Stein said: 'Prosigna testing not only allows about two-thirds of patients with this type of breast cancer to safely avoid chemotherapy, but doing so also saves the NHS money.'

The current study focused on the first two years after diagnosis, but experts predict the savings would be even greater in the longer term.

Breast cancer is the most common cancer in women worldwide, with around 60,000 women diagnosed with invasive breast cancer each year in the UK alone.

Around 80 per cent of these are oestrogen-receptor positive cancers, meaning they are fuelled by the female sex hormone oestrogen and can be treated with hormone-blocking drugs alone.

Making genetic testing more widely available could save the NHS £4m a year - and spare thousands of women the debilitating side effects of chemotherapy

Many of these patients are also offered chemotherapy, as it can help lower the risk of the disease returning.

However, there is growing concern that many women gain little or no benefit from the treatment, while still suffering its debilitating and sometimes dangerous side effects.

Now experts at UCL say Prosigna could help address this dilemma.

The trial - known as OPTIMA - saw 4,429 women aged 40 and over whose cancer had spread to up to nine lymph nodes or who had a tumour measuring 30mm or more given the test.

The test analyses the activity of 50 genes involved in breast cancer growth, taken from the tumour site during standard surgery or biopsy.

A score is then generated based on the risk of the cancer spreading - with low scores indicating that a patient can be safely treated with hormone therapy alone.

Results showed that 67 per cent of patients tested did not need chemotherapy.

According to UCL analysis, this equates to around 4,200 patients a year.

The trial also showed that in the two years following a diagnosis, chemotherapy costs the NHS around £5,500 per patient - when it's given as standard treatment.

That figure covers the cost of the drug itself, along with nursing care, time spent in hospital, medicines to manage side effects and any resulting hospital admissions.

Using the Prosigna test cut those costs by £3,611 per patient - analysis carried out by Professor Peter Hall, from the University of Edinburgh, found.

In the UK, the test is estimated to cost the NHS around £1,955 per patient.

After considering other factors including any additional costs that might have been incurred from patients who skip chemotherapy to start other treatments sooner, Prof Hall calculated that the NHS could save around £654 per patient, by offering the Prosigna test within the first two years of being diagnosed.

Over the course of a year, if the NHS gave the test to all women who met the trial criteria, the health service could save around £4 million.

Squash is my go-to winter vegetable... my six favorites that ease digestion, keep you full and ward off the flu: JOHANNAH KATZ

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Published: 13:08 GMT, 25 October 2026 | Updated: 13:47 GMT, 25 October 2026

When it comes to the healthiest winter squash, you might expect butternut or pumpkin to take the top spot.

But many of their often-overlooked cousins have a surprisingly impressive nutritional profile.

From bright orange butternut to pasta-like spaghetti squash, winter squashes offer a wide variety of vitamins, minerals, fiber and healthy plant compounds.

And as flu season gets underway, they may even have protective antioxidants that could ward off pesky winter bugs.

As a registered dietitian, I'm constantly recommending my patients add more squash to their sheet-pan dinners, curries, bowls and pastas.

While there is no single healthiest squash for everyone, certain varieties stand out for my clients and on my grocery list.

Below, I have ranked six of my favorites based on their nutritional strengths, versatility and benefits for different health goals.

Squash is packed with vital nutrients that immune, heart and digestive health

Delicata squash earns its place on this list because sometimes the healthiest vegetable is the one you'll actually make and eat.

Unlike butternut squash, which usually needs peeling, delicata has thin, edible skin that softens when cooked. That means less prep time and less food waste.

Delicata also contains fiber and potassium, which support digestion, fullness and healthy muscle function. Its slightly sweet, nutty flavor also makes it particularly appealing to people who aren't usually excited about eating their vegetables.

My favorite way to eat it is simple: slice it into half moons, toss with olive oil and roast until golden.

Add to salads or serve alongside salmon or chicken - no peeling required.

Spaghetti squash can serve as a replacement for traditional pasta. Consider pairing it with lean protein for added balance

Spaghetti squash has a unique advantage: When cooked, its flesh separates into thin strands resembling noodles.

That makes it a practical option for people looking to cut calories or carbohydrates - or simply add more vegetables to comforting fall meals.

One cup of plain cooked spaghetti squash has about 42 calories, 10g of carbohydrates and 2g of fiber. For comparison, a cup of cooked traditional pasta generally contains around 200 calories and more than 40g of carbs.

Despite the name, spaghetti squash isn't nutritionally equivalent to pasta, so what you serve with it matters when rounding out a meal. Adding chicken, turkey, shrimp, tofu or beans can make it more balanced.

And you don’t have to give up pasta altogether. Mixing spaghetti squash with regular spaghetti is another easy way to increase your vegetable intake and get used to the differences in texture and taste.

I like to roast the squash halves until tender and then use a fork to separate the strands. Toss with marinara, sautéed vegetables and your favorite protein for a lighter take on spaghetti.

Sometimes called Japanese pumpkin, kabocha has a dense, creamy texture and a naturally sweet flavor similar to a sweet potato.

Its standout nutritional feature is its carotenoid content. These plant pigments, also found in carrots and sweet potatoes, include beta-carotene, which the body converts into vitamin A, lutein and zeaxanthin.

These nutrients are concentrated in the retina and help support low-light vision and protect against degeneration.

A recent study published in HortScience found that certain kabocha varieties contained substantial amounts of carotenoids, although exact amounts varied depending on the variety, maturity and storage conditions.

Kabocha also provides fiber and carbohydrates, making it a satisfying side dish, particularly when paired with a source of protein.

Consider cutting it into wedges and roasting with olive oil and a little salt. Like delicata, the skin also softens when cooked and can be eaten.

When shopping for canned pumpkin, choose plain varieties to avoid excess amounts of added sugar

Pumpkin might be the most recognizable winter squash, and it isn’t just good for making pies.

Like butternut squash, pumpkin gets its orange color from beta-carotene, which the body converts into vitamin A to support vision, as well as immune and skin health.

Vitamin A helps maintain protective mucosal barriers in the respiratory and digestive tracks, the body's first line of defense against the flu and common cold.

It also comes in a can, which can be far cheaper and more shelf stable than fresh produce.

One cup of plain canned pumpkin contains about 83 calories, 7g of fiber and 500mg of potassium. It also provides immune-boosting iron and vitamin C.

Unlike many winter squashes that require peeling, chopping and roasting, canned pumpkin is ready to use straight from the pantry and is incredibly versatile, making it great for both sweet and savory recipes.

Stir plain pumpkin puree into oatmeal, blend it into smoothies or add it to soups, chilis and sauces for extra fiber and nutrients. Just make sure to choose 100 percent pumpkin puree and not pumpkin pie filling to cut down on added sugar.

Butternut squash can easily be blended into a soup or cubed with olive oil, cinnamon and rosemary

Butternut squash is particularly rich in beta-carotene, the pigment responsible for its bright orange color.

One cup of baked butternut squash contains about 82 calories, nearly 7g of fiber and about 580mg of potassium.

Butternut squash also provides about 34 percent of the daily recommended value of vitamin C, which helps produce disease-fighting white blood cells and reduces inflammation during infections.

That's an impressive nutritional package for relatively few calories. The fiber supports digestion and fullness, and potassium plays an important role in maintaining healthy blood pressure.

Butternut squash has a naturally sweet, slightly nutty flavor, making it an appealing way to add more vegetables to meals, even for picky eaters.

Consider roasting cubed butternut squash with olive oil, cinnamon and rosemary, then add it to grain bowls or salads. It also blends nicely into creamy soups without needing much cream.

One cup of baked acorn squash contains more than twice the potassium of a medium banana, which supports heart health

Acorn squash takes my top spot thanks to its impressive fiber and potassium content, making it a standout choice for digestive and heart health.

One cup of baked acorn squash contains around 9g of fiber and nearly 900mg of potassium. For perspective, that's about one-third of the recommended 28g daily fiber intake and more than twice the amount of potassium in a medium banana.

Fiber supports digestion and regular bowel movements, helps lower cholesterol and promotes fullness, while potassium helps counteract sodium's effects on blood pressure.

Acorn squash also provides about 21 percent of the daily value for magnesium and 24 percent for vitamin B6 in just one cup. Both support important functions such as muscle health and energy metabolism.

Johannah Katz (pictured) is a registered dietitian who frequently recommends winter squash to her patients

Acorn squash contains more carbs than some other winter varieties, but those carbs come packaged with fiber and beneficial nutrients.

I like to slice acorn squash in half, scoop out the seeds and roast it until tender. Then, stuff the center with quinoa, lentils, vegetables or ground turkey for a balanced and delicious meal.

The answer depends on your nutritional goals and preferences.

Acorn squash takes the top spot for its high fiber and potassium content, while butternut stands out for vitamin A and other essential nutrients.

Kabocha offers an impressive range of carotenoids, and canned pumpkin is an affordable, convenient source of beta-carotene. For lighter, lower-carbohydrate meals, spaghetti squash is a great option, and delicata makes prep quick and easy.

Some winter squash varieties are particularly rich in potassium, so people with kidney disease who need to limit potassium should pay attention to portions.

Ultimately, I wouldn't choose just one favorite. Diversifying the winter squashes you eat is a great way to enjoy a wider range of nutrients, flavors and textures while making healthy eating more interesting and enjoyable.

Johannah Katz, MA, RD, is a registered dietitian based in Florida.

Smoke and flames rising from a fire at King Khalid International Airport in Riyadh, Saudi Arabia on October 9, 2026. | Photo Credit: AP

Riyadh's international airport said flights would resume on Sunday (October 25, 2026) after a two-week closure following strikes from Yemen's Houthis on the Saudi capital, though the Iran-backed group warned of more to come.

The Flight Radar monitoring site showed a plane taking off from Riyadh airport early Sunday (October 25, 2026), heading to the Saudi city of Jeddah.

Saudi Arabia, along with Yemen's internationally recognised government that it backs, has faced a wave of Houthi attacks in recent weeks after a years-long truce broke down in the summer.

The fighting has shifted the focus of the wider U.S-Iran war and deepened a humanitarian disaster crippling Yemen, one of the world's poorest countries.

"Please be advised that Terminal (1) will be designated for domestic flights and Terminal (2) for international flights operated by national carriers at King Khalid International Airport in Riyadh, effective Sunday, October 25, 2026," King Khalid International Airport posted on X on Saturday (October 24, 2026).

The Houthis have carried out waves of strikes on Riyadh's airport, including an attack this month that killed 13 people and wounded more than 300, the deadliest in the kingdom in more than a decade.

Houthi military spokesman Yahya Saree warned that the airport was still "unsafe" and a "military target".

"The Saudi regime shall bear the consequences of any operations at the airport,” he added.

Trump escalates rhetoric against Abdul El-Sayed as Michigan Senate race heats up

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

PORT HURON, Mich. (AP) — President Donald Trump intensified his anti-Muslim rhetoric against Michigan Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed on Sunday, portraying him as a threat to national security and questioning his loyalty to the United States in remarks that drew a sharp rebuke from a former top Arab American ally.

Trump called El-Sayed, a former Detroit health official, a "jihadist" who was "on the side of the terrorists" and would "have to go through a metal detector before he's allowed to go into the Senate." He also repeatedly invoked the Sept. 11, 2001, terrorist attacks, drawing a comparison between stopping El-Sayed from entering the Senate chamber and the passengers who fought back against the hijackers aboard United Flight 93.

El-Sayed, who would become the nation's first Muslim senator if elected, has called attacks on his loyalty to the country "shameful" and repeatedly denied any ties to extremist groups. The Republican president's remarks were condemned by Bishara Bahbah, a founder of Arab Americans for Trump, the group that helped lead the president's outreach to Arab American voters in 2024.

"The president's statements are out of line," he said in a phone interview. "They are crossing a red line. They are racist."

With Republicans on the defensive over inflation, soaring fuel prices and the Iran war, the president has been trying to generate turnout by claiming that El-Sayed and other Democrats are existential threats to America, instead of electoral rivals. Michigan's Senate race between El-Sayed and Republican Mike Rogers is among a handful of contests that could determine control of the chamber.

Trump made his remarks in Port Huron, a Canadian border city about an hour north of Detroit and near communities where he made significant gains among Arab American voters in 2024. He became the first Republican presidential candidate since 2000 to win Dearborn, a city with one of the nation's largest concentrations of Arab Americans.

Trump, who imposed a travel ban targeting several predominantly Muslim countries during his first term, has increasingly leaned into anti-Muslim rhetoric as Election Day approaches. He also attacked Democratic Rep. Ilhan Omar of Minnesota at Sunday's rally, where he declared that he had come to Michigan "to keep a jihadist Muslim Brotherhood member from becoming a United States senator."

Responding to Trump, who also regularly mocks his name, El-Sayed told supporters at a rally in Grand Rapids later Sunday that he doesn't care how Trump pronounces or misrepresents it.

"I don't even care that he remembers my whole name because he can just go on and call me 'senator' after November," El-Sayed said, drawing cheers. "I will be Senator Muhammad to you, bro."

Central to Republicans' strategy against El-Sayed in the final weeks of the campaign has been an effort to link him to the Muslim Brotherhood, an Islamic movement with branches that the U.S. government has designated as terrorist organizations.

There is no evidence that El-Sayed has any affiliation with the Muslim Brotherhood. His half-sister, Eman Abdelhadi, wrote on social media in 2012 that "my family has long been MB" and described a conversation with her mother about her involvement in the Muslim Brotherhood in the early 1970s.

El-Sayed was born in the United States to Egyptian immigrant parents who divorced when he was young. He was raised by his father, not his biological mother.

Trump on Sunday also targeted El-Sayed's ties to popular streamer Hasan Piker, who appeared alongside the Democrat multiple times during the primary campaign, including at his election night event.

Piker in the past has said that "Hamas is a thousand times better" than Israel, describing some Orthodox Jews as "inbred" and that "America deserved 9/11."

El-Sayed has faced criticism for his own rhetoric. He has repeatedly refused to answer questions about whether Iran or Israel poses a bigger threat to the country. Earlier this year, he said "hurt people hurt people" after an attack on a synagogue in the Detroit area. The attacker had lost four relatives in an Israeli airstrike on Lebanon the week earlier. El-Sayed later apologized, saying he was not trying to justify what happened.

"To anyone who feels like my comments might have been hurtful, I'm really sorry," he said. "That was not my intention."

Bahbah, whose organization has since changed its name to Arab Americans for Peace, said Trump's remarks were unacceptable to Arab and Muslim communities, including Christian Arabs.

"The remarks are out of line and crazy. I mean, no sane individual would say these things," Bahbah said.

Dearborn helped Trump win Michigan in 2024 after many voters in the city were outraged by the Biden administration's handling of the war in Gaza.

"The president of the United States saying that about a person because of his religion, that is just not acceptable," said Bahbah. "And people are going to remember that on Nov. 3."

Sen. Bernie Sanders, speaking at the Grand Rapids rally, said Trump and his allies were using Islamophobia because they have "nothing to say" about the issues most important to working people, such as health care, housing and income inequality.

"All they can do is foment hatred and divisiveness," Sanders said.

Rogers and El-Sayed have been locked in a bitter race to fill the seat of retiring Democratic Sen. Gary Peters.

During their second and final general election campaign debate, Rogers questioned El-Sayed's loyalty to the United States.

"Are you for America, or are you for these terrorist groups overseas?" Rogers asked.

El-Sayed said that questioning was "shameful."

"I'm proud of our Constitution that guarantees all of us the right to pray as we choose to pray," he said.

Speaking before Trump's appearance Sunday, Rogers repeated his allegations linking El-Sayed to the Muslim Brotherhood.

"The Muslim Brotherhood celebrated exactly one victory in the United States in the primary this year," Rogers said on stage. "Abdul El-Sayed's."

Trump has routinely announced new investments in factories at his campaign rallies, but he did not do so as part of his speech in Michigan, a state defined by the auto industry.

The president instead repeated investments already being made by domestic automakers. Those automakers could see their finances slump if the president expands his trade war with Canada, which has been an essential partner for the U.S. automotive sector.

While it lacked a fresh promise for Michigan's economy, Trump's rally did deliver a bit of star power.

Rapper Nicki Minaj appeared on stage and urged people to get out and vote for the state's Republican ticket. Her appearance followed an awkward moment from Trump, who has increasingly been fixated on commenting about other people's body parts.

After praising the body of Ohio Sen. Jon Husted, a Republican in a close election contest, Trump turned his eye toward Minaj and commented on her posterior.

The president then signaled for Minaj to stand for the crowd and she did, after which Trump said, "I hope Nicki wasn't insulted, but we love Nicki. She's been with us for a long time."

Cappelletti reported from Washington. Associated Press writer Josh Boak in Washington contributed to this report.

I'm a doctor. I ditched traditional health insurance for my family of 4, and I'm not looking back.

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

I've spent more than a decade navigating the labyrinth of American healthcare. I've worn the white coat, spent sleepless nights on call, signed the charts, and cared for thousands of patients.

A few years ago, I realized I couldn't keep participating in a system that felt like it was designed to fail the very people it was meant to serve.

Today, my family and I are uninsured in the traditional sense — and I'm confident that it's the best financial and medical decision I've ever made.

For years, I worked in a traditional, insurance-based primary care practice. My days were a blur of 15-minute appointments, dictated not by medical necessity, but by billing codes and insurance reimbursements. I felt completely beholden to the behemoth of corporate medicine. Patients were waiting months to see me and were almost always shortchanged on the time I was able to spend with them.

I felt like the insurance companies were the invisible third party in every exam room, deciding which tests I could order and which medications my patients deserved. I wasn't just a doctor anymore; I was a data-entry clerk for multibillion-dollar payers. The result was a total, soul-crushing burnout that followed me home every night.

On the other side of it, some of my patients were forgoing necessary care because the costs presented to them were simply too high. Did it make sense to spend $300 to check a thyroid test to ensure the $10 per month medication I prescribed was working as it was supposed to? Many patients, rightfully so, decided to spend their money elsewhere.

I knew I had to get out — not just for my own sanity, but to actually provide the type of care I believed in.

I eventually traded the treadmill for Direct Primary Care (DPC). In this model, my patients (or their employers or an insurance company) pay a flat monthly membership fee directly to me (around $100 per month). This type of care is affordable— my current patient panel includes attorneys, electricians, teachers, and retirees. There are no co-pays, no deductibles, and most importantly, no insurance companies breathing down my neck.

The difference was night and day. Without the administrative bloat of insurance billing, I finally became the doctor I trained to be:

Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.

Once I saw how much better medicine functioned without traditional insurance interference, I took the ultimate leap. I took my wife and children, now 9 and 6, off our health insurance plan.

Instead, we joined a medical cost-sharing community. It isn't insurance; it's a community of members who share each other's large, unexpected medical expenses. When I combined a DPC membership for our routine care with a health share for the big stuff, I realized I had found a better way to protect my family — and was paying far, far less for it.

To understand why we left, look at the cost of childbirth. When our first child was born under a supposedly high-end traditional insurance plan, we still walked away owing about $10,000 after paying our insurance premiums. We were paying twice: once for the premium and again for the actual care. My wife spent hours of her precious maternity leave arguing with insurance companies about the services we received in the hospital. When our infant son was hospitalized a few months later, she was back on the phone again, having to advocate for his coverage during the time of a family crisis. We spent years paying off the cost of the birth and his illness.

With our second child, we looked at the numbers through the lens of a cost-share program. On a plan like ours, the Initial Unshareable Amount (IUA) — the part we pay before the community kicks in — was only $1,500.

Beyond that $1,500, the community shared the rest. We didn't have to spend my wife's recovery time fighting with a claims adjuster or waiting for pre-authorizations. We knew exactly what our responsibility was, and more importantly, we knew our family was covered by a community, not a corporation.

As a physician, I've seen the insurance trap from both sides. We've been conditioned to believe that without a blue or green plastic card in our wallets, we are unsafe.

The reality is that for some, traditional insurance has become a barrier to care rather than a bridge. By opting out and pairing Direct Primary Care with a health share, I've reclaimed my time as a doctor and my peace of mind as a father. I'm no longer waiting for an insurance company to tell me how to care for my kids or my patients. As a doctor and as a father, for the first time in my career, I feel truly secure.

This article is not a substitute for professional medical advice, diagnosis, or treatment. Always consult your qualified physician or healthcare provider.

Just two drinks a day may be far riskier than you think

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Even alcohol consumption that many Americans would describe as moderate may carry significant health risks, including a greater likelihood of premature death, disability, cancer, and heart disease. That is the conclusion of a new study published in the Journal of Studies on Alcohol and Drugs, a journal published at Rutgers University.

The research, known as the Alcohol Intake and Health Study, was originally commissioned by the U.S. federal government to help shape updated national dietary recommendations. Researchers estimated that people who consume an average of 14 alcoholic drinks per week face a 1 in 25 lifetime risk of dying from alcohol-related causes. By comparison, consuming up to seven drinks weekly was linked to only small increases in risk for most health conditions.

"Even low levels of alcohol use come with health risks," says lead study author Kevin Shield, Ph.D., an associate professor at the University of Toronto and a senior scientist who leads the World Health Organization (WHO)/Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) Collaborating Centre in Addiction and Mental Health. "And that risk continues to increase the more someone drinks."

How Scientists Measured the Health Risks of Alcohol

To better understand the long-term consequences of drinking, Shield and his colleagues in the United States and Canada investigated how alcohol consumption over a lifetime contributes to disease and premature death among Americans.

Medical experts first examined more than 7,200 scientific publications covering illnesses and injuries associated with alcohol. Using this evidence, they estimated the risks for individual health conditions and combined those findings with large national health data sets. Statistical models then allowed the team to calculate how different levels of alcohol consumption affect the likelihood of developing serious health problems or dying prematurely.

The findings provide specific estimates that are missing from the latest U.S. Dietary Guidelines. The updated recommendations simply encourage Americans to "limit alcoholic beverages" without identifying a particular consumption threshold. Earlier versions advised men to drink no more than two alcoholic beverages per day and women no more than one.

"While the new U.S. Dietary Guidelines contain a useful 'less-is-best' message, they provide no quantitative framework. Our study was designed to do just that across the drinking spectrum," says study co-author Timothy Naimi, M.D., M.P.H., director of the University of Victoria's Canadian Institute for Substance Use Research and an adjunct professor at Boston University.

"It turns out that two drinks per day, which might be considered 'moderate' from a social standpoint, is associated with a substantially elevated risk of a premature death caused by alcohol," explains Naimi.

Moderate Drinking Offers No Significant Overall Health Benefits

The researchers looked beyond the risk of premature death to examine alcohol's connection to a wide range of immediate and long-term health problems. These included cancers (e.g., esophageal, oral, and breast), cardiovascular disease, liver disease, and injuries.

One of the study's most important findings challenges the longstanding belief that consuming small amounts of alcohol can improve health, particularly heart health.

"We did not observe a significant protective effect of alcohol on health at any level of consumption," says Shield. "At low levels, alcohol may be associated with a reduced risk of ischemic heart disease and stroke. But when you look across the full range of health outcomes, including cancer and other chronic diseases, those potential benefits are outweighed by the risks even at 7 drinks per week."

In other words, while some studies have suggested that light drinking may benefit certain aspects of cardiovascular health, those possible advantages do not translate into an overall health benefit once alcohol's links to cancer and other diseases are taken into account.

Although the analysis drew on extensive scientific evidence, the researchers emphasize that its estimates describe risks across populations rather than predicting precisely what will happen to any particular person.

Shield says the statistical calculations relied on "the best possible data." However, he cautions that individual circumstances can substantially influence the results.

"However, we can't assume that means one person's individual health risk is the same as what is reported here -- that depends on other factors like lifestyle, genetics, drinking patterns, and other choices that differ person to person."

To estimate alcohol's overall impact, the researchers calculated risks for health conditions already known to be caused by alcohol and combined them into a broader assessment. However, scientists are continuing to investigate additional possible consequences of drinking, including emerging evidence connecting alcohol consumption to pancreatic cancer.

"Understanding those relationships, and how much alcohol contributes to those risks, is an area that still needs further work," says Shield.

What the Findings Mean for U.S. Drinking Guidelines

The results suggest that alcohol-related health risks become more pronounced when consumption exceeds one drink per day, regardless of whether the person is male or female. This provides a more specific reference point for people trying to understand how their drinking habits might affect their long-term health.

Rather than suggesting that any particular amount is completely safe, the findings show how risks increase with greater consumption and why drinking less generally reduces exposure to alcohol-related harm.

"Having a clearer threshold helps people better understand what level of drinking is associated with increased risk and make more informed decisions when drinking," says Shield.

The study has also raised questions about how scientific evidence was incorporated into federal dietary recommendations.

In an accompanying editorial, Robert M. Vincent, a former Associate Administrator for the U.S. Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration, offers his perspective on the circumstances surrounding the research and its role in the development of national guidance.

"The Alcohol Intake and Health report was explicitly invited to inform alcohol guidance during development of the Dietary Guidelines for Americans, 2025-2030," he writes. "Despite the study's adherence to its mandate, its findings were sidelined."

Materials provided by Journal of Studies on Alcohol and Drugs. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

Pourquoi des personnes qui n'ont jamais fumé deviennent accros à la cigarette électronique

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Crédit photo, TOLGA AKMEN/EPA-EFE/REX/Shutterstock

Lorsque le vapotage a commencé à se répandre dans le lycée de Gracey Mungulu, elle n'a pas hésité à dire haut et fort qu'elle trouvait cela aussi répugnant que de fumer.

« Je disais littéralement aux gens : "Euh, pourquoi tu fais ça ?" », raconte-t-elle.

Mais Gracey explique que cette habitude est devenue si courante parmi ses camarades de classe qu'à l'âge de 16 ans, elle s'y est elle-même mise, même si la première fois, elle s'est mise à tousser au lieu d'inhaler.

« Plus on voit ça, plus on a envie d'essayer un peu plus », explique Gracey, « et puis, au final, je m'y suis habituée et je le faisais tout le temps. »

Aujourd'hui âgée de 21 ans, elle achète chaque jour une cigarette électronique rechargeable, qu'elle utilise dès que ses mains ne sont pas occupées par son travail de coiffeuse et à l'école.

Gracey fait partie du million de personnes au Royaume-Uni qui déclarent n'avoir jamais fumé mais qui vapotent aujourd'hui, selon les données d'une récente enquête de l'Office national des statistiques (ONS) – et ce chiffre augmente d'année en année.

Lorsque la cigarette électronique a fait son apparition sur le marché, elle était considérée comme un moyen d'aider les fumeurs à arrêter la cigarette.

La consommation de nicotine par vapotage, une substance hautement addictive, est moins nocive que la cigarette, mais ses effets à long terme ne sont pas encore clairement établis, selon le NHS.

Des informations vérifiées à portée de main

Alors pourquoi des personnes comme Gracey, qui n'ont jamais fumé de cigarette, s'y mettent-elles ?

Cette Londonienne estime que la cigarette électronique l'aide lorsqu'elle ressent de l'anxiété sociale.

« C'est le geste de la main », explique-t-elle, « le simple fait d'avoir quelque chose dans la main, d'avoir quelque chose dans la bouche. »

Tout comme les cigarettes, la cigarette électronique de Gracey contient de la nicotine, une substance hautement addictive qui provoque des symptômes de sevrage, tels que l'irritabilité et l'anxiété, lorsqu'elle disparaît de l'organisme.

Le vapotage est devenu un « geste machinal », explique-t-elle. Lorsqu'elle a répondu à l'appel de BBC News pour cet article, Gracey a raconté que, sans même y penser, elle était allongée dans son lit, sa cigarette électronique dans une main, tandis que de l'autre, elle faisait défiler Instagram.

Le caractère habituel de la vape régulière est un phénomène dont le Dr Helen Wall, médecin généraliste, entend souvent parler de la part de ses patients.

« C'est ce genre de dépendance psychologique à quelque chose d'autre qui sert presque à réguler leurs émotions », explique-t-elle.

Tout comme Gracey, Malin Andersson, qui vapotait jusqu'à récemment, affirme que son habitude allait de pair avec le « doomscrolling ».

Elle avait également essayé la cigarette, mais ne s'était jamais considérée comme une fumeuse.

À 33 ans, elle fait partie de la tranche d'âge la plus susceptible de vapoter. Le rapport de l'ONS suggère que près d'une personne sur six âgée de 25 à 34 ans au Royaume-Uni pourrait utiliser des cigarettes électroniques quotidiennement ou occasionnellement.

Cette militante contre les violences conjugales et ancienne candidate de « Love Island » a commencé à vapoter alors qu'elle traversait une rupture difficile et cherchait du réconfort. Elle pense que c'est le geste physique qui l'a rendue dépendante.

« C'est une poussée de dopamine », explique Malin. « C'est littéralement la version adulte d'une tétine. »

Elle explique que cela engourdissait ses sens et la rendait démotivée.

Malin était tellement frustrée par la dépendance qu'elle avait développée vis-à-vis de sa cigarette électronique à la saveur de glace à la banane qu'elle a arrêté en mai de cette année.

« Ça m'énervait tellement de l'avoir toujours dans la main que je finissais par la jeter à travers la pièce », raconte-t-elle.

Alex, originaire de Manchester, a elle aussi progressivement pris l'habitude de vapoter, une habitude qui a commencé après qu'elle eut vapoté de temps en temps lors de ses sorties nocturnes, puis qu'elle eut commencé à ramener ses cigarettes électroniques chez elle.

La jeune femme de 26 ans, qui n'a pas souhaité donner son nom de famille, explique que cette habitude était devenue une partie si intégrante de sa vie qu'elle travaillait « allongée dans mon lit, mon ordinateur portable devant moi et ma cigarette électronique posée sur ma poitrine ».

La professeure Sarah Jackson, du groupe de recherche sur l'alcool et le tabac de l'University College London, souligne que si les chiffres récents de l'ONS peuvent « tirer la sonnette d'alarme », il se pourrait que certaines personnes de ce groupe aient commencé à fumer si elles ne s'étaient pas mises à vapoter.

« Dans ce cas, le vapotage les a détournées d'un comportement plus nocif, ce qui est une bonne nouvelle », déclare-t-elle. « D'un autre côté, certaines personnes s'exposent à davantage de risques qu'elles ne l'auraient fait autrement. »

Elle cite une étude réalisée en 2024 par des chercheurs de l'UCL, qui a révélé une augmentation de la consommation de nicotine chez les jeunes adultes en Angleterre, alors que le tabagisme avait diminué.

« On peut en déduire que certaines des personnes qui vapotent n'auraient pas fumé autrement », explique Mme Jackson.

Selon le rapport de l'ONS, environ 5,4 millions de personnes âgées de 16 ans et plus utilisent la cigarette électronique en Grande-Bretagne, tandis que le nombre de fumeurs, tous âges confondus, continue de baisser régulièrement. Quelque 4,9 millions d'adultes au Royaume-Uni fument.

Le Royaume-Uni s'est fixé pour objectif de faire baisser le taux de tabagisme et de réduire l'attrait de la cigarette électronique auprès des enfants et des jeunes.

Le marketing des cigarettes électroniques – qui se caractérise par des couleurs vives et des arômes sucrés – fait actuellement l'objet d'un examen, et une récente consultation gouvernementale sur de nouvelles mesures devrait aboutir à une réponse en temps voulu.

Parmi le 1,1 million de vapoteurs n'ayant jamais fumé, « il existe probablement un groupe qui ne vapoterait pas s'il n'y avait pas le marketing très présent autour de la vape », explique Hazel Cheeseman, de l'association caritative Action on Smoking and Health.

Le NHS met en garde les non-fumeurs et les jeunes de 17 ans et moins contre la pratique de la cigarette électronique, tandis qu'il est illégal pour toute personne de moins de 18 ans d'acheter des cigarettes électroniques. Les cigarettes électroniques jetables – considérées comme un facteur clé de l'essor de la cigarette électronique chez les jeunes – ont été interdites l'année dernière.

Wall affirme que la campagne marketing intensive menée ces dernières années en faveur des cigarettes électroniques a contribué à entretenir une « idée fausse » selon laquelle elles seraient « sans danger et ne créeraient pas de dépendance ».

Gracey estime que les cigarettes électroniques devraient être vendues dans le même emballage neutre et standardisé que les cigarettes traditionnelles, car cela « fait peur ».

« Les cigarettes électroniques proposent toutes sortes d'arômes et de jolies couleurs, ce qui ne fait que nous attirer davantage vers elles ».

Même si elle reconnaît que le vapotage est considéré comme bien plus acceptable socialement que le tabagisme au sein de sa génération, elle trouve cette habitude problématique et a envisagé d'arrêter pour économiser les 35 £ par semaine que cela lui coûte.

Mais tout comme pour le tabagisme, arrêter de vapoter peut s'avérer difficile. Et pour Alex, le vapotage l'a conduite dans la direction opposée.

Elle explique avoir réussi à arrêter de vapoter pendant un voyage de plusieurs mois, mais, n'ayant pas accès à des cigarettes électroniques, elle s'est mise à fumer des cigarettes pour la première fois.

Les experts ne considèrent pas la cigarette comme un moyen sain ou efficace d'arrêter de vapoter. Le NHS recommande vivement aux gens d'arrêter de fumer, car le tabagisme reste la principale cause de maladies évitables et de décès prématurés au Royaume-Uni.

Pour arrêter de vapoter, le NHS suggère de réduire progressivement la concentration en nicotine de la cigarette électronique, d'espacer les séances de vapotage ou de se tourner vers un produit de substitution nicotinique adapté.

Mais l'accompagnement professionnel pour arrêter de vapoter est inégal : une enquête récente a révélé que moins de la moitié (45 %) des professionnels de santé en Angleterre proposaient un accompagnement pour arrêter de vapoter. La situation est contrastée dans le reste du Royaume-Uni.

Même s'il est positif de constater que moins de personnes fument, M. Wall estime que pour les personnes comme Gracey qui se tournent directement vers la cigarette électronique, une partie du message n'a pas été bien comprise. « La cigarette électronique n'était censée [selon le NHS] être qu'un substitut aux cigarettes. »

Suspected ‘racist terrorist’ shooter put in French police psychiatric unit

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Sign up now: Get ST's newsletters delivered to your inbox

French President Emmanuel Macron meeting rescue forces at the town hall following a shooting in Trappes, near Paris, on Oct 23.

PARIS – A man suspected of a gun attack in the Paris suburbs that killed one person and wounded five has left custody and been placed in a psychiatric unit for observation, French anti-terror prosecutors said on Oct 25.

The 39-year-old suspect, partly identified as Kevin T, was arrested on Oct 23 in possession of a submachine gun and tear-gas grenades in his car, shortly after the victims were shot as a vehicle drove through the working-class town of Trappes and neighbouring La Verriere.

President Emmanuel Macron had branded the shooting a “racist terrorist attack”, as most of the victims were from foreign backgrounds.

The French National Anti-Terrorism Prosecutor’s Office (PNAT), which is handling the case, on Oct 24 extended the suspect’s time in detention to pursue its interrogation.

But a day later, the PNAT said: “After a psychiatric examination of Kevin T carried out in the evening (of Saturday), his custody has been lifted.”

It said that, in line with conclusions from a psychiatric expert, he “was transferred to the police psychiatric unit to be subjected to a psychiatric observation period likely to last 48 hours”.

That observation period “should allow us to evaluate whether his state of psychiatric health is compatible with pursuing interrogation sessions”.

The PNAT added that traces of cannabis and cocaine were found in the suspect’s blood.

Police have searched his home in the town of Plaisir outside of Paris.

According to a source close to the investigation on Oct 24, they found a Bible, a Quran and a copy of Adolf Hitler’s Mein Kampf.

The person killed in the Oct 23 shooting was a 26-year-old man of Congolese origin, Pengo Bossio Wangi.

One of those wounded was a 12-year-old girl who was hit by bullets eight times. She remains in a critical condition.

The shooting comes at a moment of deep divisions in France, which is heading for elections in six months with far-right candidate Marine le Pen leading in the polls.

Political and social tensions boiled over a week ago into riots that have marred the country’s image ahead of the vote to choose Macron’s successor. AFP

Uganda to impose 21-day quarantine on returning citizens with Congo travel history

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Sign up now: Get ST's newsletters delivered to your inbox

FILE PHOTO: Health workers and humanitarian responders on the front lines of the Ebola response gather to receive a vaccination due to their potential exposure to the virus as part of their work with suspected cases, including contacts, as aid agencies intensify efforts to contain the Ebola outbreak involving the Bundibugyo strain, in the North Kivu Province of the Democratic Republic of the Congo, October 16, 2026. REUTERS/Gradel Muyisa Mumbere/File Photo

KAMPALA, Oct 25 - Ugandan citizens returning to the country with a history of travel to Democratic Republic of Congo will be quarantined for 21 days, Health Minister Chris Baryomunsi said on Sunday as Uganda tightened Ebola precautions despite saying it remained Ebola-free.

Uganda has been investigating a Nigerian Ebola case involving a woman who had travelled from Uganda. Officials said they were reviewing genomic sequencing, travel information, interactions and movements in coordination with Nigerian authorities and international partners.

Baryomunsi said on Sunday that all 60 contacts linked to a Kenyan Ebola case had completed their 21-day monitoring period and been discharged. The contacts were traced across points of entry and exit, hotels and transport operators linked to travellers' movements through Uganda.

"The ministry has intensified traveller screening at Entebbe International Airport and all land border crossings. All travellers intending to enter Uganda by air, land or water must complete the passenger locator form," he said.

Genomic analysis of Nigeria's only confirmed Ebola case showed the virus was closely related to the strain detected in Kenya's index case, though Nigerian authorities said there was no evidence that the Kenya case travelled to Nigeria or had a direct connection.

Uganda borders Congo, where the outbreak began, and has previously battled Ebola outbreaks. REUTERS

Scientists discover how pancreatic cancer rewrites its DNA to escape treatment

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Scientists have uncovered new details about how pancreatic cancer constantly changes its genetic makeup, allowing it to become more aggressive, spread throughout the body, and resist treatment. By examining nearly half a million individual cancer cells, researchers identified genetic changes that could reveal new ways to fight one of the deadliest forms of cancer.

The study, conducted by scientists at NYU Langone Health and its Perlmutter Cancer Center, focused on pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma, a particularly difficult cancer to treat. Nearly 85 percent of patients die within five years of being diagnosed.

Although researchers have already linked more than half a dozen genes to the disease, much remains unknown about how pancreatic cancer develops and adapts. By examining tumor cells individually rather than studying them in large groups, the team gained a clearer understanding of how different populations of cancer cells change their DNA as the disease progresses.

How Pancreatic Cancer Evolves To Escape Treatment

The researchers discovered that pancreatic tumors contain a variety of distinct cell populations known as clones. Each clone consists of cancer cells with similar genetic characteristics, but their behavior can differ dramatically.

Some clones develop changes that help them withstand cancer treatments, while others become better equipped to invade surrounding tissues and spread to other parts of the body. These findings suggest that the cancer's ability to continually reorganize its genetic material plays a central role in both treatment resistance and metastasis.

Previous studies generally divided pancreatic cancer cells into two main categories: classical (less aggressive) and basal (more aggressive). However, because those studies examined large collections of cells together, scientists could not fully explain how cancer cells move from one category to the other.

The latest findings help explain this process. Genetic changes occurring within individual tumors allow cancer cells to shift between these two states, potentially making the disease more dangerous as it develops.

Scientists Identify a Gene That Could Make Pancreatic Cancer Less Aggressive

Published on October 13 in Nature Genetics, the study identified several genetic changes associated with pancreatic cancer progression. One particularly important finding involved MYC, a gene that showed heightened activity in some of the most aggressive cancer cell populations.

In additional laboratory experiments, the researchers blocked MYC activity in aggressive basal pancreatic cancer cells. Remarkably, the cells shifted back toward the less aggressive classical state.

The change also made the cancer cells more exposed to immune cells, suggesting they could become easier for the immune system to recognize and attack. Although further research is needed, the results point to MYC as a promising target for future pancreatic cancer treatments.

"Our study provides the most granular view to date of pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma, revealing how ongoing genetic reshaping drives tumor evolution over the course of disease," said study lead investigator Despoina Kalfakakou, PhD, a postdoctoral research fellow at NYU Grossman School of Medicine and Perlmutter Cancer Center. "These findings suggest that targeting the mechanisms underlying tumor evolution, including MYC, may help restrain the cancer's rapid progression."

"Our research also suggests that single-cell clonal analysis offers a better approach to understanding complex cancers, like pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma, as it develops over time, by mapping how tumors evolve and adapt at each stage of disease progression," said study co-senior investigator Aristotelis Tsirigos, PhD, a professor in the Departments of Medicine and Pathology at NYU Grossman School of Medicine and a member of Perlmutter Cancer Center.

"More broadly, our study results highlight the critical role of clonal diversity, genetic plasticity, and interactions with the tumor's microenvironment in shaping human pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma biology," said Dr. Tsirigos, who is also co-director of the Division of Precision Medicine and director of NYU Langone's Applied Bioinformatics Laboratories.

Why Pancreatic Cancer Can Evade the Immune System

The investigation also provided important clues about pancreatic cancer's ability to hide from the immune system, a characteristic that may help explain why the disease often responds poorly to immunotherapy.

Researchers discovered that certain cancer cell clones showed increased activity of the TIGIT gene, which is associated with proteins that help tumors escape immune detection.

At the same time, these clones exhibited reduced activity of the PD1 gene. This combination may help explain why immunotherapies designed to target PD1 have shown limited effectiveness against pancreatic cancer.

Understanding these differences between cancer cell populations could help scientists develop treatments that interfere with the specific mechanisms tumors use to avoid immune attack.

According to Dr. Tsirigos, clinical trials investigating drugs that inhibit MYC or TIGIT are already underway for pancreatic ductal carcinoma and other cancers. He said the latest findings underscore the need for larger studies examining the different states cancer cells can adopt and how treatments might be tailored to target them.

Nearly Half a Million Cancer Cells Reveal Hidden Tumor Diversity

To investigate how pancreatic cancer changes over time, the scientists examined gene activity (using transcriptomics) in 443,451 individual cells collected from 62 tumors belonging to 56 men and women diagnosed with pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma.

Six of these patients also provided tumor samples both before and after chemotherapy, allowing researchers to examine changes associated with treatment.

Unlike traditional approaches that measure changes in gene activity across large groups of cells, the new analysis examined individual cells. This provided a much more detailed picture of the different cancer cell populations living within the same tumor.

The results showed that individual tumors contained approximately two to 11 distinct clones, consisting of varying combinations of classical and basal cancer cells.

By tracing the genetic origins of these clones and comparing patterns of gene activity, the researchers connected differences in DNA copy numbers with the types of cells present in each tumor.

This approach allowed them to identify which genes, including MYC and TIGIT, were particularly active at different points during cancer progression.

Together, the findings provide a more detailed understanding of how pancreatic tumors evolve, resist treatment, and spread. They also suggest that interfering with the genetic mechanisms driving these changes could offer new opportunities to combat a disease that remains exceptionally difficult to treat.

The research was supported by the Sky Foundation and National Institutes of Health grants P30CA016087 and R01CA245005.

In addition to Drs. Kalfakakou and Tsirigos, the NYU Langone research team included Daniel Cameron, MS; Emily Kawaler, PhD; Ziaohong Jing, PhD; Cristina H. Hajdu, MD; Dylan Tamayo, MS; Yoona Shim; Amanda Ackermann, MD, PhD; Daniel Weissinger; Rosmel Hernandez, PhD; Matthew Beier, MD; Dacia Dimartino, PhD; Peter Meyn; Kalina Rice, MS; Shanmugapriya Selvaraj, MS; Cynthia A. Loomis, MD, PhD; Adriana Heguy, PhD; Amanda W. Lund, PhD; and Igor Dolgalev, PhD.

Additional contributors from Oregon Health and Science University were co-investigators Motoyuki Tsuda, MD, PhD; Hayley Zimny; and Rosalie Sears, PhD.

Researchers from the University of California San Diego included Lidong Wang, PhD; Qi Su, PhD; Sungsik Kim, PhD; Hannah Carter, PhD; Theodore Welling, MD; and co-senior study investigator Diane Simeone, MD.

Materials provided by NYU Langone Health / NYU Grossman School of Medicine. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

Low-dose aspirin linked to 70% lower dementia risk, but there's a catch

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A new analysis from Monash University suggests that low-dose aspirin may be linked to a dramatically lower risk of dementia in older adults with certain genetic characteristics. Researchers found that people with a specific genetic profile who took aspirin had a 70 percent lower relative risk of developing dementia, raising the possibility that genetic testing could eventually help identify people who might benefit from preventive treatments.

The findings, published in Alzheimer's & Dementia: The Journal of the Alzheimer's Association, were based on genetic data from more than 13,500 participants in the landmark ASPREE Trial (Aspirin in Reducing Events in the Elderly). Scientists examined whether differences in participants' genes could help explain why aspirin might affect dementia risk differently from one person to another.

Aspirin Linked to 70% Lower Dementia Risk in Certain People

The most striking results involved genes associated with platelet count. Platelets are small components of the blood that help form clots, and researchers wanted to determine whether genetic variations related to platelet levels could influence aspirin's potential effects on dementia.

After assigning participants genetic scores associated with platelet count, the team compared dementia rates among those who received aspirin and those given a placebo.

Among people in the highest 20 percent of platelet count genetic scores, just 1 percent of aspirin users developed dementia, compared with 3.3 percent of participants taking a placebo. That difference translates to approximately a 70 percent lower relative risk of dementia among those receiving aspirin.

However, the apparent benefit was accompanied by a significant safety concern. In the same genetic group, 4.4 percent of aspirin users experienced major bleeding, compared with 2.1 percent of those receiving a placebo.

A Genetic Clue Could Change Dementia Prevention

Lead author Dr. Peter Fransquet, a Research Fellow at Monash's School of Public Health and Preventive Medicine, said the results suggest that a person's genetic makeup could eventually play an important role in determining which dementia prevention strategies are most appropriate.

"Previous trials found aspirin didn't prevent dementia when everyone was considered together," Dr. Fransquet said.

"Our findings suggest there may be more to the story.

"In people with this particular genetic profile, we saw substantially fewer cases of dementia among those taking aspirin.

"It raises the possibility that genetics could one day help us identify who may benefit from a preventive treatment, rather than taking a one-size-fits-all approach."

The possibility of more targeted prevention is especially important as dementia becomes increasingly common. Dementia Australia estimates that approximately 446,500 people in Australia are living with the condition in 2026. That number is expected to exceed one million by 2065.

Blood Platelets Reveal an Unexpected Dementia Connection

Previous research has connected dementia with platelet activation and aggregation, the processes through which platelets become active and stick together. However, scientists have had a less complete understanding of how a person's overall platelet count might relate to dementia.

The new findings point toward platelet biology as a potentially important area of investigation. Notably, the genetic association identified by researchers did not involve the well-established genetic risk factors typically linked to Alzheimer's disease and other forms of dementia.

"What's particularly interesting is that the signal wasn't linked to the established genetic risk factors for Alzheimer's disease and dementia," Dr. Fransquet said.

"Instead, it's pointing us towards platelet biology and gives us a new avenue to investigate."

Why More Research Is Needed Before Taking Aspirin

Although the findings suggest that aspirin could have different effects depending on a person's genes, researchers emphasize that the results must be confirmed before they can guide medical recommendations.

The next step is to investigate whether the association appears in other populations. Scientists would then need to conduct a clinical trial specifically involving people with the genetic profile identified in the analysis.

"We now need to confirm the finding in other studies and ultimately test it in a trial designed specifically for people with this genetic profile.

"People shouldn't start taking aspirin to prevent dementia on the basis of this study without consulting with their doctor, particularly given the increased risk of serious bleeding.

"If it holds up, the fact that aspirin is already cheap and widely available could make personalized dementia prevention a real possibility."

Materials provided by Monash University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

The Republicans’ Remarkably Bad Midterm Campaigns

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The midterm campaigns have been going badly enough for the Republicans that they have inspired in White House principals some rare candor. “You don’t turn the Titanic around overnight,” Vice-President J. D. Vance said on the trail earlier this year, in Toledo. He was speaking about the economy, but the same might be said now about the second term of Donald Trump, or, perhaps, about Vance’s own political prospects; on Tuesday, the President said that it was “very early” to endorse him as his preferred successor. Later that day, Trump was in North Carolina, a state that he won in the 2024 Presidential election, but where his handpicked Senate candidate, Michael Whatley, is trailing by so much that the G.O.P. has essentially stopped putting money into the race. On Wednesday afternoon, at a rally in Greenville, Trump said of Whatley’s Democratic opponent, Roy Cooper, “He’s going to become your senator. And it’s crazy.”

It is at least a little crazy how sharply politics have turned on the Republicans. Last week, prediction markets favored Democrats to win Senate races not just in North Carolina but in Ohio, which Trump won in 2024 by more than ten points, and in Alaska and Texas, each of which he won by thirteen points. Democrats are still underdogs in Kansas, which their party last represented in the Senate in 1939, and in Iowa, but they are drawing close. By Wednesday, when a headline on Axios read “Senate Republicans Fear Mississippi Is in Play,” things had started to feel a little surreal. The Republican predicament was summed up by an apparent A.I. prompt, mistakenly published on the website of Eric Pratt, a nominee for Congress in Minnesota: “The President is not popular, so all messaging should be focused on local politics and needs.”

On this point, most Republican candidates have seemed on message. A Times analysis found that more than ninety-nine per cent of G.O.P. ad spending is now going to spots that do not mention Trump. But even that strategy is backfiring. The campaign of Vivek Ramaswamy, who is running for governor in Vance’s home state of Ohio, paid for an ad in which the estranged mother of his Democratic opponent, Amy Acton, a doctor and the former director of the state health department, denounced her. The ad neglected to note that Acton had been removed from her mother’s care when she was twelve, after her stepfather was indicted on a charge of raping her. Several prominent Ohio Republicans, including Senator Jon Husted, who is up for reëlection, disavowed the ad; Vance dismissed their objections as “whining,” while Trump said that the ad had hit “pay dirt.”

A similar fissure has appeared in some Senate debates. In Minnesota, the G.O.P. nominee, Michele Tafoya, answered the opening question by referring to “a number of areas where I’m going to disagree with the President.” (She also called the killings in her state of Renee Good and Alex Pretti, by federal law-enforcement agents, “devastating.”) In Michigan, the Republican candidate, Mike Rogers, said, a bit testily, that the President’s high-profile tariffs on goods from Canada “were not well thought through.” In Maine, the longtime Republican incumbent, Susan Collins, bragged that she had voted against Trump more often than she’d voted against Joe Biden. Even the Alaska senator Dan Sullivan, who has been a reliable party-line vote for Trump, said that sometimes he will “forcefully oppose” things “in private.”

One policy choice that Republicans have started to oppose in public is, in retrospect, the pivotal decision of the midterms, and likely of Trump’s second term: the war in Iran. Trump’s approval ratings have never been especially high this term, but they began to drop below forty per cent around late March, a month after the war started, and have steadily declined since then. The elections analyst Nate Silver noted last week that Trump’s ratings were nearly as bad as George W. Bush’s were in his second term. The war has, as predicted, driven up both energy prices and inflation—a situation that has made it much harder for Republican candidates to convincingly claim that their businessman President is doing anything about affordability. It has also forced those same candidates to try to defend a war that few believe in. (The Atlantic reported that even Donald Trump, Jr., has taken issue with his father over it—in private.) During an Iowa Senate debate, the moderators displayed images of two soldiers, native Iowans, killed in action in the Middle East.

In Iowa and in other Midwestern states, the price of diesel, which has risen more sharply than other fuel prices, has become a potent political issue. In response, Trump waived U.S. policy and announced a deal in October with President Vladimir Putin to purchase hundreds of thousands of tons of Russian diesel. A week later, Trump suspended a sanctions package on Russia—named for Lindsey Graham, the late Republican senator from South Carolina, who made the war in Ukraine his signature fight—which the Senate had passed and the President himself signed in September. Meanwhile, Elon Musk, who has spent more than four hundred million dollars on Republican campaigns this election cycle, slowed Ukraine’s access to his Starshield satellite network, leaving that country more vulnerable to Russian attacks. For some Republicans, this was too much. On Wednesday, Ashley Hinson, a politically savvy congresswoman who is the Senate nominee in Iowa, said before a joint rally with the President that she would tell Trump to his face that the deal with Putin was “a bad idea.”

It is also a betrayal. On Wednesday night, Trump campaigned in South Carolina with Darline Graham, who was appointed, at Trump’s behest, to finish her brother’s Senate term, and who championed the Russian-sanctions bill. She has often said on the campaign trail, “I don’t trust Putin, and I don’t trust Russia.” But she is in a surprisingly competitive race in a deep-red state, and she needs Trump’s help to win there, even if it is largely his fault that she might lose. At the rally, she watched as the President casually told a story of calling Putin and asking for a favor, as if to make a show of how responsive the Russian leader is to him. War is horrific, but clarifying. It has exposed Trump, to at least a segment of the public that has long stood with him, as someone who stands not with them but with the oligarchs. And it has shown politicians like Graham and Hinson exactly the terms of the deal they have made. ♦

Published - October 25, 2026 09:03 am IST - Rio de Janeiro

Brazilian President Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva, the Workers' Party (PT) candidate seeking re-election, waves a flag during a closing campaign rally ahead of the presidential runoff, in Sao Paulo, Brazil, October 24, 2026. | Photo Credit: Reuters

Brazilians head to the polls on Sunday (October 25, 2026) facing a stark choice — another four years under veteran leftist Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva, or a return to the right under Senator Flavio Bolsonaro.

It is the final electoral battle for Mr. Lula, who turns 81 next week and is hoping to pull off a dramatic turnaround after a shock second-place finish in the first round to the son of former president Jair Bolsonaro.

Final polls showed the two candidates neck-and-neck.

Sunday's runoff will determine whether Brazil remains one of Latin America's few leftist nations or joins a regional swing to the right championed by U.S. President Donald Trump, who has not openly endorsed the son of his ally.

Mr. Lula, who is seeking a fourth term in office overall, has pitched himself as a bulwark against U.S. interference and democratic backsliding and promised to expand social protections.

Flavio Bolsonaro, 45, has vowed to crack down on crime and slash government spending, while handing Brazil to "the Lord Jesus Christ," with a message of change that won over Brazilians looking for something new.

"Enough is enough. We've had 16 years of this administration, and it's clear it didn't work. It's time for a change," said Joao Paulo, 37, at a rally in Belo Horizonte, referring roughly to the total period Mr. Lula's Workers' Party (PT) has governed Brazil.

Flavio Bolsonaro has pitched himself as more moderate than his firebrand father, who is serving a 27-year sentence under house arrest for an attempted coup bid after his 2022 election loss.

He has vowed that if elected, his first act will be to free Jair Bolsonaro, a deeply divisive figure in Brazil whose legacy loomed large over the campaign of his eldest son.

After the first-round defeat, the left swung into action with massive rallies declaring "Never Again Bolsonaro," where protesters recalled the former President's chaotic handling of the Covid pandemic, devastation of the Amazon rainforest, and cuts to public programs.

Deforestation of the world's largest rainforest has hit its lowest level in four decades under Mr. Lula.

Yet, as the El Nino weather phenomenon fueled fires that have choked Amazonian cities with smoke in recent weeks, climate change barely featured in the campaign.

Mr. Lula, revered for social welfare programs that lifted millions out of poverty, made a dramatic comeback to office in 2022 elections after a massive corruption scandal that saw him jailed for over 18 months.

The charges, thrown out on procedural grounds, have continued to haunt him.

In his third term he has overseen a drop in inflation and unemployment, and lifted millions out of hunger, but voters complain about a high cost of living and are fed up with crime.

Mr. Lula's famed social policies became a point of tension, as he expanded welfare programs during the campaign, while critics want the government to rein in spending.

"Even if people call it handouts, for us they were an enormous help. Because anyone who has ever gone hungry knows not to take these programs for granted," said Walter dos Santos, 56, who lives in northeastern Brazil, a bastion of support for the President, who hails from the same region.

After his first-round blow, Mr. Lula revamped his social media strategy, turning to video selfies and even buying his first cellphone.

A fierce social media battle drew in Meta, parent company of WhatsApp, Facebook and Instagram, which Mr. Lula's campaign accused of favouring his opponent with "algorithmic manipulation."

Meta has denied this, but acknowledged that some of Mr. Lula's campaign ads had been mistakenly deactivated due to technical errors.

Whoever wins, Brazil is facing a bruising aftermath to the election.

"If the left wins, it faces four years with a hostile Congress and threats of impeachment," said political analyst Thomas Traumann, referring to sweeping legislative gains by the right.

"If the far right wins, it will be a government of retribution, directed at the Supreme Court," hated by the right after its conviction of Jair Bolsonaro over the attempted coup.

Flavio Bolsonaro has said he will seek the impeachment of Supreme Court Judge Alexandre de Moraes, who led the case against his father.

More dads taking paid family leave for a new baby, sometimes as much as moms are

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Karlo Campana plays Magnatiles with his daughters Gianna and Giselle, while his 5-month-old son Gianluca watches. Campana is one of the growing number of California men taking advantage of the state's paid bonding leave program. Dania Maxwell for NPR hide caption

Karlo Campana is usually outnumbered these days. He has three young kids under age 4.

On a recent Saturday, he played a mini-game of basketball with his two older kids in their Southern California home, shooting a ball into a hoop on the door, while his baby boy watched from his bouncy chair.

This story was produced in partnership with KFF Health News.

Campana took paid time off in May after the birth of his son — just as he did for his daughters.

It's sometimes called "bonding" or "family" leave, to distinguish it from the paid leave ("maternity" or "disability" leave) that mothers can take to physically recover from childbirth.

"You need that adjustment period," he said. Campana, who works at an ad agency, said having the time off not to think about work — or worry about losing income during that time — was crucial for him and his family during a sleep-deprived period.

"My wife isn't doing it on her own," he said. "She doesn't feel like she's alone on this journey. She feels like she has support." He jokes that by kid No. 3, he thought he had nailed diaper changes without too much mess — but he was wrong.

"The benefits are I get to spend time with each of them when they were in the infant stage, the baby stage, and I get to hold them and, you know, change the poopy diapers," he said. " It's like the funny moments of, like, being a parent, like, 'This is it right here, you're in the thick of it.'"

Karlo Campana plays with his daughter Giselle and his infant son Gianluca at home on October 12 in Santa Ana, Calif. With three children under 4, paid paternity leave gave him something invaluable: time to bond with his kids and support his wife in the thick of early parenthood. Dania Maxwell for NPR hide caption

Campana is among a growing number of fathers who take leave to bond with a new child in the states that offer paid family leave programs — and are part of a larger cultural shift that has seen dads handle more caregiving duties.

In California — as in several other states — men make up about half of bonding claim applications. Last year, men even surpassed women — as in Colorado and Washington — for the first time in the state's history. This trend comes at a time when the federal government has said it wants Americans to have more babies, and families report affordability is an obstacle in raising them.

There's no federal law guaranteeing paid parental leave, but California is one of 14 states, plus Washington, D.C., that have passed legislation to enact paid family leave programs — and in many of those places, more dads are opting in.

"We're in a very different place in terms of our understanding of gender roles, of paternity leave, of dads' roles than we were 20-plus years ago," said Molly Weston Williamson, policy director at Paid Leave for All, an organization that advocates for paid family leave policies.

California's program offers, within a year, up to eight weeks of paid bonding leave for parents after a birth, adoption, or foster care placement. That's on top of six to eight weeks of paid disability leave that moms get to recover after birth.

Karlo Campana at dinner with his wife Lily and their three children. His experience reflects a broader shift across California, where fathers accounted for 51% of paid bonding leave claims in 2025, surpassing mothers for the first time. Dania Maxwell for NPR hide caption

Campana said he initially wasn't sure if he should take paternity leave because of lingering gender stereotypes.

" I felt guilty, for sure," Campana said. " I think dads maybe feel they need to be the provider. They need to be the ones that are only helping their family by bringing money or working."

Campana said that when he was growing up, his father worked three jobs. "It was hard for him to be present, and I think that's the one thing now — it's like, 'No, be present. Be there for your kids.'"

Research has shown that paternity leave confers benefits beyond allowing a father extra time to bond with a new child: It has positive effects on the whole family, including better health outcomes for both parents.

Paid parental leave has also been linked to lower odds of postpartum depression and a decrease in infant mortality rates.

In California, men accounted for 51% of bonding claims filed in 2025. It's a massive shift from when the state program started in 2004, and men made up about 18% of claim applications.

States fund their paid family and medical leave programs with different combinations of employer and employee contributions; California is among six states where family leave is entirely funded by employee payroll deductions.

Karlo Campana poses for a portrait with his wife Lily and their three kids (from left to right) Gianna, Gianluca, and Giselle. Dania Maxwell for NPR hide caption

In addition to changing gender norms, Williamson said other factors are likely contributing to the increase in claims by men in the state: greater awareness about the program, and recent changes to the benefit.

In 2025, the state boosted the amount of income a worker can recoup while on family leave. Now, in California and in other states, the leave benefit can be as high as 90% of their regular salary.

"That makes it a realistic option for a lot more families, and I think we're seeing dads and moms responding to that change," Williamson said. Still, she said, there's a lot the new data about dads isn't telling us.

"We're getting one particular vantage point into this data, which is useful and valuable, but it's only telling us sort of part of the story, and we'd need other kinds of information to better understand the full picture," Williamson said.

"Is it that more dads are working and therefore are potentially eligible for these benefits? Is it that women are deciding not to take leave?"

While private employers can offer their own paid leave as a benefit, this data also reflects only people who took leave through California's paid family leave program.

Karlo Campana washes Giselle's hands after dinner on October 12 at home in Santa Ana, Calif. "My wife isn't doing it on her own," he said of his decision to take paid leave from his job after the birth of their third child. "She doesn't feel like she's alone on this journey. She feels like she has support." Dania Maxwell for NPR hide caption

A spokesperson for the California Employment Development Department, which administers the state's family leave program, said the department couldn't provide definitive reasons for the increase in male bonding claims.

One thing is clear: The growth in these states comes as more parents are feeling strained economically.

President Trump has said, "We want more babies," and has promoted policy initiatives like expanding access to in vitro fertilization and offering investment accounts for newborns.

"People really are very worried about the affordability of kids," said Jeremy Pope, a professor of political science at Brigham Young University, and a principal investigator of the American Family Survey.

Karlo Campana plays with his new son Gianluca as his wife Lily prepares dinner in Santa Ana, Calif. "You need that adjustment period," he said. Dania Maxwell for NPR hide caption

According to the latest survey in 2025, 7 in 10 Americans said raising children had become unaffordable, according to the survey. It was the biggest single-year jump since the survey started more than a decade ago.

"I don't think they want government involved in the decision to have kids," Pope said. "But they worry a lot about how much it costs and they would like to see ways of making those costs smaller or getting help with those costs."

The Pew Research Center found in a recent survey of full-time working parents who are not self-employed, 84% said paid parental, family, or medical leave would be "extremely or very helpful" to them, but only half said it was available to them.
