Published - October 25, 2026 08:34 am IST - Dakar

The attacks hit the restive west African country’s Menaka region, where gunmen reportedly rode in on motorcycles and opened fire on camps mainly housing members of the Daoussahak community. | Photo Credit: AP

Mali's armed forces said on Saturday (October 24, 2026) that they had discovered 54 bodies after attacks on camps in northeastern Mali last weekend, upping the toll by six.

The attacks hit the restive west African country's Menaka region, where gunmen reportedly rode in on motorcycles and opened fire on camps mainly housing members of the Daoussahak community.

The Daoussahak community is where the armed Movement for the Salvation of Azawad (MSA) originated, a group aligned with Mali's ruling junta, according to security and community sources.

Following the attack, a patrol was launched in the area which "discovered fifty-four (54) lifeless bodies in a camp in Tagadayat", Mali's armed forces said.

Sources had previously indicated to AFP that at least 48 people had died.

The armed forces said that the victims were buried on site and that nine survivors were evacuated to the city of Menaka.

It added that testimonies indicated that the attack was carried out by the Islamic State group's Sahel branch.

The targeted camps lie about 50 kilometres (30 miles) from the town of Menaka, in or around the village of Tinabow and the Tagadayat area.

The armed forces said that patrols and security operations were continuing in the region.

A security source based in the city of Gao told AFP last week that the massacre occurred "in retaliation for military operations carried out in the area recently and for attacks by the MSA".

Mali has been plagued since 2012 by a security crisis involving jihadist fighters, separatist groups and criminal gangs.

Published - October 25, 2026 08:08 am IST - SOFIA

An election billboard featuring Bulgarian presidential candidate and incumbent President Iliana Iotova and vice-presidential candidate Kiril Valchev ahead of the country's presidential election on October 25. | Photo Credit: Reuters

Bulgarians vote ⁠in presidential elections on Sunday (October 25, 2026) as Russia’s war in Ukraine spreads to the waters off Bulgaria’s Black Sea coast.

Polls place acting President Iliana Iotova, ‌61, and ex-caretaker Prime Minister Andrey Gyurov, 50, as leaders in a race of 27 candidates.

While the presidency is ‌largely ceremonial, Sunday’s (October 25, 2026) vote has taken on added significance after ‌a ⁠series of drone attacks on merchant ships off Bulgaria’s ⁠Black Sea coast that likely killed some two-dozen international seafarers.

The strikes, which sparked international condemnation and concerns about the growing reach of the war in Ukraine, prompted the ​government to seek allied ‌assistance to bolster maritime security.

Bulgaria has stopped short of directly accusing Russia of carrying out the attacks, but Prime Minister Rumen Radev said their outcome “benefits the Russian Federation”.

Both leading presidential candidates ‌condemned the strikes, but they differ over the broader war.

Mr. Gyurov, ​who served as caretaker Prime Minister this year, is supported by pro-Western, reformist parties and has long expressed ⁠support for Ukraine. He signed a security cooperation agreement with Kyiv during his time as premier.

“I want Bulgaria to have a voice ‌of its own in Europe. A reliable NATO ally that defends its interests through preparedness, strong diplomacy and a clear position,” he said at a rally on Thursday (October 22, 2026).

Ms. Iotova, formerly Vice President, became acting president in January after Mr. Radev resigned to enter the parliamentary race, which he won on what many political analysts considered a Kremlin-friendly ‌ticket. Mr. Radev supports Ms. Iotova, and they both call for a diplomatic end ​to the war.

A victory for Ms. Iotova would make her Bulgaria’s first elected female President.

“My opponents argue that there ⁠must be a war of attrition.” she told a rally on Thursday (October 22, 2026). “The ⁠truth is, we are all the ones who are most exhausted.”

Polls by Alpha Research give Ms. Iotova 41% of ‌the vote and Mr. Gyurov 28%. If no candidate wins a majority, a runoff vote will be held on November 1.

Voting starts ​at 7 a.m. (0500 GMT) and polls close at 8 p.m.

‘I can’t believe I get to see that’: One Born Every Minute is back for a 12th series

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Reality TV show returns to focus on childbirth, diversity and the sharing of stories in a social media age

When One Born Every Minute first aired in 2010, viewers loved its fly-on-the-wall take on life in a maternity unit, from midwives idly chatting about unusual pregnancy cravings to parents squabbling over whether to call their child Zeus.

Almost two decades later, as the show is rebooted for a generation who spend their lives on social media, much has changed. Parents now share far more about their struggles with miscarriages, infertility and mental health; audiences have to be lured away from watching reels on their phones with more rigorous storytelling.

“Quite a bit is different about the way that people have children,” said Will Rowson, executive producer at Dragonfly, which makes the show for Channel 4. “People are so used to sharing. They expect to share, there is a willingness to share, and that is a generational thing.”

The first episode of the show, which airs on Sunday night, tackles topics that have come to the foreground in the years since the Bafta-winning first run ended in 2018. These include caesarean births – it notes that these have doubled over the past decade – as well as health anxiety and pregnancy loss.

The series focuses on parents – a shift from medical staff being the prominent characters in the previous 11 series. Rowson said it would delve into an “enormously diverse range of stories” thanks to a wider approach to casting and a move away on TV from “eclectic, whimsical” scenes. A tighter budget was also a factor.

He said: “We’re recognising that people will get their miscellany and gentle anthropology elsewhere, so we make sure we’re giving the narratives, the backstories of the families, and understanding what the birth means to them. That’s the sort of context and richness you don’t get in a 30-second clip.”

Like its predecessor, the show takes a visceral, no-holds-barred approach to what’s shown, from bellies being sliced open to a baby being pulled out smeared in faeces. Set in St Mary’s hospital in Manchester, which specialises in maternity care, the series will feature more complicated pregnancies and surgical procedures. “It’s an extraordinary thing to witness, you go ‘oh my goodness, I can’t believe I get to see that’,” said Rowson.

Faye, who is shown giving birth by C-section, said the show had given her a way to experience the birth of her son differently. “I remember shaking, and feeling this overwhelming feeling of being cold and quite scared. And actually, when I’ve seen it back, I’m in really safe hands, and I got to see my little boy being born.”

She was also motivated to take part because – as an NHS clinical lead – she wanted viewers to see “what’s going on in the background, and how much hard work people do”. She added: “You always hear the bad stuff and not the good stuff.”

In the first episode, Faye shares the heartbreak of multiple miscarriages, leading her to believe she would not have children until a surprise pregnancy at the age of 38. She said she wanted to help people understand “how many women it happens to, and how difficult it is to experience”.

“I just felt something was wrong with me. I felt this lack of being a woman, and it fed into my [former] marriage, and everything broke down further from that,” she said. When she finally became pregnant, her earlier losses intensified her anxiety: “I said to [my partner] Will ‘if I experience it again, I think I’ll be broken’.”

Peace, who also features on the show, wanted to showcase the experience of having twins, from concerns over their growth, to the decision to have a C-section. “I would love to watch that,” she said, adding that she started watching the show as a child: “I love seeing normal women with normal bodies having normal pregnancies.”

Through sharing her experience of miscarriage, the unexpectedness of having twins, and the health complications her daughter was born with, Peace wanted viewers to understand the importance of “not letting things out of your control get to you”.

She added: “I didn’t want to do it at first, because I didn’t want to open up about [miscarriage], and because I feel it seems like a taboo topic to talk about, and you blame yourself. There is this perception of having a perfect pregnancy.”

Like many other parents who took part, her partner, Kwamdeen, felt the episode would be “a great memento” to show their children.

The casting director, Nsofwa Nwannadi, said she was surprised by how open people were and was moved by the desire among those who experienced challenges for “people to understand that they are not alone, it’s not stigmatised, it’s not your fault”.

Some participants applied to be on the show, with many responding to communications sent out by the hospital, Nwannadi said. Her team sought out a diverse range of participants and experiences by engaging with minority ethnic, LGBT and disability communities.

Unlike reality TV, in which participants are cast for their personalities, “this is real life, we’re reflecting what’s actually there at the hospital”, she said.

This included fathers ruminating on fatherhood and modern masculinity, mothers expressing fears about sublimating their identities to motherhood, and birthing partners discussing their decision to remain child-free.

“It’s been lovely to have been privy to a myriad of different stories, backgrounds and journeys. It’s not a one-size-fits-all situation, and that’s great, actually,” Nwannadi said.

One Born Every Minute airs on E4 from Sunday at 9pm.

Fresh clashes rock France as protesters march on

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The Hindu On Books Books of the week, reviews, excerpts, new titles and features.

Updated - October 25, 2026 07:51 am IST - Paris, France

Protesters taking cover behind a plywood board face off with French gendarmes amid clashes during a demonstration organised by labour unions in Paris as part of nationwide protests called by the "Yellow Vests" movement, joined by French high school and university students against the cost of living and rising fuel prices, in France, October 24, 2026. | Photo Credit: Reuters

Clashes erupted across France on Saturday (October 24, 2026) as protesters took to the streets, a week after marches that degenerated into violence and prompted a firm security backlash from President Emmanuel Macron, who is scrambling to restore order.

Police arrested nearly 1,000 people as officers faced “an extremely elevated level of violence” from black-clad “ultra-left” extremists, Interior Minister Laurent Nunez said.

Riot police squads fired tear gas at protesters in the capital as well as in Marseille, Lyon, Bordeaux, Lille, Toulouse, Montpellier and Nantes.

Two people were “seriously” injured, Mr. Nunez said: one man in Toulouse, who reportedly had fingers blown off, and another person in Clermont-Ferrand. He did not give details of either case.

Authorities had deployed 70,000 security personnel across the country for the new marches, called to protest the cost of living.

The violence came a week after rioting that saw public buildings vandalised, hundreds of shops looted and 2,700 people arrested, casting a pall over the country six months before an election to choose Mr. Macron’s successor.

Mr. Nunez said on Saturday’s (October 24, 2026) outcome was more “positive” than a week ago, with more police on the streets acting more rapidly, conducting more pre-demonstration checks and better protecting businesses.

But there was widespread anger at the government over a protest crackdown viewed as an assault on the right to protest.

Saturday’s (October 24, 2026) demonstrations drew between 4,10,000 and 8,00,000 people across the country, according to different estimates by authorities and the organising CGT union.

The main Paris march had between 1,10,000 and 2,50,000 attendees, according to those sources.

Mr. Macron’s government has ordered police to prevent protesters from reaching city centres, where shops are concentrated, and in Paris and some other cities, partial curfews were imposed in certain urban zones.

The French President had ordered non-union demonstrations be banned as “illegitimate”.

In Paris, where many shops had boarded up windows to prevent looting, and the Louvre and the Musee d’Orsay museums shut for the day, the main demonstration passed off largely peacefully.

The march had been pushed to the capital’s southeast rim under a last-minute order by authorities.

Afterwards, though, clashes broke out as the crowd was dispersing, with Mr. Nunez blaming “500 rioters” at the back of the march.

AFP journalists saw police charge several times, blasting tear gas and using batons against some, mostly young, protesters.

“They were just taking people down, you know, beating protesters,” one demonstrator bleeding from a baton blow to his face, who gave his first name as David, told AFP.

Thousands of protesters in the western city of Nantes broke through lines of police firing tear gas and a water cannon to reach the city centre, an AFP journalist witnessed.

Emma, 23, who came out in the northwest city of Rennes, denounced the bans on demonstrations and the “repression” of protests by high school students demanding better learning conditions.

Marching in the rain at the head of the protest in the northern city of Lille, 47-year-old Delphine insisted: “Neither the President, nor the weather will stop me from demonstrating.”

In Lyon, police used tear gas to disperse around 1,000 people gathered in the south of the city.

“If they ban protests, that means we’re heading more and more towards a regime that is fascist, that is dictatorial,” said one 20-year-old protester in Lyon, who gave only his first name, Silem.

In the southern cities of Cannes and Montpellier, far-right activists clashed with “ultra-left militants”, in what authorities said was an unprecedented development.

There was looting on the sidelines of the marches in Marseille and Grenoble, police said.

The Yellow Vests, who staged disruptive protests against Macron eight years ago, called for more demonstrations on October 31 for “the freedom to protest”.

Far-left presidential candidate Jean-Luc Mélenchon, who marched in Paris, said the movement “is going to repeat itself every week”.

Tensions were heightened by a rally in the city of Orleans by far-right leader Marine Le Pen’s National Rally party to kick off her campaign for the presidential election.

Thousands of counter-protesters chanting anti-fascist slogans marched in the city.

The unrest across France highlighted the febrile political atmosphere ahead of the vote, in which Le Pen leads in surveys.

Inflation in France, particularly affecting the cost of fuel, has surged since the start of the Middle East war in February.

“I’m going to try and instill a bit of optimism,” said the four-time presidential hopeful, who has cast herself as the law-and-order candidate, ahead of her first major campaign speech on Sunday (October 25, 2026).

Protests have also broken out in Italy, Belgium, Spain and Portugal over different issues, yet with some overlap on inflation and education problems.

Published - October 25, 2026 07:23 am IST

World / France

Former President Barack Obama, centre, joined by Democratic nominee for U.S. Senate Abdul El-Sayed, left, and Democratic gubernatorial nominee and Michigan Secretary of State Jocelyn Benson, raise hands during a campaign event at Cass Technical High School Saturday, October 24, 2026, in Detroit. | Photo Credit: AP

Nationally, polls also suggest that Democrats could regain control of the House of Representatives but face a much more competitive contest for the Senate.

The Public Health Consequences of an Inaccurate Census

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.

Update your preferences in Account Settings

Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.

Update your preferences in Account Settings

In 2015, Chicago’s public health department identified three cases of invasive meningococcal disease, a highly dangerous pathogen that can kill within hours. To determine whether these cases signaled a larger outbreak, the department—which one of us, Dr. Julie Morita, led at the time—needed to know not just total cases, but the total population and demographics of affected areas. The team turned to a pillar of our nation’s public health system: the U.S. Census.

The data showed that men who have sex with men—including gay and bisexual men—among the city’s Black population were disproportionately affected. That finding drove the health department’s response, which included working with community partners to surge thousands of free vaccines into neighborhoods across Chicago. Instead of a larger and far deadlier outbreak, the outbreak ended with 11 cases in Chicago and one death.

Census data informs every aspect of our nation’s public health system. As former leaders of local and federal health agencies, we have seen this firsthand. Unfortunately, the Trump Administration has proposed radical changes to the census that would make our country less healthy, less safe, and less fair.

The public comment period on this proposal closes on Nov. 2. Health leaders must move quickly to oppose these deeply misguided changes. It is clear to us that these changes would amount not only to an assault on our public health system but also on the U.S. Constitution.

Per the Constitution, the federal government has carried out a national census every decade since 1790—through wars, depressions, and pandemics. Everyone who resides in our country matters and should be counted. But the Trump Administration is suggesting that the census should intentionally exclude people. This includes undocumented residents and nonimmigrant visa holders, such as foreign national students and temporary workers whose visas may permit them to live in the U.S. for several years. The Administration is further attempting to remove demographic questions related to race, ethnicity, and sexual orientation from the census questionnaire.

The repercussions would be vast. An inaccurate count would negatively affect congressional representation. The idea that some people do not deserve to be counted uncomfortably echoes the “3/5 compromise,” an insidious and later repealed clause in the original Constitution under which enslaved persons were counted as less than full people. Incomplete and unreliable demographic data could distort how trillions of federal dollars are distributed across hundreds of programs such as Medicaid, SNAP, WIC, and Head Start. Communities with the greatest needs, especially those with immigrant or mixed-status families, would likely be hardest hit.

These are our neighbors, families, and friends. But let’s be clear: the impacts would extend beyond those communities, especially the public health consequences. As the Chicago meningococcal outbreak illustrates, health officials need a complete picture to protect people from disease and other threats. They must know who is getting sick, where, and at what rate. Take any of those data out of the equation, and the people who need the most help are far less likely to get it.

During the H1N1 influenza pandemic in 2009, when one of us, Dr. Richard Besser, served as acting director of the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), census data were instrumental in tracking the outbreak. CDC relied on this data to calculate infection rates by age, which showed that children and young adults were getting sick at far higher rates than older adults—a striking departure from typical patterns observed with seasonal flu. When a vaccine became available, these data directly informed recommendations that prioritized children and young adults.

A decade later, the COVID pandemic demonstrated the importance of these data on a massive scale. As case counts surged in the initial months of the outbreak, population and demographic data revealed who was shouldering the greatest burdens. CDC found that hospitalization rates were highest for American Indian, Alaska Native, Black, and Hispanic populations. CDC later used census population estimates to calculate vaccination rates by race and ethnicity, allowing health officials to identify disparities in vaccine uptake. States like North Carolina relied successfully on census-tract data to increase vaccination rates among target populations.

These examples span different locations, diseases, and levels of government, but illustrate common themes: public health officials rely heavily on census data to save lives and protect communities, and protecting communities at greatest risk helps keep us all safer and healthier.

Health officials use population counts to study cancer, heart disease, HIV/AIDS, and many other diseases. Demographic data expose disparities that raw numbers can obscure and show whether those gaps are growing or shrinking. Together, they offer a roadmap to direct resources where they can do the most good.

The next census count is still a few years away, but what our leaders decide now matters a great deal. A census that deliberately counts fewer people while telling us less about them will leave our nation ill-prepared for future outbreaks and pandemics, while making it harder to handle daily health challenges. It is essential for the public health community to speak out now.

Mysterious Brain Signal Linked to Several Schizophrenia Symptoms

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Schizophrenia affects how people perceive their surroundings, organize their thoughts, and communicate with others. Although these symptoms are often studied separately, new research suggests they may be connected to a common disruption in brain activity.

In a study published in Science Advances, scientists identified changes in a brain rhythm known as beta-band activity that were associated with several symptoms of schizophrenia. Patients with the most pronounced changes also had greater difficulty processing sensory information, more disorganized speech, and more severe clinical symptoms.

Researchers examined brain activity in 25 people with schizophrenia and 25 healthy participants using high-resolution magnetoencephalography, or MEG. The recordings were collected at the McConnell Brain Imaging Centre at the Montreal Neurological Institute-Hospital (The Neuro). Participants completed a task involving the timing of sights and sounds, and researchers separately analyzed their speech for signs of disorganization.

Compared with the control group, participants with schizophrenia showed weaker and slower beta-band activity. These brain rhythms are involved in processes that help the brain interpret sensory information and coordinate responses to the environment.

“What struck us was that the same weakened brain signal kept showing up wherever we looked, whether patients were processing a sound, a sight or completing a movement,” said Hsi (Tiana) Wei, lead author of the study and a postdoctoral scholar in McGill University’s Department of Psychiatry and the Douglas Research Centre. “Seeing the same pattern across these different domains suggested there may be a common underlying process linking the symptoms.”

The results support the possibility that some schizophrenia symptoms arise from disturbances in how the brain constructs and updates its understanding of the world. Rather than representing entirely independent problems, difficulties with perception, behavior, and speech may share an underlying neurological process.

“This gives us early but compelling evidence that measuring the brain’s operations, especially how it builds and revises its own model of the world, may help us appreciate that there is a common thread behind the confusing array of symptoms we see in disorders like schizophrenia,” said Dr. Lena Palaniyappan, senior author of the study, Professor of Psychiatry at McGill University, and director of the Centre of Excellence in Youth Mental Health at the Douglas Hospital.

Palaniyappan believes that further research could eventually help clinicians identify young people who need additional mental health support before psychosis develops. “The very first sign of schizophrenia begins in our adolescence, but this often gets missed for a long time. If this finding is confirmed in larger samples, we may have a potentially useful method to identify who, among youth seeking help for mental health, needs extra support to prevent psychosis,” he said.

The study involved just 50 participants, and its findings establish associations rather than demonstrating that altered beta-band activity causes schizophrenia symptoms. The researchers also have not established whether measuring this signal can reliably predict psychosis.

“This is a first, modest-size study, and the associations with speech and clinical severity will need to be tested in larger independent samples before we can be confident in them,” Wei said.

If future studies confirm the findings, beta-band activity could potentially serve as a measurable indicator of brain dysfunction associated with schizophrenia. Wei also suggested that the signal may be worth investigating as a future treatment target, although its clinical usefulness remains unproven.

Reference: “Neurophysiological dynamics related to perception, action, and language in schizophrenia” by Hsi T. Wei, Dominic Boutet, Rukun Dou, Jessica Ahrens, Nadia Zeramdini, Alban Voppel, Fernando Gonzales-Aste, Fatme Abboud, Sylvain Baillet and Lena Palaniyappan, 21 October 2026, Science Advances. DOI: 10.1126/sciadv.aeh5819

Myanmar’s Aung San Suu Kyi ‘sounds strong’ but is isolated, son says

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Kim Aris tells the Guardian chance to speak with detained Nobel laureate was ‘public relations’ move from military leaders

Myanmar’s former democratic leader Aung San Suu Kyi appears to be in good physical and mental health but is living in near-complete isolation, her son has said after the first call with his 81-year-old mother in more than five years.

“She still sounds certainly all there mentally, and physically she sounds strong,” Kim Aris said. “I don’t think she has anybody outside of the military or the police around her, so essentially she’s been held in solitary confinement from what I gather.”

After being deposed in a 2021 military coup, the Nobel Peace prize laureate has been moved to an unspecified location in the capital. “I’m still not entirely sure where she is,” said Aris. “I think it’s a residence in Naypyidaw, which is fenced off and it has a scrubby bit of land around it.”

Aris, who has lived in the UK most of his life and whose late father was a British historian, was allowed to speak to his mother on Thursday at the Myanmar embassy in London. While he welcomed the chance to talk, he said the invitation was part of a “public relations” campaign by the country’s military leaders.

The former junta chief Min Aung Hlaing has sought to rebuild international diplomatic ties, and in April switched his military fatigues for civilian clothes after elections that were widely condemned as a sham.

Activists have long demanded “proof of life” for Aung San Suu Kyi, as well as scores of other political prisoners. In August, she was finally photographed during a Red Cross visit. Last week, a government spokesperson published images of her smiling in a wood-panelled room as officials delivered two gardening books given to her as gifts by Britain’s King Charles. A paper calendar on the wall behind her showed the date.

Aris said these photo-ops were well-known stunts that the regime had used before to pretend his mother, a national icon, was being treated fairly. The daughter of the assassinated national independence hero Gen Aung San has spent much of her life since the late 1980s in and out of house arrest for leading pro-democracy movements.

“She remains completely shut off from the rest of the world. I think she’s unaware of what’s happening outside of where she is because she’s not allowed any newspapers or radio or television,” Aris said. “She’s been locked up for five years on trumped-up, politically motivated charges and she should be free.”

Ruled for decades by generals and suffering from western sanctions, Myanmar experienced a brief democratic moment after Aung San Suu Kyi was allowed to run in a 2015 election that her party overwhelmingly won. She took up the role of “state counsellor” under a civilian-military power-sharing agreement.

Her international reputation was soon tarnished after the 2016-2017 mass killings and expulsions of Rohingya Muslims. Aung San Suu Kyi flew to the international court of justice in The Hague, where she said she acknowledged military units might have used “disproportionate force”, but defended the government against accusations of genocide.

Aris said that his mother was never “silent” on the Rohingya crisis and what she said on the issue “could fill volumes”. Under her leadership, Myanmar launched a domestic inquiry and appointed the former UN chief Kofi Annan to head a commission.

He added that as state counsellor, Aung San Suu Kyi was “always walking on a tightrope”, knowing the military could strip her of her fragile power at any moment.

“The very hard-won nascent democracy that she was trying to lead was holding on to things by a thread,” he said. “The military could have taken over at any point, and they were doing their best to make my mother’s situation a lot more difficult and to undermine her position. And I think in that respect they did a pretty good job.”

The 2021 power grab by the military triggered a civil war that has killed more than 100,000 people, according to conflict monitors.

Aris said the level of public global support for his mother had “dwindled incredibly” since the Rohingya crisis and that the whole country was now suffering from a lack of international attention. While many governments have called for Aung San Suu Kyi’s release, she is not treated as the beloved international figure that she once was.

“Never mind my mother, the fact that people ignore what’s happening in Burma shouldn’t be down to what was being done to the Rohingya,” he said, using the country’s former name. “And that seems to be the case sometimes, that people say: ‘Oh, well, look how the Rohingya were treated. We can ignore what’s happening in Burma.’”

Scientists Discover That Ovaries May Be Doing Something Unexpected Decades After Menopause

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Ovaries often dismissed as inactive after menopause may still shape how women age, with human tissue revealing inflammatory changes and molecular similarities to ovarian cancer.

Ovaries continue to change for decades after menopause, long after their role in reproduction has ended. In a study of ovarian tissue from 28 women ages 50 to 75, scientists found age-related shifts in proteins associated with inflammation, immune activity, and the structure of the tissue surrounding cells.

The research, conducted by scientists at Northwestern University and the Buck Institute for Research on Aging, was published in Aging Cell.

“In the ovary, we reach a point of no return with respect to fertility in which it’s no longer producing eggs, and I would venture to say most people assume those ovaries are just sitting there doing nothing, similar to the appendix,” said co-corresponding author Francesca Duncan, associate professor of reproductive science in medicine at Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine. “But that’s what makes this study very different.”

To investigate what changes after menopause, the researchers looked beyond ovarian hormones to examine the tissue’s protein composition. Co-corresponding author Birgit Schilling, professor and director of the Mass Spectrometry Center at the Buck Institute, led the team responsible for that analysis. Spearheaded by Buck Institute staff scientist and Schilling lab member Mark Watson, the work used advanced proteomic technologies that can identify and measure thousands of proteins at once. The researchers analyzed 5,812 groups of proteins in normal ovarian tissue.

Human ovarian tissue is difficult to obtain, making the collection of 28 ovaries unusually valuable, Duncan said. Concentrating on a 25-year age range after menopause allowed the team to isolate differences within the postmenopausal years, rather than comparing young and old ovaries across an entire lifespan. The technology also made it possible to examine thousands of proteins in every sample.

“When we analyzed the data in multiple different statistical ways, what became clear across all these methodologies is that the ovary was not stagnant,” Schilling said. “In fact, over the decades, there were quite dramatic differences in terms of some proteins’ levels would go up with age and some proteins’ levels would go down, which tells us this organ is quite dynamic.”

Ovaries from younger postmenopausal women contained more proteins involved in maintaining cells and tissue structure. Older ovaries had more proteins associated with inflammation, immune activity, and the breakdown and rebuilding of the tissue around cells. The differences were greatest between women in their 50s and those ages 70 and older.

Some of the molecular patterns also resembled those seen in cellular senescence. In this state, aging or damaged cells stop dividing but remain biologically active and can send signals that affect nearby tissue.

“Our data points to progressive aging features in which the ovary appears to lose its original identity and ends up doing something different,” Duncan said. “We do not yet know if there’s ever a point in a woman’s life when this organ is no longer beneficial and it’s actually harmful. These results really point to the need to ask this question.”

Duncan’s group had previously found similar evidence of continued remodeling in postmenopausal ovaries of mice. She began both that research and the human study with expectations that the findings ultimately challenged.

“I was predicting that if we took a 50-year-old ovary, a 60-year-old ovary, a 70-year-old, an 80-year-old ovary, they would all look exactly the same, have no eggs, and basically be non-functional,” Duncan said. “The fact that across species we’re seeing this gives us confidence that this isn’t just some random event, but something truly meaningful is happening in the biology.”

Women now routinely live for decades after menopause, Duncan noted, making the ovary’s role during this long period increasingly relevant to understanding women’s health beyond fertility.

“The ovary is not just a reproductive organ,” Schilling said. “It really influences how somebody ages because it speaks to all these other organs, such as the brain, and also to the bone, and women after menopause experience often a lot of bone problems. So, we think there is a connection between all these organs.”

The changes in tissue structure also connect the new findings to an earlier question about ovarian cancer. In 2016, Duncan’s lab was the first to find that aging ovaries become increasingly inflamed, fibrotic, and stiff, resembling scarring in other tissues. Cancer cells can thrive in stiff, collagen-rich environments, prompting the researchers to investigate whether these changes could make older ovaries more hospitable to cancer cells. The new protein analysis provides a molecular view of that remodeling process after menopause.

All the tissue in the current study appeared normal and cancer-free. Nevertheless, some proteins previously associated with ovarian cancer were more abundant in aging ovaries, with the strongest signal among women in their 60s. This does not mean the ovaries were cancerous or precancerous. It suggests that healthy ovarian aging and ovarian cancer share some biological processes.

“It’s not that all postmenopausal women get ovarian cancer, but we know that that fibrotic inflammatory environment is certainly an environment that cancer cells prefer to home in on,” Duncan said. “This is all consistent with the idea that the aging ovary is a potential third hit for ovarian cancer, with the first hit being genetic and the second hit being environmental. So can we target the aging ovary therapeutically to lower the risk of ovarian cancer, or can we develop novel biomarkers of ovarian cancer?”

Whether aging in the ovary can drive changes elsewhere in the body remains an open question.

“Is the ovary the canary in the coal mine in women?” said Duncan, who is also an associate professor in residence at the Buck Institute. “Could aging in the ovary set off a cascade of changes that continues throughout the body as women get older?”

The findings do not answer whether healthy ovaries should ever be removed after menopause, the researchers emphasized. Much more research is needed to determine exactly what the organ does during these years and whether the changes observed contribute to chronic disease or are simply features of normal aging.

Reference: “The Human Ovary Exhibits Dynamic Molecular Remodeling in the Decades Post-Menopause” by Mark A. Watson, Bikem Soygur, Christina D. King, Pooja R. Devrukhkar, Simon Melov, Elisheva D. Shanes, Mary Ellen G. Pavone, Francesca E. Duncan and Birgit Schilling, 7 September 2026, Aging Cell. DOI: 10.1111/acel.70714

Mediterranean diet may boost tiny proteins that protect the heart and brain

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A new study suggests that the Mediterranean diet may protect the heart, brain, and aging cells through a surprising biological mechanism involving tiny proteins produced by mitochondria, the structures responsible for generating most of the energy our cells need.

Researchers at the USC Leonard Davis School of Gerontology discovered that people who more closely followed the Mediterranean diet had higher levels of two mitochondrial microproteins called humanin and SHMOOSE. Both have previously been linked to protection against cardiovascular disease and neurodegeneration, the gradual deterioration of nerve cells that occurs in conditions such as Alzheimer's disease.

The study, led by USC Leonard Davis Instructional Associate Professor of Gerontology Roberto Vicinanza, offers a possible explanation for how everyday food choices influence cellular health and the aging process.

"These microproteins may act as molecular messengers that translate what we eat into how our cells function and age," Vicinanza said. "It's a new biological pathway that helps explain why the Mediterranean diet is so powerful."

How the Mediterranean Diet May Protect Aging Cells

The Mediterranean diet emphasizes foods such as olive oil, fish, beans, lentils, fruits, and vegetables. It has consistently been associated with a lower risk of heart disease, type 2 diabetes, and cognitive decline. However, scientists are still working to understand exactly how this eating pattern produces its wide-ranging health benefits.

To investigate, the researchers examined blood samples from older adults who followed the Mediterranean diet to different degrees. Those whose eating habits most closely matched the diet had significantly higher concentrations of humanin and SHMOOSE.

They also showed lower indicators of oxidative stress, a process in which harmful molecules called reactive oxygen species overwhelm the body's protective defenses. Over time, oxidative stress can damage proteins, DNA, and other cellular components, contributing to aging and chronic disease.

Interestingly, the researchers found that certain foods were associated with different microproteins. Higher consumption of olive oil, fish, and legumes was linked to increased humanin levels. Meanwhile, olive oil consumption and eating fewer refined carbohydrates were associated with higher levels of SHMOOSE.

Refined carbohydrates include foods made with heavily processed grains and sugars, such as white bread, pastries, and many sugary snacks. These foods generally contain less fiber and fewer naturally occurring nutrients than their minimally processed counterparts.

"These findings suggest that specific components of the Mediterranean diet may directly influence mitochondrial biology," said USC Leonard Davis School Dean and USC Distinguished Professor Pinchas Cohen, the study's senior author. "Humanin and SHMOOSE could serve as biomarkers for adherence to the Mediterranean diet and have clinical significance."

Biomarkers are measurable biological indicators that can provide information about a person's health or physiological state. In this case, the researchers suggest that humanin and SHMOOSE could eventually help scientists understand how closely someone's diet aligns with healthy eating patterns and how their cells respond.

Tiny Mitochondrial Proteins With Powerful Effects

The findings extend a scientific effort spanning more than 20 years to investigate small proteins produced by mitochondria. Cohen has played a leading role in discovering and studying these molecules, which are increasingly recognized as important participants in cellular health.

Mitochondria are often described as the powerhouses of cells because they produce much of the energy required for essential biological activities. But their role extends beyond energy production. They also help regulate metabolism, cellular stress responses, and processes associated with aging.

Unlike most proteins, which are produced using instructions stored in the DNA inside the cell's nucleus, mitochondrial microproteins originate from genetic instructions within mitochondria themselves.

These instructions are found in small segments of mitochondrial DNA known as open reading frames. Some of these regions were once considered nonfunctional, but researchers have discovered that they can encode biologically active molecules.

One of the best understood is humanin, first identified by Cohen and fellow researchers in 2003. Subsequent studies have associated humanin with improved insulin sensitivity, better cardiovascular health, increased longevity, and protection of cognitive abilities.

Insulin sensitivity refers to how effectively the body's cells respond to insulin, the hormone that helps regulate blood sugar. Maintaining healthy insulin sensitivity is important for metabolic health and reducing the risk of type 2 diabetes.

Another intriguing microprotein is SHMOOSE (Small Human Mitochondrial ORF Over SErine tRNA), which was identified more recently by Cohen's research team.

SHMOOSE has attracted particular interest because of its possible role in protecting the brain. Scientists have found that a genetic variant of SHMOOSE is associated with an increased risk of Alzheimer's disease. The normal version, meanwhile, appears to help protect neurons from damage involving amyloid, a protein that can accumulate abnormally in the brains of people with Alzheimer's.

"These peptides are emerging as key regulators of aging biology," Cohen said. "They connect mitochondrial function to diseases like Alzheimer's and heart disease and now, potentially, to nutrition."

A Hidden Mechanism for Protecting the Heart

The scientists also identified a potentially important connection between humanin and an enzyme called Nox2, which helps generate reactive oxygen species.

Although these molecules have useful roles in normal cell signaling and immune defense, excessive production can contribute to oxidative stress and damage blood vessels and other tissues.

The researchers observed that people with higher humanin levels tended to have lower Nox2 activity. This relationship raises the possibility that humanin helps limit damaging oxidative processes, potentially offering an additional layer of cardiovascular protection.

Based on these findings, the researchers propose that the Mediterranean diet could support heart health in two complementary ways. The diet may help reduce oxidative stress directly while also supporting the production of mitochondrial microproteins that influence pathways involved in cellular damage.

However, the observed associations do not establish that the diet directly increases these proteins or that the proteins themselves prevent cardiovascular disease.

"This could represent a new cardioprotective mechanism of the Mediterranean diet," Vicinanza said.

Bringing the Mediterranean Diet to a Global Audience

Vicinanza's interest in the Mediterranean diet extends beyond investigating its effects inside the body. He has also worked to promote this traditional eating pattern internationally as a way to support human health, cultural heritage, and environmental sustainability.

As part of those efforts, he collaborated with the Municipality of Pollica in Italy, which is recognized as a UNESCO Mediterranean Diet emblematic community.

That collaboration helped support the establishment of the International Day of the Mediterranean Diet through the United Nations.

Observed every year on November 16, the international event is intended to draw attention to the Mediterranean diet's health benefits as well as its cultural traditions and environmental significance.

For Vicinanza, the new findings reinforce the relationship between traditional food practices and modern discoveries about how the body functions.

"We're connecting centuries-old dietary traditions with cutting-edge molecular biology," Vicinanza said. "It supports the idea that healthy eating patterns with little to no ultra-processed foods reflect how humans have eaten over long periods and may create conditions to which mitochondria -- ancient cellular organelles -- are likely adapted."

Could Tiny Proteins Help Unlock Healthier Aging?

Although the findings are promising, the researchers emphasize that the study was relatively small and observational. That means it identified relationships between dietary habits and biological measurements but could not determine whether eating a Mediterranean diet directly caused the differences in microprotein levels.

Other factors, including participants' overall health and lifestyle, could also influence the results.

Nevertheless, the discovery opens an intriguing possibility for the emerging field of precision nutrition, which seeks to develop dietary recommendations based on an individual's biological characteristics and needs.

If future research confirms that dietary changes can reliably influence humanin, SHMOOSE, and related microproteins, these molecules could potentially become useful tools for evaluating nutritional interventions and developing more personalized strategies for healthy aging.

The next step is to determine whether adopting a Mediterranean-style diet actually increases these proteins and whether those changes lead to measurable improvements in cardiovascular health, brain function, or disease risk.

"Our goal is to move from observing associations to understanding causality," Vicinanza said. "If we can harness these pathways, we may be able to design nutritional strategies that promote healthy aging at the molecular level."

The work was funded by the USC Daryl and Irwin Simon Nutrition for Alzheimer's Disease Prevention Research Fund (to Vicinanza), the Hanson-Thorell Family Research Award (to Vicinanza), National Institutes of Health grant P30AG094848 (to Cohen) and PRIN 2022 grant 000031_23_PP_PIGNATELLI_PRIN_2022-B53D23021240006 (to Pignatelli).

Snakebites Are Far Deadlier Than We Thought, New Study Reveals

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Even fatal snakebites can go uncounted, and a model estimating up to 513,000 deaths annually could help direct lifesaving antivenom to communities that health records miss.

Not every snakebite injects venom. When venom enters the body, however, it can cause snake envenoming. This is a potentially fatal condition, and it mostly affects poor rural communities across tropical and subtropical regions who don’t have easy access to medical care.

As researchers with decades of combined experience in snakebite and tropical disease epidemiology, we came to realize that snake envenoming faces a data crisis. Over half of the countries where venomous snakes pose a threat don’t keep count of how many people are bitten or envenomed.

In rural sub-Saharan Africa and south Asia, many victims live hours away from the nearest clinic, lack transport, or seek care from traditional healers. As a result, their cases are never captured in national health statistics.

To bridge this massive gap, we conducted a study to map the global burden of snake envenoming and related deaths.

We looked at data from 79 countries by reviewing studies published up to 31 March 2025. This allowed us to update the findings from our milestone 2008 global burden study. To fill the remaining data gaps, we built a geostatistical model that combines real-world data with local environmental and socioeconomic factors. This allowed us to estimate envenoming and fatality rates.

We conclude from our findings that snake envenoming is a public health crisis on a far greater scale than official statistics have shown. Our results show that sub-Saharan Africa bears the heaviest toll.

The World Health Organization has set an ambitious target to halve snakebite mortality and disability by 2030. Mapping the problem at the country level is the first crucial step.

To overcome the lack of reliable data, we turned to a model-based geostatistics framework.

To understand how this modelling works, think of it as predicting invisible patterns by combining known data points with environmental signals. Instead of assuming data for every region, this modelling uses statistical probability to calculate a range of likely outcomes based on environmental signals.

We fed our model with data including climate, snake species richness, and socioeconomic indicators. The model then calculated the probability of snakebites occurring in specific locations, even where official health records were completely blank.

By comparing different data sources (including hospital-reported data and regional statistics) against ground level community-survey data, our framework allowed us to account for unreported snake envenomings. It produced both a baseline floor and a potential upper ceiling for snake envenomings and fatalities.

Based on this, we estimate that at least 2.1 million people experience snake envenoming each year globally. The true number could be high as 7 million. Using our model, which adjusts for substantial under-reporting, annual deaths may range from 274,000 to 513,000.

The geographic distribution of this burden is uneven. Our research shows that sub-Saharan Africa alone accounts for 45% of all global envenomings and fatalities. That burden has likely been underestimated for decades. People in sub-Saharan Africa experience snake envenoming and death at nearly three times the rate seen in south Asia.

Countries like Nigeria, the Democratic Republic of Congo and Ethiopia face a high burden, driven by dense rural populations and high exposure in agricultural fields.

The reasons sub-Saharan Africa suffers such disproportionate harm are structural rather than accidental.

Firstly, the continent hosts a rich diversity of medically important venomous species. These include saw-scaled vipers (Echis spp.), puff adders (Bitis arietans), African cobras (Naja spp.), and mambas (Dendroaspis spp.). They are responsible for substantial morbidity and mortality across the continent.

Secondly, millions of rural agricultural workers labor in open fields without protective boots and walk on unlit footpaths at night. When a bite occurs, fragile health systems, long travel distances to regional facilities, and the high-cost or total absence of effective antivenom mean a treatable medical emergency can quickly become fatal. Similar conditions exist in parts of Asia.

We estimate that over 800,000 survivors are left with long-term physical disabilities each year, including amputations and other permanent impairments. These disabilities can reduce household income, increase healthcare costs, and push already vulnerable farming families deeper into poverty.

Meeting the World Health Organization’s goal requires moving away from guesswork and using spatial risk data to guide real-world policy.

By identifying high-risk hotspots, governments and global health partners can strategically stockpile antivenom where it is needed most, train primary healthcare workers in high-burden rural zones, and improve rapid transport infrastructure.

Snake envenoming has remained hidden for too long. Better data cannot solve the problem, but can help ensure that antivenom, healthcare services, and prevention efforts reach the communities that need them most.

Adapted from an article originally published in The Conversation.

A French flag flies as protesters stand on the Marianne statue of the Place de la République during a demonstration against the cost of living and rising fuel prices in Paris as part of nationwide protests called by the "Yellow Vests" movement, joined by French high school and university students, teachers and parents demanding better school conditions and government action, as well as labour unions, workers, farmers and retirees, in France, on October 17, 2026. | Photo Credit: Reuters

France is in turmoil. More than half a million people hit the streets on October 17, as protests swept across the country. As the administration greeted the protesters with a large deployment of riot police, peaceful demonstrations were engulfed by violence in many places. Arsonists set fire to the Lyon tourist office and burnt down the Limoges City Hall, a historic municipal building. Protesters stormed the townhall in Marseille and attacked the prefect’s residence in Bordeaux. In Paris’s Place de la Republique, protesting youth who clashed with the police torched vehicles and looted shops. Luxury retailers such as Louis Vuitton, Hermes, Dior and Galeries Lafayette suffered millions of dollars in losses as their stores were ransacked. Police arrested or detained more than 6,000 people, including hundreds of minors.

With the police outnumbered and unable to protect public and private property, several cities resorted to full or partial curfews. Government officials have blamed the unrest on “seditionists from far-left fringe groups”. They have accused La France Insoumise, or LFI (France Unbowed), the leftwing political party launched by veteran politician Jean-Luc Melenchon (75), of inciting the protests with the aim of extracting political mileage ahead of the presidential elections in April 2027.

Red Cross trains staff as Kenya records its second death from Ebola

📅 2026-10-25  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

As Kenya records its second death from Ebola, with nine confirmed cases, the Red Cross has been training it staff in Nairobi in how to handle victims.

It said the fight to contain Ebola does not end when a patient dies, saying the handling and burial of the body is one of the highest-risk ways the disease is transmitted.

"We are basically just trying to do some bit of simulations. In the country we are the ones who have been delegated the mandate to be able to handle burials of victims as a result of Ebola,” said Dr Paul Olale, Head of public health emergencies at Kenya Red Cross.

“So we are just trying to refresh our teams on the safe and dignified burial to just ensure that the teams are always ready to be able to undertake any case whenever they appear."

Kenya's first case was a man who travelled to Nairobi from the Democratic Republic of Congo ​and died in the city on 5 October.

The health ministry has traced 135 people who had potentially be in contact with him, all of whom were placed in quarantine.

It says the person who died on Friday, a 65-year-old woman, was a close family member of the first victim.

and says the person who died on Friday was a direct contact of the index ​patient.

Kenya's first case was a man who travelled to Nairobi from the Democratic Republic of Congo ​and died in the city on 5 October.

Since then, the authorities ​have traced 135 contacts, all of whom were placed in quarantine.

Olale said all nine confirmed cases are linked to this “index case”.

“The details are quite clear, that based on the chain from the index case, that these are close relatives which basically interacted at one point or another with the index case,” he said.

He added that there have been no new cases reported outside Nairobi.

The ministry said the remaining seven confirmed patients continue to receive care in ‌two ⁠isolation facilities.

More than 4,000 people have died in Africa since Ebola was confirmed in the Congo in May, with the outbreak now reported in four countries on the continent.

Storm Boelo: Travel disrupted and events cancelled as UK hit by wind and rain

📅 Oct 24, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Fresh deluge of wet weather comes after Storm Austen brought strong winds to many regions on Friday

Amber weather warnings have come into force as the worst of Storm Boelo could be set to hit parts of the UK.

Storm Boelo’s winds will peak from Saturday evening, leaving areas in the warning zone, including Scotland, parts of Northern Ireland and the far north of England, braced for gusts of 60-70mph and even 80mph in exposed coastal spots, the Met Office said.

The forecaster’s amber warnings came into force on Saturday at 9pm.

Maximum gust speeds of 75mph have already hit South Uist Range, Western Isles, Highland and Eilean Siar, Scotland on Saturday, according to the Met Office, which has also issued several yellow warnings for rain.

Storm Boelo follows Storm Austen, the first named storm of the season, which brought strong winds to parts of the UK on Friday.

Gusts of 76mph were recorded at St Mary’s airport in the Isles of Scilly, with 74mph at Aberporth in south-west Wales and 71mph at Culdrose in Cornwall.

Heavy downpours since 8am on Saturday have already seen rainfall totals of 72mm in White Barrow, Wiltshire, along with 43mm in both Cwm Dyli, just south of the Snowdon range in North Wales, and Inveruglas on the west shore of Loch Lomond, Scotland.

Hundreds of homes were still without power in south-west England after Storm Austen hit the region on Friday where the National Grid said power had been restored to 131,225 properties by Saturday at 7pm.

An amber wind warning, the Met Office’s second most severe, is active across Scotland, the north of England down to Carlisle from 9pm on Saturday to the same time on Sunday.

A further amber warning runs from 9pm on Saturday to 10am on Sunday in the north-east of Northern Ireland.

Storm Boelo, the second named storm of the season, will bring a danger to life from flying debris and crashing waves and people should stay indoors as much as possible, the forecaster said.

The amber warning sits within wider yellow wind warnings which extend south into parts of England and North Wales and cover the whole of Northern Ireland.

The Environment Agency had issued 58 alerts for spots across England where flooding is possible by 8pm on Saturday, as well as 12 warnings stating that flooding is expected.

Met Office chief forecaster Jason Kelly said: “Storm Boelo will be felt across the UK this weekend, with the strongest winds and greatest potential for disruption in the north.

“Amber warnings are in place across mainland Scotland, parts of Northern Ireland and the far north of England, where gusts of 60-70mph are expected widely, with the potential for 80mph in exposed coastal locations.

“Strong winds will also affect other parts of the UK, with gusts of 50-60mph possible in places. Heavy rain will provide an additional hazard, particularly in the north, bringing the potential for further impacts.

“People should prepare for the potential for disruption, particularly to travel and power supplies.”

People should secure loose items outside, plan ahead if they are travelling and keep an eye on the latest forecast and warnings, he advised.

Storm Boelo has already caused widespread disruption.

Boelo’s strong winds disrupted flights at Dublin airport, where as of 4pm on Saturday, nine outbound and 10 inbound flights had been cancelled by airlines because of weather issues.

It also reported that six inbound flights performed go-arounds – five of which landed on the second attempt, with the other diverting to another airport before returning a short time later.

Network Rail shared images of a trampoline beneath a train and a shed by the tracks, in a call for those living near rail lines to secure their garden furniture firmly.

Visit Liverpool announced that the region’s River of Light event, featuring illuminated artwork, had been cancelled “in the interests of safety”.

London’s annual Diwali on the Square celebrations this Sunday were also cancelled on independent health and safety experts’ recommendations in light of the storm.

Other attractions closed on Sunday include Edinburgh Castle and the Fountains Abbey estate in North Yorkshire, which was due to host its Fountains by Floodlight event.

Scientists Studied 865 Humpback Whales and Found They Grow Faster Than Expected

📅 Oct 24, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Despite decades of environmental change and population recovery, eastern Australian humpback whales appear to have maintained their historical adult size.

Eastern Australian humpback whales grow at an extraordinary pace, reaching approximately 10.9 meters (35.8 feet) in length by age five. By six or seven years old, they have already attained about 90 percent of their eventual adult body length, according to new research led by Griffith University.

The findings, published in Marine Mammal Science, suggest these whales grow faster than humpback populations studied elsewhere. “This is faster growth than other humpback whale populations that have been measured,” said lead researcher Stephanie Stack, a Griffith University PhD candidate.

To investigate how humpbacks develop throughout their lives, researchers measured 865 individually identified whales between 2021 and 2024. Using drones to photograph the animals from above, they calculated body lengths without physically disturbing the whales. Long-term photo-identification records helped the team track growth across newborn calves, juveniles, and adults more than 40 years old.

The measurements showed that humpbacks experience their fastest growth during early life, with the rate gradually declining as they approach adult size. Including calves and young whales allowed scientists to examine these crucial developmental stages directly rather than relying on estimates derived from older animals.

“These are also the first contemporary growth curves developed for eastern Australian humpback whales using measurements from living animals, updating knowledge that has relied heavily on measurements collected during the commercial whaling era in the mid-20th century,” Stack said.

Eastern Australia’s humpback population has recovered dramatically since commercial whaling ended, becoming one of the world’s most successful examples of whale conservation. Despite this population rebound and decades of environmental change, the new measurements closely match historical estimates of adult humpback size in Australian waters.

“These new growth curves establish a baseline for investigating how whale biology may respond to changing environmental conditions.”

“Understanding how quickly whales grow provides another piece of the puzzle in understanding the biology of this recovering population,” Stack said. “Growth influences many aspects of a whale’s life, including its health, energy requirements and potentially the timing of reproduction.”

The new growth curves provide researchers with a reference point for tracking possible changes in humpback development as ocean conditions shift. Future measurements could help determine whether environmental changes are affecting growth patterns in this recovering population.

“As environmental conditions continue to change, having robust biological benchmarks like these will be essential for understanding how whale populations are responding,” Stack said.

Reference: “Contemporary Growth Curves for Individual Humpback Whales in Eastern Australia Using Aerial Photogrammetry” by S. H. Stack, T. Fettermann, B. McGovern and S. M. Bengtson Nash, 15 October 2026, Marine Mammal Science. DOI: 10.1111/mms.70296

Dementia Will Cost America a Staggering $818 Billion This Year

📅 Oct 24, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Lost quality of life accounts for the largest share of dementia’s estimated $818 billion U.S. toll, offering a broader measure of what better care could preserve.

Family members and friends caring for Americans with dementia provide billions of hours of unpaid help, often during their prime working years. Their time, lost earnings, and the physical and emotional strain of caregiving contribute to an economic burden that reaches far beyond medical bills.

A new study led by the University of Southern California (USC) estimates that Alzheimer’s disease and related dementias will cost the United States $818 billion in 2026, driven largely by costs borne by people living with dementia and those who care for them. The figure includes medical and long-term care spending alongside the estimated value of unpaid caregiving, forgone earnings, and diminished quality of life. These broader losses often go uncounted in other estimates of dementia’s economic toll.

As new care models help people with dementia stay at home longer while supporting their families, the distribution of care costs could change. New therapies are also slowing Alzheimer’s, and FDA-approved blood tests can detect the disease before symptoms appear.

“Remarkable innovations can alter the trajectory of dementia in the United States if they are used effectively,” said Dana Goldman, founding director of the USC Schaeffer Institute. “The evidence we are building on dementia costs will provide the healthcare system with a clearer understanding of these and future innovations.”

Understanding those changes requires accounting for both the money spent on care and the losses absorbed by patients and families. The study, published in Alzheimer’s & Dementia: The Journal of the Alzheimer’s Association, estimates the following for 2026:

These estimates come from the U.S. Cost of Dementia Project, a multiyear research effort funded through a cooperative agreement with the National Institute on Aging. Researchers build the model using large, nationally representative datasets, including the Health and Retirement Study and administrative health data from the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services.

The accounting is still being refined. Following last year’s inaugural report, the researchers expanded the 2026 peer-reviewed estimates to include forgone earnings among people living with dementia and new models of health-related quality-of-life impacts on care partners. They also published a peer-reviewed methods paper this year to make their data, assumptions, and modeling accessible and transparent.

Beyond calculating annual totals, the team uses dynamic microsimulation, a method that follows simulated individuals over time, to project how new treatments, care models, and policies could affect future dementia costs. Those projections include often-hidden consequences for well-being and finances. The project aims to provide the most comprehensive accounting yet of dementia’s economic impact, tracking changes in who bears the costs and when to inform policy and treatment decisions.

“By providing annual, comprehensive and transparent estimates of dementia’s total costs, our research can help guide decisions about how to allocate resources as the dementia population is set to grow substantially,” said lead researcher Julie Zissimopoulos, principal investigator of the U.S. Cost of Dementia Project, co-director of the Aging and Cognition program at the USC Schaeffer Center for Health Policy & Economics, and a professor at the USC Price School of Public Policy. “With our modeling approach, we can ask and answer questions such as: How will a new treatment that slows dementia improve quality of life or affect demand for nursing home care?”

Reference: “The cost of dementia in the United States in 2026” by Johanna Thunell, Bryan Tysinger, Matthew Baumgart, Maria Carrillo, Eileen Crimmins, Dana Goldman, Hanke Heun-Johnson, Mireille Jacobson, Geoff Joyce, Duncan Leaf, Lycia Tramujas Vasconcellos Neumann and Julie Zissimopoulos, 24 June 2026, Alzheimer’s & Dementia. DOI: 10.1002/alz.71480

This work was funded through a cooperative agreement with the National Institute on Aging (U01AG086827).

Nine killed and 40 injured after gunmen open fire at church funeral in Jordan

📅 Oct 24, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Footage broadcast on state TV shows three attackers approaching Greek Orthodox church and firing shots

Nine people have been killed after gunmen opened fire at a funeral at a church in Jordan in a rare attack ⁠on the country’s Christian minority.

Authorities described the attack on Friday as a terrorist act by extremists.

Maj gen Abdallah al-Maaytah, the head of Jordan’s ⁠Public Security Directorate, said most of those injured in the shooting at St George’s Greek Orthodox church in Hamoud, in Karak governorate, were receiving treatment, and that the ⁠hunt for the attackers was continuing. Jordan’s health minister said 40 people were injured.

The interior minister, Mazin Al Farrayeh, said the attack had been carried out by “extremists”, but it was too early to tell whether they had any links to known groups ⁠inside or outside Jordan.

Such attacks are rare in Jordan, which is relatively stable, though authorities in the US say in recent years they have repeatedly ​foiled attempted attacks by Islamist militants.

There was no immediate claim of responsibility.

A ‌video, aired on Jordan’s state-owned Al-Mamlaka television station ‌that appeared to be from a security camera on the church, showed three people firing at it from a pavement and hiding behind trees and a ‌low wall. It then showed the attackers running towards the church’s entrance. Reuters was able to verify the video from the entry path and wall surrounding the church, as well as other details.

In a post on X, Jordan’s prime minister, Jafar Hassan, said: “This is a result of a terrorist ideology and blind extremism and we will not allow society to go down this path.”

A priest described how he and ‌others ordered people to ‌get to the ground at the church. “A sense of panic took hold among those present,” Theodoros Soueiss said. “The gunfire was indiscriminate, and you never knew where a bullet might land.”

Khaled Burqan, who was at the funeral, told Al-Mamlaka that dozens of people were present and the gunmen opened fire on them for several minutes.

Ihab Ammareen, another witness, told the station he saw bloodied people collapse ‌on the floor after the attack. “People were crying, wailing, screaming – it’s hard to describe,” he said.

Jordan’s King Abdullah, a leading US partner in the fight against militant Islamist groups, visited Karak hours after the attack and said the perpetrators would ​be punished “with an iron fist”.

“The terrorist attack against Karak will be met with the harshest retaliation. Those who delude themselves into thinking they can divide Jordanians with their bullets have no chance in the face of our unity,” he said in a statement.

Reuters footage from the church after the attack showed blood smeared on the floor near its entrance, where security forces were deployed.

Security has been ⁠stepped up at malls, ⁠foreign embassies ​and other locations across Jordan, the US embassy in Amman said on Saturday.

In a post on X, the UN secretary general, António Guterres, condemned the violence and said attacks against civilians and places of ⁠worship were unacceptable.

People who gathered at a Karak government hospital to support the injured expressed shock. “What happened today has ​left people unable to speak or raise our voices. ​But our only reproach to the state is that ​it must investigate these dark ideas – where they come from and how they enter the minds of our children,” said Hani Al-Mbaydeen, who was outside the hospital.

Christians make up ‌a minority of Jordan’s population. ​The country is home to several ​biblical sites that attract Christian pilgrims from around the world. The US ambassador to the UN, Mike Waltz, described it on X as a “depraved and brutal attack on a house of worship”. Jordan was among the US allies in the region to be hit by Iranian attacks this year after the US and Israel launched strikes against Iran in February.

Scientists Find That Cats and Their Owners Share a Surprising Biological Connection

📅 Oct 24, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The comfort you find in a cat’s company may have less to do with years together than with everyday closeness, a small observational study suggests.

Cat owners often credit their pets with making life better, but research has produced mixed evidence about whether owning a cat benefits human health and well-being. Some studies have found positive effects, while others have reported possible negative ones.

Researchers at Kyoto University wondered whether those differences might reflect the quality of the relationship between people and their cats, particularly their everyday interactions. To investigate, the team followed the daily lives of 29 cats and their 19 owners for one month, recruiting participants through flyers and social media. They assessed owners’ moods through questionnaires and observed how the cats behaved toward their owners and unfamiliar people.

To measure everyday physical closeness, the researchers attached small Bluetooth Low Energy (BLE) beacons to the cats’ collars. Owners who spent more time physically close to their cats tended to report more positive feelings. How long they had owned their cats, however, was not associated with those positive mood states. The distinction suggests that daily contact may matter more to this aspect of owners’ well-being than the length of the relationship.

Urine samples from both cats and owners revealed connections involving oxytocin, a hormone associated with social relationships, and cortisol, a hormone associated with stress. Higher oxytocin concentrations in cats’ urine were associated with higher oxytocin concentrations in their owners’ urine. Cats with higher oxytocin levels also tended to have owners with lower cortisol levels and fewer negative feelings. The cats’ oxytocin levels were more closely associated with their owners’ negative mood states than the owners’ own oxytocin levels were.

“I think many cat owners are familiar with the sense of comfort and peace they feel when their cat climbs onto their lap seeking affection, they start to pet it, and the cat begins purring contentedly,” says corresponding author Saho Takagi. “Our findings suggest that this feeling may be partly reflected in the hormonal connection between cats and their owners, particularly through oxytocin, a hormone involved in the formation of parent-child bonds and social relationships.”

The findings offer a possible explanation for the inconsistent evidence about cat ownership and health. Benefits or drawbacks may depend on the relationship between a person and their cat, rather than ownership alone. However, the study establishes associations, not cause and effect. The team hopes to explore more deeply how cats and humans influence each other through their everyday interactions.

Reference: “Cats and humans show correlated affiliative physiological states” by Saho Takagi, Taisuke Tsubomura, Miho Nagasawa and Takefumi Kikusui, 13 October 2026, iScience. DOI: 10.1016/j.isci.2026.117934

This work was supported by the Japan Society for the Promotion of Science Research Fellowship for Young Scientists (grant no 22KJ2970) and a Grant-in-Aid for Transformative Research Areas (A) (grant no 26A102), both awarded to S.T., by the Ayako Ueda Fund Research Grant Program of the Japanese Society of Feline Medicine, and by the Hakubi Project at Kyoto University.
